L p \mathrm{L}_{p} -maximal regularity for parabolic and elliptic boundary value problems with boundary conditions of mixed differentiability orders
Björn Augner

Abstract
. We consider vector-valued elliptic boundary value problems and parabolic initial-boundary value problems on abstract Banach spaces of class ℋ ​ 𝒯 \mathcal{HT} . For a very general class of boundary conditions, which includes, but is not limited to the classical cases of Dirichlet, Neumann and Robin boundary conditions, we show L p \mathrm{L}_{p} -maximal regularity of the system. We do not restrict ourselves to the homogeneous case, but also identify the temporal and boundary trace spaces, which are both sufficient and necessary to obtain a unique solution in the L p \mathrm{L}_{p} -maximal regularity space. Our results are based on a (partial) Fourier–Laplace transform of the problem in the half-space case, leading to elliptic boundary value problems which have to satisfy variants of the Lopatinskii–Shapiro condition to be solvable. The parabolic theory is then developed with use of tools from modern harmonic analysis such as the bounded ℋ ∞ \mathcal{H}^{\infty} -calculus and the Kalton–Weis theorem.



2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
35K52, 35B65, 35D35, 35J58, 42B15

Version of August 9, 2026.

1.
Introduction

We will consider a quite general class of elliptic and parabolic systems with values in a general Banach space of class
\mathcal{HT}
.
To give a first motivation for the relevance of the investigation of such systems, let us start with a rather simple example which illustrates the main point:
From the theory of parabolic equations it is well-known that, for a bounded domain
\Omega\subseteq\R^{d}
with smooth boundary, both the
Dirichlet heat equation

(D)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}u_{1}-d_{1}\Delta u_{1}=f_{1}&\text{in }(0,T)\times\Omega,\\
u_{1}=g_{1}&\text{on }(0,T)\times\partial\Omega,\\
u_{1}(0,\cdot)=u_{1}^{0}&\text{in }\Omega\end{cases}

(for some
d_{1}>0
) and the
Neumann heat equation

(N)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}u_{2}-d_{2}\Delta u_{2}=f_{1}&\text{in }(0,T)\times\Omega,\\
-d_{2}\partial_{\boldsymbol{n}}u_{2}=g_{2}&\text{on }(0,T)\times\partial\Omega,\\
u_{2}(0,\cdot)=u_{2}^{0}&\text{in }\Omega\end{cases}

(for some
d_{2}>0
) have the property of
\mathrm{L}_{p}
-maximal regularity on the base space
\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega)
, where we consider parameters
p,q\in(1,\infty)
.
This means that (
D
) has a unique solution
u_{1}
in the class
\mathrm{W}_{p}^{1}((0,T);\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega))\cap\mathrm{L}_{p}((0,T);\mathrm{W}_{q}^{2}(\Omega))
if and only if the data
f_{1}
,
g_{1}
and
u_{1}^{0}
belong to the classes
f_{1}\in\mathrm{L}_{p}((0,T);\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega))
,
g_{1}\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{1-1/2q}((0,T);\mathrm{L}_{q}(\partial\Omega))\cap\mathrm{L}_{p}((0,T);\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2-1/q}(\partial\Omega))
and
u_{1}^{0}\in\mathrm{W}_{q}^{2-2/p}(\Omega)
and
u_{1}^{0}
and
g_{1}
fullfil a certain compatibility condition.
Similarly, the problem (
N
) has a unique solution
u_{2}\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{1}((0,T);\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega))\cap\mathrm{L}_{p}((0,T);\mathrm{W}_{q}^{2}(\Omega))
if and only if
f_{2}\in\mathrm{L}_{p}((0,T);\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega))
,
g_{2}\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{1/2-1/2q}((0,T);\mathrm{L}_{q}(\partial\Omega))\cap\mathrm{L}_{p}((0,T);\mathrm{W}_{q}^{1-1/q}(\partial\Omega))
and
u_{2}^{0}\in\mathrm{W}_{q}^{2-2/p}(\Omega)
.
Combining these two scalar results, it easily follows that the two-component system of parabolic equations

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}-\boldsymbol{D}\Delta\boldsymbol{u}=\boldsymbol{f}&\text{in }(0,T)\times\Omega,\\
u_{1}=g_{1}&\text{on }(0,T)\times\partial\Omega,\\
-d_{2}\partial_{\boldsymbol{n}}u_{2}=g_{2}&\text{on }(0,T)\times\partial\Omega,\\
\boldsymbol{u}(0,\cdot)=u^{0}&\text{on }\Omega,\end{cases}

where
\boldsymbol{u}=(u_{1},u_{2})
,
\boldsymbol{f}=(f_{1},f_{2})
,
\boldsymbol{u}^{0}=(u_{1}^{0},u_{2}^{0})
are two-component vector-fields and
\boldsymbol{D}=\operatorname{diag}(d_{1},d_{2})
is a diagonal matrix, has a unique solution in the class
\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{1}((0,T);\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega;\K^{2}))\cap\mathrm{L}_{p}((0,T);\mathrm{W}_{q}^{2}(\Omega;\K^{2}))
if and only if
\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathrm{L}_{p}((0,T);\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega;\K^{2}))
,
\boldsymbol{u}^{0}\in\mathrm{W}_{q}^{2-2/p}(\Omega;\K^{2}))
and the components
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
of the function
\boldsymbol{g}
on the boundary satisfy
g_{1}\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{1-1/2q}((0,T);\mathrm{L}_{q}(\partial\Omega))\cap\mathrm{L}_{p}((0,T);\mathrm{W}_{q}^{2-1/q}(\partial\Omega))
and
g_{2}\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{1/2-1/2q}((0,T);\mathrm{L}_{q}(\partial\Omega))\cap\mathrm{L}_{p}((0,T);\mathrm{W}_{q}^{1-1/q}(\partial\Omega))
and the respective compatibility conditions.
However, as soon as we allow for non-diagonal, but, say, symmetric and positive definite,
\boldsymbol{D}\in\R^{2\times 2}
, the situation becomes less clear, as then the two equations in
\Omega
do
not decouple
anymore.
Still, one can
expect
maximal regularity also for this case.

As similar situation occurs, if not the equations in the domain are coupled, but the boundary conditions are of mixed type, say

\begin{cases}\alpha_{1}u_{1}+\alpha_{2}u_{2}=g_{1}&\text{on }(0,T)\times\partial\Omega,\\
-\beta_{1}d_{1}\partial_{\boldsymbol{n}}u_{1}-\beta_{2}d_{2}\partial_{\boldsymbol{n}}u_{2}=g_{2}\end{cases}

for some parameters
\alpha_{1}
,
\alpha_{2}
,
\beta_{1}
and
\beta_{2}\in\R
.
We will consider a general class of such parabolic systems.
More precisely, we will consider systems of the abstract form

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}u+\mathcal{A}(t,\boldsymbol{x},\mathrm{D})u=f&\text{in }(0,T)\times\Omega,\\
\mathcal{B}_{j}(t,\boldsymbol{x},\mathrm{D})u=g_{j}&\text{on }(0,T)\times\partial\Omega,\,j=1,\ldots,m,\\
u(0,\cdot)=u^{0}&\text{in }\Omega.\end{cases}

Here,
\mathcal{A}(t,\boldsymbol{x},\mathrm{D})
is a parameter-elliptic differential operator of order
2m
, where the coefficients may, in general, both depend on the time variable
t\in[0,T]
and on the spatial position
\boldsymbol{x}\in\Omega
, whereas the operators
\mathcal{B}_{j}(t,\boldsymbol{x},\mathrm{D})
are boundary differential operators of order
2m-1
at most.
The mixed type boundary conditions are reflected by the boundary symbols
\mathcal{B}_{j}(t,\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\xi})
which will not necessarily have a homogeneous (in
\boldsymbol{\xi}
) principal part
\mathcal{B}^{\#}_{j}(t,\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\xi})
– in contrast to related results in
[
17
]
and
[
18
]
, which only apply for systems with homogeneous principle parts.
Note that in the abstract formulation of the above presented second-order in space two-component example, we have
m=1
, so that the two (Dirichlet and Neumann) boundary conditions will be described by only
one
boundary operator, which thus includes both these scalar boundary conditions at once.

Before presenting further instances, for which such mixed type boundary conditions will be of importance, let us give a short overview over the development of the theory of elliptic boundary value problems and parabolic initial–boundary value problems and its close connection to modern harmonic analysis:
In the middle of the 20th century some for the further development of the theory of partial differential equations, especially evolutionary partial differential equations, fundamentally important works were written, which were groundbreaking not only for the linear theory, but in particular for the treatment of semi- and quasilinear, up to fully nonlinear parabolic systems. Ladyshenskaya, Solonnikov and Ural’ceva
[
32
]
basically completely solved the question of
\mathrm{L}_{p}
-maximal regularity for the case of sufficiently regular bounded domains and an exhaustive class of scalar or
\R^{n}
-valued functions.
Up to today, this work represents a milestone in the theory of parabolic equations. At a similar time, de Simon
[
21
]
was able to successfully treat the case of abstract parabolic initial boundary value problems with values in an abstract Hilbert space
X
instead of the concrete Lebesgue space
\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Omega)
, but at first the question remained open whether and to what extent an extension of his results to more general abstract Banach spaces is possible.
From then on, at the latest, the question of characterizing
\mathrm{L}_{p}
-maximal regularity constituted an intensively studied area of research. Sobolevskii
[
38
]
was able to show that (for a fixed base space
X
)
\mathrm{L}_{p}
-maximal regularity of an operator does not depend on the chosen parameter
p\in(1,\infty)
, i.e. if an operator has
\mathrm{L}_{p}
-maximal regularity for one
p\in(1,\infty)
, then it already has
\mathrm{L}_{q}
-maximal regularity for all
q\in(1,\infty)
.
With the sum method for real interpolation spaces of da Prato and Grisvard
[
16
]
rich subclasses of operators with the maximal regularity could be identified.
Using a functional calculus, Dore and Venni
[
22
]
, Prüss and Sohr
[
37
]
were able to show that any operator with bounded imaginary power of power angle
\theta_{A}<\frac{\pi}{2}
has
\mathrm{L}_{p}
-maximal regularity, provided the Banach space
X
under consideration belongs to the class
\mathcal{HT}
, i.e., Banach spaces with a bounded Hilbert transform (which – as it turned out in works by Bourgain
[
11
]
and Burkholder
[
13
]
– are exactly the UMD spaces).
Meanwhile, further sufficient conditions for
\mathrm{L}_{p}
-maximal regularity have been provided for various relevant cases, including boundedness of the semigroup associated to an abstract Cauchy problem on all spaces
\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega)
for
1\leq q\leq\infty
by Lamberton
[
33
]
or Gaussian kernel estimate problems
[
25
]
,
[
14
]
.
The question of actual characterization of
\mathrm{L}_{p}
-maximal regularity, however, proved to be much more difficult: Kalton and Lancien
[
27
]
first showed that de Simon’s technique cannot be applied to abstract Banach spaces of class
\mathcal{HT}
, consequently not to more general Banach spaces.
For a generalisation of de Simon’s results it is mandatory that the basic space
X
is at least topologically
isomorphic
to a Hilbert space, which is a far too restrictive constraint for many concrete problems. The breakthrough then came with Weis
[
46
]
, who was able to characterize maximal regularity of operators on Banach spaces of type
\mathcal{HT}
completely via
\mathcal{R}
-boundedness of the set
\{\mathrm{i}\rho(\mathrm{i}\rho+A)^{-1}:\,\rho\in\R\setminus\{0\}\}
. This property, called
Riesz-property
or (later)
randomized boundedness
, provides just the tightening of the notion of uniform boundedness as a requirement in the Mikhlin multiplier theorem, which is necessary for its generalisation to Banach spaces of class
\mathcal{HT}
.
Simultaneously, for families of bounded linear operators this gave a new approach to the proof of maximal regularity; namely, Kalton and Weis
[
28
]
showed how to obtain maximal regularity for
\mathcal{R}
-sectorial operators using the
\mathcal{H}^{\infty}
-calculus.
Based on these developments in modern harmonic analysis and multiplier theory on Banach spaces of class
\mathcal{HT}
, see, e.g., Berksen and Gillespie
[
9
]
, Clément, de Pagter, Sukochev and Witvliet
[
15
]
, Kalton and Weis
[
28
]
, Denk, Hieber and Prüss
[
17
]
,
[
18
]
extended a large class of
\R^{n}
-valued results by Ladyshenskaya, Solonnikov and Ural’ceva
[
32
]
on linear parabolic systems on bounded domains to the general vector-valued case.
The boundary conditions considered there mainly includes boundary conditions of the same type, so either on the Dirichlet data of the functions itself or on their normal flux through the boundary, which corresponds to homogeneity (w.r.t.
\boldsymbol{\xi}
) of the principal part of the boundary symbols.

In a recent paper
[
6
]
, cf. Example
1.1
below, however, while performing an analysis of fast-surface chemistry and fast-adsorption limit models for heterogeneous catalysis systems, we encountered a situation where the boundary conditions have a combined type:
Instead of a Dirichtlet or Neumann condition in all components equally, a no-flux boundary condition occurred at certain linear combinations of the individual normal fluxes, i.e., the normal direction of the gradient of the individual components, together with nonlinear boundary conditions at the boundary trace of the individual concentrations due to equilibrium conditions in extremely fast surface chemical reactions.
Though that problem can be solved by employing the results and techniques of Ladyshenskaya, Solonnikov and Uralceva
[
32
]
, which are valid for finite-component systems of parabolic type, this problem stimulated the search for more abstract and more general results on vector-valued parabolic and elliptic boundary value problems in the spirit of
[
17
]
and
[
18
]
.
In contrast to the situation where, say the boundary is decomposed into a Dirichlet and a Neumann part, cf. e.g. the works by Auscher et al.
[
7
]
, Egert
[
23
]
, Tolksdorff
[
41
]
, Bechtel, Egert and Haller-Dintelmann
[
8
]
and related works, there seems to be no extension of these results for this particular
mixed type
boundary conditions case, and we want to close this gap in the present manuscript.

As a further motivation for the subsequent development of an abstract theory, and before we comment on the organisation of this manuscript, let us shortly discuss more examples, where such mixed type boundary condition play a role, and for which the results of this paper shall be valuable.

Example 1.1

(Fast surface chemistry limits)
.

In
[
5
]
and
[
6
]
, we derived and analysed a class of reaction-diffusion-systems inspired by heterogeneous catalysis, which includes, for example, the following three component model problem
[
5
]

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}c_{i}-d_{i}\Delta c_{i}=0,&i=1,2,3,\,t\geq 0,\,\boldsymbol{x}\in\Omega\\
\kappa_{f}c_{1}c_{2}-\kappa_{b}c_{3}=0,&t\geq 0,\,\boldsymbol{x}\in\Sigma,\\
-d_{1}\partial_{\nu}c_{1}+d_{2}\partial_{\nu}c_{2}=0,&t\geq 0,\,\boldsymbol{x}\in\Sigma,\\
-d_{1}\partial_{\nu}c_{1}-d_{2}\partial_{\nu}c_{3}=0,&t\geq 0,\,\boldsymbol{x}\in\Sigma,\\
c_{i}(0,\cdot)=c_{i}^{0},&\boldsymbol{x}\in\Omega\end{cases}

on some bounded domain
\Omega\subseteq\R^{n}
with regular boundary
\Sigma=\partial\Omega
, and parameters
k_{f}
,
k_{b}
and
d_{i}>0
,
i=1,2,3
.
This model describes a chemical process inside a reactor with catalytic boundary
\Sigma
, at which an instantaneous reversible chemical reaction of the form
A_{1}^{\Sigma}+A_{2}^{\Sigma}\rightleftharpoons A_{3}^{\Sigma}
takes place.
A linearisation of this system leads to a system of parabolic-initial boundary value problems of the form

(1)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}v_{i}-d_{i}\Delta v_{i}=f_{i},&i=1,2,3,\,t\geq 0,\,\boldsymbol{x}\in\Omega,\\
\alpha_{1}v_{1}+\alpha_{2}v_{2}+\alpha_{3}v_{3}=g_{1},&t\geq 0,\,\boldsymbol{x}\in\Sigma,\\
\beta_{1}\partial_{\nu}v_{1}+\beta_{2}\partial_{\nu}v_{2}=g_{2},&t\geq 0,\,\boldsymbol{x}\in\Sigma,\\
\gamma_{1}\partial_{\nu}v_{1}+\gamma_{3}\partial_{\nu}v_{3}=g_{3},&t\geq 0,\,\boldsymbol{x}\in\Sigma,\\
v_{i}(0,\cdot)=v_{i}^{0},&i=1,2,3,\,\boldsymbol{x}\in\Omega,\end{cases}

and, evidently, the orders of the boundary differential operator differs between the second and the two subsequent lines.
Similar parabolic systems with mixed type boundary conditions naturally appear also for more general reaction networks on the surface, see
[
6
]
.
Whereas for the particular model (
1
) one may exploit the special structure of the boundary conditions on the surface to derive maximal regularity from the scalar case, cf. the strategy used in
[
5
, Theorem 3.2]
, this will no longer be true for the latter, more general class of reaction-networks.

Example 1.2

(Navier–Stokes equations)
.

In
[
10
]
, the authors consider several energy-preserving boundary conditions of the abstract form
\mathcal{B}(u,p)=0
for the incompressible Navier–Stokes equation

\begin{cases}\rho\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}+\dv(\rho\boldsymbol{u}\otimes\boldsymbol{u}-\boldsymbol{S})=\rho f,&t\in(0,T),\,\boldsymbol{x}\in\Omega,\\
\dv\boldsymbol{u}=0,&t\in(0,T),\,\boldsymbol{x}\in\Omega,\\
\boldsymbol{u}(0,\cdot)=\boldsymbol{u}^{0}&\boldsymbol{x}\in\Omega.\end{cases}

The classes of boundary conditions considered there include

•

no-slip boundary conditions
leading to the homogeneous Dirichlet boundary condition

\boldsymbol{u}=\boldsymbol{0}\quad\text{on }\Omega\times(0,T);

•

free slip (or perfect slip) boundary conditions
which lead to mixed type boundary conditions of the form

\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}=0\quad\text{and}\quad 2\mu P_{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{D}\nu=\boldsymbol{0}\quad\text{on}\quad\partial\Omega\times(0,T).

Here,
P_{\Gamma}(\boldsymbol{x})=\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{n}(\boldsymbol{x})\otimes\boldsymbol{n}(\boldsymbol{x})
is the orthogonal projection onto the tangent space
T_{x}\Gamma
of
\Gamma=\partial\Omega
at
\boldsymbol{x}
and
\boldsymbol{D}=\frac{1}{2}\big(\nabla u+(\nabla u)^{\mathsf{T}}\big)
is the
rate of deformation tensor
;

•

Navier boundary conditions

\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}=0\quad\text{and}\quad P_{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{u}+\alpha P_{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{S}\boldsymbol{n}=\boldsymbol{0}\quad\text{on}\quad\partial\Omega\times(0,T),

where
\alpha>0
and
\boldsymbol{S}=2\mu\boldsymbol{D}-p\boldsymbol{I}
is the total stress tensor;

•

Neumann boundary conditions

\boldsymbol{S}\boldsymbol{n}=\boldsymbol{0}\quad\text{on}\quad\partial\Omega\times(0,T).

The boundary symbols related to the Dirichlet resp. Neumann boundary conditions are homogeneous of order
0
resp. order
1
.
On the other hand, free slip conditions and the Navier boundary conditions are – in general – not homogeneous (neither of order
0
nor
1
) due to the involvement of the orthogonal projection
P_{\Gamma}
on the tangent space.

In this sense, mixed type boundary conditions appear naturally in the theory of Navier–Stokes equations.

Example 1.3

(Pattern formation)
.

In a recent research article
[
30
]
, the authors motivate and investigate the pattern which are formed for a particular two component reaction-diffusion system of the form

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}u-d_{u}\Delta u=f(u,v)&\text{in }(0,T)\times\Omega,\\
\partial_{t}v-d_{v}\Delta v=g(u,v)&\text{in }(0,T)\times\Omega,\end{cases}

depending on the type of boundary conditions which are imposed.
One of the several types of boundary conditions considered there is given by the mixed type boundary condition

u=R\quad\text{and}\quad\partial_{\boldsymbol{n}}v=0\quad\text{on }(0,T)\times\partial\Omega,

where
R\in\R
is a constant.
In their derivation of such specific boundary conditions, they make a case for their relevance.
Note that in this particular example, maximal regularity for the quasi-autonomous linear system follows from the scalar theory, since the diffusion process is modelled by Fickean diffusion.
However, as soon as cross-diffusion is allowed for, in the sense of a Fick–Onsager or Maxwell–Stefan diffusion model, this approach is not available.

Example 1.4

(Thermo-diffusion systems)
.

For thermo-diffusive reaction-diffusion systems like

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\vartheta-\kappa\Delta\vartheta=h(\vartheta,\boldsymbol{c}),&t\geq 0,\,\boldsymbol{x}\in\Omega,\\
\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{c}-\dv(\boldsymbol{D}\nabla\boldsymbol{c})=\boldsymbol{r}(\vartheta,\boldsymbol{c}),&t\geq 0,\,\boldsymbol{x}\in\Omega\end{cases}

it is reasonable to fix boundary conditions as follows: For the temperature
\vartheta
, one might, for example, fix the temperature at the boundary
\partial\Omega
, i.e. impose Dirichlet boundary conditions
\vartheta|_{\Sigma}=\Theta
, whereas for the concentrations
c_{i}
it might be more natural to impose no-flux boundary conditions or controlled in-flow conditions, i.e.
-\boldsymbol{D}\partial_{\boldsymbol{n}}\boldsymbol{c}=\boldsymbol{g}
.
Again, this approach leads to mixed type boundary conditions for the combined vector variable
(\vartheta,\boldsymbol{c})
.

With these examples in mind, let us comment on the structural restrictions imposed in
[
17
]
and
[
18
]
:
The
\mathrm{L}_{p}
-maximal regularity results in
[
17
, Theorem 7.11]
(half-space problem) and
[
17
, Theorem 8.2]
(domains with compact boundary), both rely on a version of the Lopatinskii-Shapiro condition in which all the boundary operators
\mathcal{B}_{j}
,
j=1,\ldots,N
, are
homogeneous
of degree
m_{j}\in\N_{0}
up to some lower order perturbation. Therefore, in the definition of the principle symbol as used there, e.g. in
[
17
, Section 8.1]
, the principle part of the boundary operators for
mixed type
boundary conditions could not ’see’ the Dirichlet type contribution, as their order of differentiation is
0
, i.e. strictly smaller than the order
1
for the Neumann type parts.
However, this can
not
be considered as a relatively compact, lower order perturbation of the first order part of the boundary operator
\mathcal{B}_{j}
. We, therefore, extend the results in
[
17
]
and
[
18
]
to boundary conditions of such mixed type. For this purpose, we have to closely follow the lines of
[
17
, Chapter 6–8]
and
[
18
]
, and observe which adjustments are needed to transfer the results there to this more general and, thus, more flexible setup.
In particular, we have to introduce range conditions for lower order terms which respect the structure of the boundary conditions.
Note that the latter obstacle does not appear in the case of homogeneous principal parts, since in that case it is clear what should be a lower order perturbation of the boundary condition (namely, all linear boundary operators of strictly lesser differentiability order than the principle part).

To conclude this introductory section, let us now give a brief overview over this manuscript:
Since we will need notation and results from modern harmonic analysis, in Section
2
we begin by providing the basic notation as well as introducing important notions such as
\mathcal{R}
-boundedness,
\mathcal{R}
-sectoriality and the Kalton–Weis generalisation of Mikhlin’s multiplier theorem.
Also, we present a useful auxiliary result on holomorphic operator families on sectors, see Proposition
2.7
, where
\mathcal{R}
-boundedness of the family of operators
T(\lambda)
on one sector implies
\mathcal{R}
-boundedness of
\lambda T^{\prime}(\lambda)
on any smaller sector.
In Section
3
, we review the existing theory of vector-valued elliptic differential operators on the full space
\R^{n}
, where the solution can be constructed via the Fourier transform on the full-space
\R^{n}
, see Theorem
3.5
.
Then, in Section
4
we outline the basic ideas for solving the parabolic initial-boundary value problem on the half-space
\R_{+}^{n+1}
by employing the Laplace transform with respect to time
t>0
, and partial Fourier transform with respect to the first
n
variables
\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
of the vector
\boldsymbol{x}\in\R_{+}^{n+1}
, leading to a (vector-valued) system of ordinary differential equations, which can be solved by rewriting it as a first order system in
y:=x_{n+1}
system, provided the
Lopatinskii–Shapiro condition
is met.
The solution operator constructed in this way formally provides the solution of the corresponding elliptic boundary value problem, so that (in view of the multiplier theorems) we need to establish kernel estimates and
\mathcal{R}
-bounds for these solution operators, establishing maximal regularity for parabolic initial-boundary value problems on the half-space.
Then, in Section
5.1
by localisation techniques, we may transfer the previous results to general domains with a compact and sufficiently regular boundary.
Whereas the previous sections have been restricted to zero initial data
u(0,\cdot)=0
, in the last section, Section
6
, we extend the results to non-zero initial data and give the corresponding optimal regularity results.

2.
Notation and preliminaries

2.1.
Notation

In this manuscript, all Banach spaces under consideration will be Banach spaces over the field
\C
of complex numbers, and will typically be denoted by capital Roman letters.
Some of the results, however, transfer to the case of real Banach spaces
E
, possibly via complexification of the Banach space, cf. e.g.
[
3
]
.
A very important subclass of these Banach spaces consists of the
Banach spaces
E
of class
\mathcal{HT}
which are characterised by the condition that the
Hilbert transform

(Hf)(x):=\frac{1}{\pi}\big(\operatorname{PV}\frac{1}{t}\ast f\big)(x)=\frac{1}{\pi}\lim_{R\rightarrow\infty}\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0+}\int_{(-R,-\varepsilon)\cup(\varepsilon,R)}\frac{1}{t}f(x-t)\,\mathrm{d}t,\quad f\in\mathcal{S}(\R;E),\,\boldsymbol{x}\in\R

extends to a bounded linear operator on the
E
-valued Lebesgue-Bochner space
\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R;E)
for some (and then, for all)
p\in(1,\infty)
. Important examples of such Banach spaces of class
\mathcal{HT}
are given by Hilbert spaces (or Banach spaces which are isomorphic to a Hilbert space, which includes any finite dimensional Banach space) and the standard Lebesgue spaces
\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega)
,
q\in(1,\infty)
, where the set
\Omega\subseteq\R^{n}
is open and non-empty, see e.g.
[
26
, Propositions 4.2.14 and 4.2.15]
.
Also, if
E
is a Banach space of class
\mathcal{HT}
, so is the Lebesgue–Bochner space
\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Omega;E)
for any open set
\Omega\subseteq\R^{n}
and
p\in(1,\infty)
.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
E
be a Banach space of class
\mathcal{HT}
and
p\in(1,\infty)
.
Then the Lebesgue–Bochner space
\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Omega;E)
is a Banach space of class
\mathcal{HT}
.

For a proof of this result, see
[
26
, Proposition 4.2.15(b)]
.

Given two Banach spaces
X
and
Y
, we denote by
\B(X,Y)
the Banach space of bounded linear operators
T:X\rightarrow Y
.
Moreover,
\rho(A)=\{\lambda\in\C:(\lambda I-A):\dom(A)\rightarrow X\text{ is bijective and }(\lambda I-A)^{-1}\in\B(X)\}
and
\sigma(A)=\C\setminus\rho(A)
denote the resolvent set and the spectrum of a linear operator
A:\dom(A)\subseteq X\rightarrow X
, and
\dom(A)
,
\ran(A)
and
\operatorname{ker}(A)
the domain, range and kernel, resp., of a linear operator
A:\dom(A)\subseteq X\rightarrow Y
.

For the (
E
-valued) spaces of continuous functions
\mathrm{C}(\overline{\Omega};E)
, of
k
-times continuously differentiable functions
\mathrm{C}^{k}(\overline{\Omega};E)
, Lebesgue-Bochner spaces
\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Omega;E)
, Sobolev spaces
\mathrm{W}_{p}^{k}(\Omega;E)
, Sobolev-Slobodetskii spaces
\mathrm{W}_{p}^{s}(\Omega;E)
and Bessel-potential spaces
\mathrm{H}_{p}^{s}(\Omega;E)
, we use standard notation.
Moreover, by

\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{l}}(\overline{\Omega}):=\{f\in\mathrm{C}(\overline{\Omega}):\,\lim_{\left|\boldsymbol{x}\right|\rightarrow\infty}f(\boldsymbol{x})=:f(\infty)\text{ exists, if }\Omega\text{ is unbounded}\}

we mean the functions which are continuous on
\Omega
up to its boundary, and which additionally converge as
\left|\boldsymbol{z}\right|\rightarrow\infty
, provided
\Omega
is unbounded.

Later on, inhomogeneous Lebesgue spaces
\mathrm{L}_{p,q}(J\times\Omega):=\mathrm{L}_{p}(J;\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega))
for
p,q\in[1,\infty)
and any proper interval
J\subseteq\R
and Sobolev spaces or Sobolev–Slobodetskii spaces
\mathrm{W}_{p,q}^{(\tau,\sigma)}(J\times\Omega):=\mathrm{W}_{p}^{\tau}(J;\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega))\cap\mathrm{L}_{p}(J;\mathrm{W}_{q}^{\sigma}(\Omega))
will appear, as well as Besov spaces
\mathrm{B}_{p,q}^{s}(\Omega)
and Triebel–Lizorkin spaces
\mathrm{F}_{p,q}^{s}(\Omega)
.
As before, we denote by
\mathrm{L}_{p,q}(J\times\Omega;E)
etc. their vector-valued versions.
Moreover, sometimes we write
\mathrm{W}_{p,q}^{(\tau,\sigma)\cdot s}
for
\mathrm{W}_{p,q}^{(\tau s,\sigma s)}
.
For more information on these spaces, we refer to the rich existing literature, e.g.
[
1
]
,
[
12
]
,
[
40
]
,
[
42
]
,
[
43
]
,
[
44
]
,
[
45
]
and
[
48
]
.

2.2.
Preliminaries

Since the results will heavily rely on modern results on harmonic analysis, especially the theory of
\mathcal{R}
-bounded operator families and its relation to maximal regularity of abstract Cauchy problems, let us in a preliminary section recall some of the (for our purpose) most relevant results in this perspective.
A fundamental role will be played by
\mathcal{R}
-bounded subsets in a space of bounded linear operators, cf.
[
26
, Definition 5.3.13]
.

Definition 2.2
.

Let
X
and
Y
be Banach spaces and
\mathcal{T}\subseteq\B(X,Y)
be a family of bounded linear operators.

\mathcal{T}
is called
\mathcal{R}
-bounded
if for some (then: for all)
p\in[1,\infty)
there is a constant
C_{p}>0
such that for every finite subfamily
\{T_{\nu}\}_{\nu=1}^{N}\subseteq\mathcal{T}
, vectors
\{x_{\nu}\}_{\nu=1}^{N}\subseteq X
, and independent random variables
\{\varepsilon_{\nu}\}_{\nu=1}^{N}
with
\boldsymbol{P}(\varepsilon_{\nu}=\pm 1)=\frac{1}{2}
on some probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{M},\mu)
, for arbitrary
N\in\N
, the estimate

(2)

\left\|\sum_{\nu=1}^{N}\varepsilon_{\nu}T_{\nu}x_{\nu}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Omega;Y)}\leq C_{p}\left\|\sum_{\nu=1}^{N}\varepsilon_{\nu}x_{\nu}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Omega;X)}

is valid.
In this case, we write

\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{T})=\mathcal{R}_{p}(\mathcal{T})=\inf\{C_{p}>0:\,\eqref{eqn:R-bound}\text{ is valid }\}

for its
\mathcal{R}
-bound.

Remark 2.3
.

Note the following:

(1)

If
X
is (topologically isomorphic to) a Hilbert space,
\mathcal{T}\subseteq\B(X,Y)
is
\mathcal{R}
-bounded if and only if
\mathcal{T}\subseteq\B(X,Y)
is (uniformly) bounded, cf. the remarks right after
[
26
, Definition 5.3.13]
.
For general Banach spaces, this equivalence is no longer true, but in any case an
\mathcal{R}
-bounded operator family is always (uniformly) bounded.

(2)

If
X=Y=\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Omega)
for some open
\Omega\subseteq\R^{n}
, then via the
Khintchine inequality
, see
[
26
, Theorem 3.2.23]
,
\mathcal{R}
-boundedness can be characterised by the
square-function estimate

\left\|\big(\sum_{\nu=1}^{N}\left|T_{\nu}x_{\nu}\right|^{2}\big)^{1/2}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Omega)}\leq M_{p}\left\|\big(\sum_{\nu=1}^{N}\left|x_{\nu}\right|^{2}\big)^{1/2}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Omega)}

for any finite
\{T_{\nu}\}_{\nu=1}^{N}\subseteq\mathcal{T}
and
\{x_{\nu}\}_{\nu=1}^{N}\subseteq\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Omega)
, cf.
[
34
, Theorem 1.d.6]
for more general (
q
-concave for some
q<\infty
) Banach spaces.

(3)

For any fixed
p\in[1,\infty)
, the
\mathcal{R}
-bounds are subadditive and submultiplicative
[
26
, Remark 5.3.14(1)]
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{S}+\mathcal{T})

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{S})+\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{T})

\displaystyle\text{for }\mathcal{S},\mathcal{T}\subseteq\B(X,Y),

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T})

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{S})\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{T})

\displaystyle\text{for }\mathcal{T}\subseteq\B(X,Y),\,\mathcal{S}\subseteq\B(Y,Z).

(4)

If
\mathcal{T}
,
\mathcal{T}_{n}\subseteq\B(X,Y)
are such that

\sup_{n\geq 1}\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{T}_{n})<\infty\quad\text{and}\quad\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\sup_{T\in\mathcal{T}}\inf_{T_{n}\in\mathcal{T}_{n}}\left\|T-T_{n}\right\|_{\B(X;Y)}=0,

then

\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{T})\leq\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{T}_{n})<\infty.

Proof.

We only prove the last statement here.
Let
N\in\N
be an arbitrary natural number,
\{x_{\nu}\}_{\nu=1,\ldots,N}\subseteq X
,
\{T_{\nu}\}_{\nu=1,\ldots,N}\subseteq\mathcal{T}
and
\{\varepsilon_{\nu}\}_{\nu=1,\ldots,N}
i.i.d. random variables with
\boldsymbol{P}(\varepsilon_{\nu}=\pm 1)=\frac{1}{2}
on common probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{M},\mu)
.
Then, setting
C_{n}:=\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{T}_{n})+\frac{1}{n}
, and choosing
T_{n,\nu}\in\mathcal{T}_{n}
such that
\left\|T_{\nu}-T_{n,\nu}\right\|_{\B(X;Y)}\leq\inf_{S_{n}\in\mathcal{T}_{n}}\left\|T_{\nu}-S_{n}\right\|+\frac{1}{n}
, we may conclude that

\displaystyle\left\|\sum_{\nu=1}^{N}\varepsilon_{\nu}T_{\nu}x_{\nu}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Omega;Y)}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\sum_{\nu=1}^{N}\varepsilon_{\nu}T_{n,\nu}x_{\nu}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Omega;Y)}+\left\|\sum_{\nu=1}^{N}\varepsilon_{\nu}(T_{\nu}-T_{n,\nu})x_{\nu}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Omega;Y)}

\displaystyle\leq C_{n}\left\|\sum_{\nu=1}^{N}\varepsilon_{\nu}x_{\nu}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Omega;X)}+\sum_{\nu=1}^{N}\left\|T_{\nu}-T_{n,\nu}\right\|_{\B(X)}\left\|\varepsilon_{\nu}x_{\nu}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Omega;X)}.

By assumption, the second sum tends to zero as
n\rightarrow\infty
, whereas the first term can be estimated by

\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}C_{n}\cdot\left\|\sum_{\nu=1}^{N}\varepsilon_{\nu}x_{\nu}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Omega;X)}=\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{T}_{n})\left\|\sum_{\nu=1}^{N}\varepsilon_{\nu}x_{\nu}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Omega;X)}.

Hence,
\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{T})\leq\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{T}_{n})
and, in particular,
\mathcal{T}
is
\mathcal{R}
-bounded.
∎

Corollary 2.4
.

Let
\Lambda
be an arbitrary index set and
\{T_{\lambda}:\lambda\in\Lambda\}\subseteq\B(X)
be a family of bounded linear operators on a Banach space
X
.
Assume that

\mathcal{R}(\{T_{\lambda}:\lambda\in\Lambda\})=:\rho<1.

Then, the resolvent operator
R_{\lambda}:=(I-T_{\lambda})^{-1}\in\B(X)
exists for all
\lambda\in\Lambda
and

\mathcal{R}(\{R_{\lambda}:\lambda\in\Lambda\})\leq\frac{1}{1-\rho}.

Proof.

Since any
\mathcal{R}
-bounded family is uniformly bounded with uniform bound less or equal the
\mathcal{R}
-bound,
\sup_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\left\|T_{\lambda}\right\|_{\B(X)}\leq\rho<1
, so that the Neumann series
(I-T_{\lambda})^{-1}=\sum_{\nu=0}^{\infty}T_{\lambda}^{\nu}
converges in
\B(X)
for all
\lambda\in\Lambda
.
Since the
\mathcal{R}
-bounds are subadditive, for every finite
K\in\N
, and submultiplicative, we obtain that

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(\{\sum_{\nu=0}^{K}T_{\lambda}^{\nu}:\,\lambda\in\Lambda\})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\nu=0}^{K}\mathcal{R}(\{T_{\lambda}^{\nu}:\,\lambda\in\Lambda\})\leq\sum_{\nu=0}^{K}\mathcal{R}(\{T_{\lambda}:\,\lambda\in\Lambda\})^{\nu}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\nu=0}^{K}\rho^{\nu}=\frac{1-\rho^{K+1}}{1-\rho}.

Therefore, by Remark
2.3

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(\{R_{\lambda}:\,\lambda\in\Lambda\})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{R}(\{\sum_{\nu=0}^{\infty}T_{\lambda}^{\nu}:\,\lambda\in\Lambda\})\leq\limsup_{K\rightarrow\infty}\mathcal{R}(\{\sum_{\nu=0}^{K}T_{\lambda}^{\nu}:\,\lambda\in\Lambda\})

\displaystyle\leq\limsup_{K\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1-\rho^{K+1}}{1-\rho}=\frac{1}{1-\rho}.

∎

Remark 2.5
.

In Corollary
2.4
, it suffices to assume that

\limsup_{\nu\rightarrow\infty}\big(\mathcal{R}\{T_{\lambda}^{\nu}:\,\lambda\in\Lambda\}\big)^{1/\nu}<1.

(The left-hand side may be seen as a
\mathcal{R}
-boundedness based version of the spectral radius.)
In that case there are
\nu_{0}\in\N
,
C_{0}\geq 0
and
\rho\in(0,1)
such that
\big(\mathcal{R}\{T_{\lambda}^{\nu}:\,\lambda\in\Lambda\}\big)^{1/\nu}\leq\rho
for all
\nu\geq\nu_{0}
and
\sum_{\nu=0}^{\nu_{0}-1}\mathcal{R}\{T_{\lambda}^{\nu}:\,\lambda\in\Lambda\}\leq C_{0}
, and in this case,

\mathcal{R}\{R_{\lambda}:\,\lambda\in\Lambda\}\leq C_{0}+\frac{1}{1-\rho}.

Lutz Weis showed that, for Banach spaces of class
\mathcal{HT}
, Mikhlin’s Multiplier Theorem can be generalised from the scalar case by demanding
\mathcal{R}
-boundedness of the (now operator-valued) functions
\left|\boldsymbol{\xi}\right|^{\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|}\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}M(\boldsymbol{\xi})
on
\R^{n}\setminus\{0\}
instead of merely uniform boundedness as in the classical Mikhlin Multiplier Theorem.
For a proof of this result, see
[
39
, Theorem 4.4]
(or
[
17
, Theorem 3.25]
for the case
M\in\mathrm{C}^{n}(\R^{n}\setminus\{0\};\B(X,Y))
).

Theorem 2.6

(Mikhlin’s Theorem in
n
variables)
.

Let
X,Y
be Banach spaces of class
\mathcal{HT}
.
Assume that a function
M:\R^{n}\setminus\{0\}\rightarrow\B(X,Y)
has distributional derivates
\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}M
which for each
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
can be represented by a function and satisfy

\mathcal{R}(\{\left|\boldsymbol{\xi}\right|^{\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|}\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}M(\boldsymbol{\xi}):\quad\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\R^{n}\setminus\{0\}\})=:\kappa_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}<\infty

for all
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
.
Then the operator
T_{M}
defined by
T_{M}f:=\mathcal{F}^{-1}M\mathcal{F}f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\R^{n};Y)
for all
\mathcal{F}f\in\mathcal{D}(\R^{n};X)
(here,
\mathcal{F}
denotes the Fourier transform on
\R^{n}
) extends to a bounded linear operator
\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R^{n};X)\rightarrow\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R^{n};Y)
with operator norm
\left\|T_{M}\right\|_{\B(\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R^{n};X);\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R^{n};Y))}\leq C\sum_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\{0,1\}^{n}}\kappa_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
.

As a rule of thumb, the
\mathcal{R}
-boundedness of the function itself typically is the most critical step in checking the assumptions of Mikhlin’s theorem. In fact, we can provide the following result for operator families which are holomorphic and
\mathcal{R}
-bounded on sectors.
We did not find this precise statement in the existing literature, although Robert Denk draw our attention to a paper of Kunstmann and Weis, who used a similar technique in the proof of
[
31
, Example 2.16]
.

For every
\vartheta\in(0,\pi]
we define the
sector

\Sigma_{\vartheta}\subseteq\C
as

\Sigma_{\vartheta}=\{z\in\C\setminus\{0\}:\,\left|\arg(z)\right|<\vartheta\},

see Figure
1
.

Figure 1.
A sector in
\C
.

Proposition 2.7
.

Let
X
be a complex Banach space,
\phi\in(0,\pi)
and let
T\in\mathrm{C}(\overline{\Sigma_{\pi-\phi}};\B(X))\cap\mathcal{H}(\Sigma_{\pi-\phi};\B(X))
be holomorphic on a sector and continuous up to its boundary.
If

\mathcal{R}(\{T(\lambda):\,\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\phi}\})=:C<\infty,

then for all
\phi^{\prime}\in(\phi,\pi)
it holds that

\mathcal{R}(\{\lambda T^{\prime}(\lambda):\,\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\phi^{\prime}}\})\leq\frac{C}{\sin(\phi^{\prime}-\phi)^{2}}.

Consequently, for every
k\in\N
and
\phi^{\prime}\in(\phi,\pi)
,
\mathcal{R}(\{\lambda^{k}T^{(k)}(\lambda):\,\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\phi^{\prime}}\})<\infty
.

Remark 2.8
.

Note that it suffices to demand weak holomorphy since every weakly holomorphic function
T:\Sigma_{\pi-\phi}\rightarrow\B(X)
is already holomorphic.

Proof of Proposition
2.7
.

Let us fix some
\phi^{\prime}\in(\phi,\pi)
and first consider any
\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\phi^{\prime}}
and estimate its distance to the boundary
\partial\Sigma_{\pi-\phi}
.
In fact, we can distinguish between two cases, namely
\lambda\in\overline{\Sigma_{\frac{\pi}{2}-\phi}}
(only possible if
\phi^{\prime}<\frac{\pi}{2}
) and
\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\phi^{\prime}}\setminus\overline{\Sigma_{\frac{\pi}{2}-\phi}}
.
In the first case, the distance from
\lambda
to the boundary
\partial\Sigma_{\pi-\phi}
can easily be seen to be
\left|\lambda\right|
as
0
is the closest point to
\lambda
on
\partial\Sigma_{\pi-\phi}
.
In particular, in that case
B_{\left|\lambda\right|}(\lambda)\subseteq\Sigma_{\pi-\phi}
.
On the other hand, if
\lambda\not\in\overline{\Sigma_{\frac{\pi}{2}-\phi}}
this will not be the case, but the closest point to
\lambda
on
\partial\Sigma_{\pi-\phi}
will be the orthogonal projection onto the line
\{r\mathrm{e}^{\pm\mathrm{i}(\pi-\phi)}:\,r>0\}
, where the sign is positive or negative depending on whether
\operatorname{Im}\,\lambda_{0}
is positive or negative, resp.
W.l.o.g. let us assume that
\operatorname{Im}\,\lambda>0
(the case
\operatorname{Im}\,\lambda<0
follows similarly).
Then,

\displaystyle\operatorname{dist}(\lambda,\partial\Sigma_{\pi-\phi})=\operatorname{Im}\,(\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\phi}\lambda)\geq\sin(\phi^{\prime}-\phi)\left|\lambda\right|,

since
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\phi}\lambda\in\Sigma_{\phi^{\prime}-\phi}\cap\{z\in\C:\,\operatorname{Re}\,z<0,\,\operatorname{Im}\,z>0\}
, and, hence,
B_{\sin(\phi^{\prime}-\phi)\left|\lambda\right|}(\lambda)\subseteq\Sigma_{\pi-\phi}
.
Using the Cauchy integral formula and the contraction principle for
\mathcal{R}
-bounds of parameter-dependent integrals, we infer that, fixing an arbitrary
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(\{\lambda T^{\prime}(\lambda):\,\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\phi^{\prime}}\})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{R}\big(\big\{\frac{\lambda}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\partial B_{\varepsilon\sin(\phi^{\prime}-\phi)\left|\lambda\right|}(\lambda)}\frac{T(\omega)}{(\lambda-\omega)^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}\omega:\,\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\phi^{\prime}}\big\}\big)

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{R}\big(\big\{\frac{\left|\lambda\right|^{2}}{\left|\lambda-\omega\right|^{2}}:\,\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\phi^{\prime}},\,\omega\in\partial B_{\varepsilon\sin(\phi^{\prime}-\phi)\left|\lambda\right|}(\lambda)\}\cdot\mathcal{R}\big(\big\{T(\omega):\,\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\phi^{\prime}},\,\omega\in\partial B_{\varepsilon\sin(\phi^{\prime}-\phi)\left|\lambda\right|}(\lambda)\big\}\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(\varepsilon\sin(\phi^{\prime}-\phi))^{2}}\mathcal{R}(\{T(\lambda):\,\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\phi}\})=\frac{C}{\varepsilon^{2}\sin(\phi^{\prime}-\phi)^{2}}.

Letting
\varepsilon\rightarrow 1-
, we may, thus conclude that

\mathcal{R}(\{\lambda T^{\prime}(\lambda):\,\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\phi^{\prime}}\})\leq\frac{C}{\sin(\phi^{\prime}-\phi)^{2}}.

The
\mathcal{R}
-boundedness of the sets
\{\lambda^{k}T^{(k)}(\lambda):\,\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\phi^{\prime}}\}
follows by induction over
k\in\N
.
∎

This property comes in handy for elliptic boundary value problems and parabolic initial-boundary value problems, since for these usually
\mathcal{R}
-boundedness can be guaranteed on some complex sector rather than merely on
(0,\infty)
, cf. the characterisation of
\mathrm{L}_{p}
-maximal regularity in Theorem
2.12
below.
In fact, in the theory of parabolic initial-boundary value problems, sectorial operators appear naturally as generators of analytic semigroups, see e.g.
[
24
, Theorem II.4.6]
.
Combining the property of sectoriality with the notion of
\mathcal{R}
-bounded operators then leads to the (in general much smaller) class of
\mathcal{R}
-sectorial operators, which – as we will immediately see in Theorem
2.12
below – appears inevitably when looking for
\mathrm{L}_{p}
-maximal regularity of abstract Cauchy problems.

Definition 2.9

(
\mathcal{R}
-sectorial operators)
.

A sectorial operator
A
on a Banach space
X
(write
A\in\mathcal{S}(X)
) is called
\mathcal{R}
-sectorial (write
A\in\mathcal{R}\mathcal{S}(X)
), if

\mathcal{R}_{A}(0):=\mathcal{R}(\{\lambda(\lambda+A)^{-1}:\,\lambda>0\})<\infty.

In this case, the
\mathcal{R}
-angle

\phi_{A}^{\mathcal{R}}
of
A
is defined as

\phi_{A}^{\mathcal{R}}:=\inf\{\theta\in(0,\pi):\mathcal{R}_{A}(\pi-\theta)<\infty\},

where

\mathcal{R}_{A}(\theta):=\mathcal{R}(\{\lambda(\lambda+A)^{-1}:\,\left|\arg\lambda\right|\leq\theta\}).

Remark 2.10
.

By expanding the resolvent operator
(\lambda-A)^{-1}
as a Taylor series around some
s>0
, using the Neumann series and the algebraic properties (subadditive, submultiplicative) of the
\mathcal{R}
-bounds, one finds that
\phi_{A}^{\mathcal{R}}>0
for every
\mathcal{R}
-sectorial operator, i.e. the definition of the
\mathcal{R}
-angle is meaningful.

Proof.

Let
\eta\in(0,\frac{1}{\mathcal{R}_{A}(0)})
.
Then, for every
s>0
and
\lambda\in\C
such that
\operatorname{Re}\,\lambda=s
and
\left|\operatorname{Im}\,\lambda\right|<\eta s
(i.e.,
\lambda\in\Sigma_{\vartheta}
for
\vartheta=\arctan(\eta)>0
) it holds that
\lambda+A=(s+A)+(\lambda-s)
, hence,

(s+A)^{-1}(\lambda+A)=(\lambda+A)(s+A)^{-1}=I+(\lambda-s)(s+A)^{-1},

where

\mathcal{R}(\{(\lambda-s)(s+A)^{-1}:\,s>0,\,\operatorname{Re}\,\lambda=s,\,\left|\operatorname{Im}\,\lambda\right|<\eta s\})\leq\eta\mathcal{R}(\{s(s+A)^{-1}:s>0\})=\eta\mathcal{R}_{A}(0)<1,

so that

\mathcal{R}(\{(I+(\lambda-s)(s+A)^{-1})^{-1}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(\lambda-s)^{k}(s+A)^{-k}:\,s>0,\,\operatorname{Re}\,\lambda=s,\,\left|\operatorname{Im}\,\lambda\right|<\eta s\})<\frac{1}{1-\eta\mathcal{R}_{A}(0)},

and, thus, we may deduce that

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(\{\lambda(\lambda+A)^{-1}:\operatorname{Re}\,\lambda=s,\,\left|\operatorname{Im}\,\lambda\right|<\eta s,\,s>0\})

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{R}(\{\frac{\lambda}{\operatorname{Re}\,\lambda}:\,\left|\operatorname{Im}\,\lambda\right|<\eta\operatorname{Re}\,\lambda,\,\operatorname{Re}\,\lambda>0\})\cdot\mathcal{R}(\{s(s+A)^{-1}:\,s>0\})

\displaystyle\quad\cdot\mathcal{R}(\{(I+(\lambda-s)(s+A)^{-1})^{-1}:\,\operatorname{Re}\,\lambda=s>0,\,\left|\operatorname{Im}\,\lambda\right|<\eta s\})

\displaystyle<\sqrt{1+\eta^{2}}\cdot\mathcal{R}_{A}(0)\cdot\frac{1}{1-\eta\mathcal{R}_{A}(0)}<\infty.

∎

Similar to the perturbation theorems for contractive or analytic semigroup generators,
\mathcal{R}
-sectorial operators allow for a relative bounded perturbation theorem, see
[
17
, Propositions 4.2 and 4.3]
.

Proposition 2.11

(Relatively bounded perturbation of
\mathcal{R}
-sectorial operators)
.

Suppose that
A
is an
\mathcal{R}
-sectorial operator on a Banach space
X
and set

a:=\mathcal{R}(\{A(\lambda+A)^{-1}:\,\lambda\in\Sigma_{\theta}\})<\infty

for some fixed
\theta\in(0,\pi-\phi_{A}^{\mathcal{R}})
.

(1)

Assume that
B
is a linear operator with
\dom(B)\supseteq\dom(A)
and such that, for some
\alpha\in(0,\nicefrac{{1}}{{a}})
,

\left\|Bu\right\|\leq\alpha\left\|Au\right\|\quad\text{for all }u\in\dom(A).

Then the operator
A+B
is
\mathcal{R}
-sectorial with
\mathcal{R}
-angle
\phi_{A+B}^{\mathcal{R}}\geq\pi-\theta
and

\mathcal{R}(\{\lambda(\lambda+A+B)^{-1}:\,\lambda\in\Sigma_{\theta}\})\leq\frac{1}{1-\gamma}\mathcal{R}(\{\lambda(\lambda+A)^{-1}:\,\lambda\in\Sigma_{\theta}\})

where
\gamma:=\left\|BA^{-1}\right\|_{\B(X)}\mathcal{R}(\{A(\lambda+A)^{-1}:\,\lambda\in\Sigma_{\theta}\})
.

(2)

Let

C_{A}=\sup_{\lambda>0}\left\|A(\lambda+A)^{-1}\right\|_{\B(X)},\quad M_{A}=\sup_{\lambda>0}\left\|\lambda(\lambda+A)^{-1}\right\|_{\B(X)}<\infty

and assume that
B
is a linear operator with
\dom(B)\supseteq\dom(A)
and such that, for some
\alpha,\beta>0
,

\left\|Bu\right\|\leq\alpha\left\|Au\right\|+\beta\left\|u\right\|\quad\text{for all }u\in\dom(A).

Whenever
\alpha<\frac{1}{1+a}C_{A}
and
\mu>\beta M_{A}\frac{1+a}{1-\alpha C_{A}(1+a)}
,
then
A+B
is
\mathcal{R}
-sectorial with
\mathcal{R}
-angle
\phi_{A+B}^{\mathcal{R}}\geq\pi-\theta
and

\mathcal{R}(\{\lambda(\lambda+\mu+A+B)^{-1}:\,\lambda\in\Sigma_{\theta}\})<\infty.

As announced above,
\mathcal{R}
-sectorial operators and
\mathrm{L}_{p}
-maximal regularity of abstract Cauchy problems are closely related.
In fact, the following result is known for Banach spaces of class
\mathcal{HT}
, see
[
17
, Theorem 4.4]
.

Theorem 2.12

(Characterisation of
\mathrm{L}^{p}
-maximal regularity)
.

Let
X
be a Banach space of class
\mathcal{HT}
,
p\in(1,\infty)
and let
A
be a sectorial operator on
X
with spectral angle
\phi_{A}^{\sigma}=\inf\{\phi>0:\,\sigma(A)\subseteq\Sigma_{\pi-\phi}\}<\frac{\pi}{2}
.
In this case, the
abstract Cauchy problem
with homogeneous initial data

(
\mathrm{ACP}_{0}
)

\begin{cases}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}u(t)+Au(t)&=f(t)\quad\text{for }t\geq 0,\\
u(0)&=0\end{cases}

admits
\mathrm{L}^{p}
-maximal regularity on the ray
\R_{+}
, i.e. (

ACP
0

) has a unique solution in class
u:\R_{+}\rightarrow X
with
Au\in\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R_{+};X)
if and only if
f\in\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R_{+};X)
, if and only if
A
is
\mathcal{R}
-sectorial with angle
\phi_{A}^{\mathcal{R}}<\frac{\pi}{2}
.
More precisely, the following statements are equivalent:

(1)

The abstract Cauchy problem (

ACP
0

) has
\mathrm{L}^{p}
-maximal regularity on
\R_{+}
.

(2)

The set
\{A(\mathrm{i}\rho+A)^{-1}:\,\rho\in\R\setminus\{0\}\}
is
\mathcal{R}
-bounded.

(3)

The set
\{A(\lambda+A)^{-1}:\,\lambda\in\Sigma_{\theta}\}
is
\mathcal{R}
-bounded for some
\theta>\frac{\pi}{2}
.

(4)

The set
\{\mathrm{e}^{-A\lambda}:\,\lambda\in\Sigma_{\vartheta}\}
is
\mathcal{R}
-bounded for some
\vartheta>0
.

(5)

The sets
\{\mathrm{e}^{-At}:\,t>0\}
and
\{tA\mathrm{e}^{-At}:\,t>0\}
are
\mathcal{R}
-bounded.

Remark 2.13
.

It may be interesting to compare the conditions on
\mathrm{L}_{p}
-maximal regularity with those of the (weaker) conditions on an operator
A
to generate a bounded analytic semigroup:

(1)

First note that, for the case that
A
is invertible, to have maximal regularity on the (unbounded) ray
\R_{+}
one has to expect exponential stability of the
C_{0}
-semigroup
(\mathrm{e}^{-tA})_{t\geq 0}
generated by
-A
, since then from
Au\in\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R_{+};X)
it follows
u\in\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R_{+};X)
, and for
f=0
and by the Datko lemma this implies exponential stability of the semigroup.

(2)

In the context of bounded analytic semigroups, the following assertions are equivalent, see e.g.
[
24
, Theorem II.4.6]
:

•

-A
generates a bounded (on some sector
\Sigma_{\theta}
,
\theta>0
) analytic
C_{0}
-semigroup
(\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda A})_{\lambda\in\Sigma_{\theta}}
.

•

-A
generates a bounded (on
(0,\infty)
)
C_{0}
-semigroup
(\mathrm{e}^{-tA})_{t\geq 0}
such that
\ran(\mathrm{e}^{-tA})\subseteq\dom(A)
for every
t>0
and

M:=\sup_{t>0}\left\|tA\mathrm{e}^{-tA}\right\|<\infty.

•

There is
\vartheta\in(0,\tfrac{\pi}{2})
such that
\pm\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\vartheta}(-A)
generate bounded
C_{0}
-semigroups.

•

-A
generates a bounded
C_{0}
-semigroup and

\sup_{\lambda\in\C_{0}^{+}}\left\|(\operatorname{Im}\,\lambda)(A+\lambda)^{-1}\right\|<\infty,

where
\C_{\omega}^{+}:=\{z\in\C:\,\operatorname{Re}\,z>\omega\}
for
\omega\in\R
.

For families of integral operators,
\mathcal{R}
-bounds on their kernel functions may help to derive
\mathcal{R}
-boundedness of the familiy, see
[
17
, Proposition 4.12]
.

Proposition 2.14
.

Let
X
and
Y
be Banach spaces,
\Omega\subseteq\R^{n}
be open and
p\in(1,\infty)
.
Further, let
\mathcal{K}\subseteq\B(\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Omega;X);\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Omega;Y))
be a family of kernel operators, i.e. for each
K\in\mathcal{K}
there is a measurable kernel
k:\Omega\times\Omega\rightarrow\B(X;Y)
such that

(Kf)(\boldsymbol{x})=\int_{\Omega}k(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})f(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\quad\text{for all }f\in\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Omega;X)\text{ and a.e.\ }\boldsymbol{x}\in\Omega.

Further, assume that the family of kernels can be
\mathcal{R}
-bounded pointwise

\mathcal{R}(\{k(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}):\,k\in\mathcal{K}\})\leq\kappa_{0}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})\quad\text{for a.e.\ }\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\in\Omega

by some scalar, measurable function
\kappa_{0}:\Omega\times\Omega\rightarrow\R_{+}
which itself is the kernel of a scalar integral operator
K_{0}\in\B(\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Omega))
.
Then the family
\mathcal{K}\subseteq\B(\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Omega;X);\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Omega;Y))
is
\mathcal{R}
-bounded with
\mathcal{R}
-bound
\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{K})\leq\left\|K_{0}\right\|_{\B(\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Omega))}
.

Remark 2.15
.

For comparison, note that a similar result is true if all
\mathcal{R}
-bounds are replaced by (uniform) bounds.

Definition 2.16

(The classes
\mathcal{H}^{\infty}(X)
,
\mathcal{R}\mathcal{H}^{\infty}(X)
and
\mathcal{BIP}(X)
)
.

A sectorial operator
A\in\mathcal{S}(X)
is said to have
bounded imaginary powers
(write
A\in\mathcal{BIP}(X)
), if
A^{\mathrm{i}s}
(defined via the Dunford calculus for sectorial operators) is in
\B(X)
for every
s\in\R
and there is a constant
C>0
such that
\left\|A^{\mathrm{i}s}\right\|_{\B(X)}\leq C
for all
s\in[-1,1]
, cf.
[
17
, Definition 2.4]
.

A sectorial operator
A\in\mathcal{S}(X)
is said to have
bounded holomorphic functional calculus
(write
A\in\mathcal{H}^{\infty}(X)
), if there are
\phi>0
and
C_{\phi}>0
such that for all
h\in\mathcal{H}_{0}(\Sigma_{\phi}):=\{h:\Sigma_{\phi}\rightarrow\C:\,h\text{ holomorphic and bounded}\}
which tend polynomially to zero as
\left|\boldsymbol{z}\right|\rightarrow\infty
, i.e. there are
C,s>0
such that

\left|h(z)\right|\leq C(1+\left|z\right|)^{-s},\quad z\in\Sigma_{\phi}

the operator
h(A)\in\B(X)
, which in this case can be defined via the Dunford calculus, satisfies the norm estimate

\left\|h(A)\right\|_{\B(X)}\leq C_{\phi}\left\|h\right\|_{\infty}.

We set
\phi_{A}^{\infty}
to be the infimum of those
\phi
for which such a constant
C_{\phi}>0
exists, cf.
[
17
, Definition 2.9]
.

An operator
A\in\mathcal{H}^{\infty}(X)
is said to have an
\mathcal{R}
-bounded functional calculus (write
A\in\mathcal{R}\mathcal{H}^{\infty}(X)
), if

\mathcal{R}(\{h(A):\,h\in\mathcal{H}_{0}(\Sigma_{\phi}),\,\left\|h\right\|_{\infty}\leq 1\})<\infty

for some
\phi>0
. The infimum of those
\phi
which are admissible for
\mathcal{R}
-boundedness of the set on the left-hand side is denoted by
\phi_{A}^{\mathcal{R},\infty}
, cf.
[
17
, Definition 4.9]
.

Remark 2.17

(Hilbert space and Banach space case)
.

If
X
is a Hilbert space, then

\mathcal{R}\mathcal{H}^{\infty}(X)=\mathcal{H}^{\infty}(X)=\mathcal{BIP}(X)\subseteq\mathcal{R}\mathcal{S}(X)=\mathcal{S}(X),

where the inclusion of the (set of) operators of bounded imaginary powers in the (set of)
\mathcal{R}
-sectorial operators is strict, in general.
For general Banach spaces the equalities may become strict inclusions, too.
In any case, for every
A\in\mathcal{R}\mathcal{H}^{\infty}(X)

\phi_{A}^{\mathcal{R},\infty}\geq\phi_{A}^{\infty}\geq\theta_{A}\geq\phi_{A}^{\mathcal{R}}\geq\phi_{A}^{\sigma},

but in general these inequalities may be strict.

A nice property of operators with
\mathcal{R}
-bounded
\mathcal{H}^{\infty}
-calculus is the following, see
[
17
, Proposition 4.10]
.

Proposition 2.18
.

Let
X
be a Banach space,
A\in\mathcal{R}\mathcal{H}^{\infty}(X)
and let
\{h_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
be a family of bounded, holomorphic, scalar functions which is uniformly bounded in
\mathcal{H}^{\infty}(\Sigma_{\theta})
for some
\theta>\phi_{A}^{\mathcal{R},\infty}
and
\Lambda
be an arbitrary index set.
Then the family of operators
\{h_{\lambda}(A)\}_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
given by the
\mathcal{H}^{\infty}
-calculus for
A
is
\mathcal{R}
-bounded.

3.
Elliptic differential operators on
\R^{n}

Our primary goal is to consider abstract initial-boundary value problems (IBVP) of parabolic type. However, it is well-known that the Laplace transform of parabolic equation with respect to the time variable leads to elliptic boundary value problems (BVP), which in many cases are easier to handle than directly starting with the parabolic equations themselves.
Therefore, we begin our investigation with elliptic problems arising from parabolic inital-boundary value problems by formally taking the Laplace transform of these.

3.1.
The homogeneous, constant coefficient case:

Let
k\in\N
,
E
be an arbitrary Banach space and
\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{\xi})
be a
\B(E)
-valued polynomial in
\R^{n}
which is homogeneous of order
k
, i.e.

\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{\xi})=\sum_{\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|=k}a_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}

for some operator-valued constant coefficients
a_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in\B(E)
.
To this we associate the differential operator
\mathcal{A}(\mathrm{D})
by formally plugging in
\mathrm{D}=-\mathrm{i}\nabla=(\mathrm{D}_{i})_{i=1,\ldots,n}=(-\mathrm{i}\partial_{x_{i}})_{i=1,\ldots,n}
as
\boldsymbol{\xi}
:

\mathcal{A}(\mathrm{D})=\sum_{\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|=k}a_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}=\sum_{\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|=k}a_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(-\mathrm{i})^{k}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\partial^{\alpha_{i}}}{\partial x_{i}^{\alpha_{i}}}.

If, for each given function
f
which is sufficiently regular and integrable and suitable
\lambda\in\C
, the vector-valued partial differential equation

(3)

\lambda u(\boldsymbol{x})+\mathcal{A}(\mathrm{D})u(\boldsymbol{x})=f(\boldsymbol{x})\quad\text{for }\boldsymbol{x}\in\R^{n}

has a unique solution which can be represented by an integral formula

u(\boldsymbol{x})=\int_{\R^{n}}\gamma_{\lambda}(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{z})f(\boldsymbol{z})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{z}

for some kernel function
\gamma_{\lambda}:\R^{n}\rightarrow\C
, then, by homogeneity of degree
k
for the polynomial
\mathcal{A}
, the kernel function
\gamma_{\lambda}
must scale as

\gamma_{\lambda}(\boldsymbol{x})=\left|\lambda\right|^{\tfrac{n}{k}-1}\tilde{\gamma}_{\theta}(\left|\lambda\right|^{1/k}\boldsymbol{x})\quad\text{for }\boldsymbol{x}\in\R^{n},\,\theta=\arg(\lambda),\,\lambda\neq 0

and
\tilde{\gamma}_{\theta}=\gamma_{\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\theta}}
is the fundamental solution of the vector-valued partial differential equation (
3
), i.e.

\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\theta}\tilde{\gamma}_{\theta}+\mathcal{A}(\mathrm{D})\tilde{\gamma}_{\theta}=\delta_{0}

in the sense of distributions.
To see this, assume that
\lambda=\rho\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\theta}
for some radius
\rho>0
and some angle
\theta\in(-\pi,\pi)
and let
u
be the solution to the elliptic PDE

\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\theta}u+\mathcal{A}(\mathrm{D})u=f

for some given function
f:\R^{n}\rightarrow E
and which has the integral representation

u(\boldsymbol{x})=\int_{\R^{n}}\tilde{\gamma}_{\theta}(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})f(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},\quad\boldsymbol{x}\in\R^{n}.

Then, since
\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{\xi})
is homogeneous of degree
k
in
\boldsymbol{\xi}
, the function
u_{\lambda}(\boldsymbol{x}):=u(\rho^{1/k}\boldsymbol{x})
solves the elliptic PDE

\displaystyle\lambda u_{\lambda}(\boldsymbol{x})+\mathcal{A}(\mathrm{D})u_{\lambda}(\boldsymbol{x})

\displaystyle=\lambda u(\rho^{1/k}\boldsymbol{x})+\rho(\mathcal{A}(\mathrm{D})u)(\rho^{1/k}\boldsymbol{x})

\displaystyle=\rho\big[\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\theta}u(\rho^{1/k}\boldsymbol{x})+(\mathcal{A}(\mathrm{D})u)(\rho^{1/k}\boldsymbol{x})\big]=\rho f(\rho^{1/k}\boldsymbol{x})

and has the integral representation

\displaystyle u_{\lambda}(\boldsymbol{x})

\displaystyle=u(\rho^{1/k}\boldsymbol{x})=\int_{\R^{n}}\tilde{\gamma}_{\theta}(\rho^{1/k}\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{z})f(\boldsymbol{z})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{z}

\displaystyle=\int_{\R^{n}}\rho^{\frac{n}{k}}\tilde{\gamma}_{\theta}(\rho^{1/k}(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}))f(\rho^{1/k}\boldsymbol{y})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{y}

\displaystyle=\int_{\R^{n}}\rho^{\frac{n}{k}-1}\tilde{\gamma}_{\theta}(\rho^{\frac{1}{k}}(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}))\rho f(\rho^{\frac{1}{k}}\boldsymbol{y})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{y}.

Thus, we may conclude that
\gamma_{\lambda}(\boldsymbol{x})=\rho^{\frac{n}{k}-1}\tilde{\gamma}_{\theta}(\rho^{1/k}\boldsymbol{x})
has the scaling property which we claimed.

Definition 3.1

(Parameter-ellipticity)
.

A homogeneous,
\B(E)
-valued polynomial
\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{\xi})
is called parameter-elliptic, if there is an angle
\phi\in[0,\pi)
such that the spectrum
\sigma(\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{\xi}))
of
\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{\xi})\in\B(E)
is contained in the sector
\Sigma_{\phi}
for every
\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}\subseteq\R^{n}
.
The infimum
\phi_{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{ellipt}}
of these
\phi>0
is called
angle of ellipticity
.

Remark 3.2
.

Let us make two short remarks on why later on we will choose
k=2m
to be even, and how parameter-ellipticity and the Lopatinskii–Shapiro condition introduced below in Assumption
4.3
will carry over from real vectors
\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\R^{n}
to complex vectors
\boldsymbol{\xi}+\mathrm{i}\boldsymbol{\eta}\in\C^{n}
with sufficiently small imaginary parts of the components.

(1)

To have an angle of ellipticity
\phi_{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{ellipt}}<\frac{\pi}{2}
, the degree
k
of the polynomial
\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{\xi})
must necessarily be even, see, e.g., the remarks after
[
17
, Definition 5.1]
.
Therefore, soon we will restrict ourselves to the most relevant case that
k=2m
for some
m\in\N
.

(2)

Although parameter-ellipticity is first formulated for real unit vectors
\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
, by homogeneity of
\mathcal{A}
of order
k
, the spectrum
\sigma(\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{\xi}))\subseteq\Sigma_{\phi}
is contained in some sector
\Sigma_{\phi}
, uniformly for all
\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\R^{n}\setminus\{\boldsymbol{0}\}
.
Moreover, and this will become relevant when considering the Lopatinskii–Shapiro condition for elliptic boundary value problems, for any
\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\R^{n}
and
\boldsymbol{\eta}\in\R^{n}
sufficiently small compared to
\boldsymbol{\xi}
, say,
\left|\boldsymbol{\eta}\right|\leq\varepsilon\left|\boldsymbol{\xi}\right|
for some
\varepsilon>0
, we may deduce that

\sigma(\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{\xi}+\mathrm{i}\boldsymbol{\eta}))=\sigma\big(\sum_{\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|=k}a_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}+\sum_{\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|=k}a_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{0}\lneq\boldsymbol{\beta}\leq\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}-\boldsymbol{\beta}}(\mathrm{i}\boldsymbol{\eta})^{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\big)\subseteq\Sigma_{\phi^{\prime}}

for some, in general smaller, angle
\phi^{\prime}\in(0,\phi)
(which depends on the chosen
\varepsilon>0
), and where we write
\boldsymbol{\beta}\leq\boldsymbol{\alpha}
if component-wise
\beta_{i}\leq\alpha_{i}
for
i=1,\ldots,n
.

For some kernel estimates which we will derive right below, we employ the functions

p_{k,\nu}^{n}:\R_{+}\rightarrow\R,\quad p_{k,\nu}^{n}(r):=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{s^{n-2}}{(1+s)^{k-1-\nu}}\mathrm{e}^{-(s+1)r}\,\mathrm{d}s,\quad k,n,\nu\in\N,\,r>0.

These are
completely monotone
, i.e.
(-1)^{p}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{p}}{\mathrm{d}r^{p}}p_{k,\nu}^{l}\geq 0
for all
p\in\N_{0}
, hence, by the Bernstein–Widder theorem, see e.g.
[
47
, Theorem 17]
, is the Laplace transform of a non-negative measure, i.e.
p_{k,\nu}^{l}(x)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-sx}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{j,\nu}^{l}(s)
, and

\int_{0}^{\infty}r^{n+\rho-1}p_{k,n}^{k}(r)\,\mathrm{d}r<\infty\quad\text{if and only if}\quad\rho>\max\{-n,\nu-k\}.

Moreover, the following integral relation between the functions
p_{k,\nu}^{n}
is valid.

Lemma 3.3
.

For all
c,y>0
and
k,n,\nu\in\N_{0}
, the following identity is valid:

\int_{0}^{\infty}p_{k,\nu}^{n}(c(y+r))r^{n-1}\,\mathrm{d}r=\frac{(n-1)!}{c^{n}}p_{k+n,\nu}^{n}(cy).

This identity can be validated straightforwardly via integration by parts, see
[
17
, Corollary 5.3]
.

Theorem 3.4

(Kernel estimate for parameter-elliptic operators)
.

Let
k,n\in\N
be natural numbers,
E
be a Banach space and operator-valued coefficients
a_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in\B(E)
be given such that

\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{\xi})=\sum_{\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|=k}a_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\quad\text{for }\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\R^{n}

is parameter-elliptic with angle of ellipticity
\phi_{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{ellipt}}<\pi
.
Then, for each
\phi>\phi_{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{ellipt}}
and
\nu\in\N_{0}
there exist constants
c_{\phi},C_{\phi,\nu}>0
such that the fundamental solution
\gamma_{\lambda}
of

\lambda u+\mathcal{A}(\mathrm{D})u=\delta_{0}

satisfies the kernel estimates

\displaystyle\left|\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\gamma_{\lambda}(\boldsymbol{x})\right|\leq C_{\phi}\left|\lambda\right|^{(\nu+1)\tfrac{n}{k}-1}p_{k,\nu}^{n}(c_{\phi}\left|\lambda\right|^{1/k}\left|\boldsymbol{x}\right|)\quad\text{for all }\boldsymbol{x}\in\R^{n}\setminus\{0\},\,\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\phi}\text{ and }\left|\boldsymbol{\beta}\right|=\nu.

Proof.

By
[
17
, Corollary 5.3]
,

\left|\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\tilde{\gamma}_{\theta}(\boldsymbol{x})\right|\leq C_{\phi,\nu}p_{k,\nu}^{n}(c_{\phi}\left|\boldsymbol{x}\right|),

so from
\gamma_{\lambda}(\boldsymbol{x})=\left|\lambda\right|^{\frac{n}{k}-1}\tilde{\gamma}_{\theta}(\left|\lambda\right|^{1/k}\boldsymbol{x})
we find that

\displaystyle\left|\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\gamma_{\lambda}(\boldsymbol{x})\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\lambda\right|^{(\nu+1)\frac{n}{k}-1}\left|(\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\tilde{\gamma}_{\theta})(\left|\lambda\right|^{1/k}\boldsymbol{x})\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\lambda\right|^{(\nu+1)\frac{n}{k}-1}C_{\phi,\nu}p_{k,\nu}^{n}(c_{\phi}\left|\lambda\right|^{1/k}\left|\boldsymbol{x}\right|).

∎

The most important results on the full space problem with constant coefficients can be summarised as follows, cf.
[
17
, Theorems 5.4 and 5.5 and Corollary 5.6]
.

Theorem 3.5

(Full-space problem)
.

Let
n,m\in\N
and
p\in(1,\infty)
.
Assume that the interior symbol

\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{\xi})=\sum_{\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|=2m}a_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}},\quad\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\R^{n+1}

is parameter-elliptic with angle of ellipticity
\phi_{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{ellipt}}<\pi
.

(1)

The
\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R^{n+1})
-realisation
A_{\R^{n+1}}
of
\mathcal{A}
, defined as the closure
A_{\R^{n+1}}=\overline{A_{0}}
of the minimal realisation of the interior symbol on
\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R^{n+1})

\displaystyle A_{0}:\quad\dom(A_{0})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2m}(\R^{n};E)\subseteq\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R^{n};E)\rightarrow\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R^{n};E),

\displaystyle[A_{0}u](\boldsymbol{x})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{A}(\mathrm{D})u(\boldsymbol{x}),\quad\text{a.e.\ }\boldsymbol{x}\in\R^{n+1}

is sectorial with
spectral angle

\phi_{A_{\R^{n+1}}}^{\sigma}\leq\phi_{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{ellipt}}
.
For its domain
\dom(A_{\R^{n+1}})
the inclusions

\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2m}(\R^{n};E)\subseteq\dom(A_{\R^{n+1}})\subseteq\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2m-1}(\R^{n};E)

are valid.

(2)

If
E
is a Banach space of class
\mathcal{HT}
, then
A_{\R^{n+1}}=A_{0}
, i.e.
\dom(A_{\R^{n+1}})=\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2m}(\R^{n+1})
, and
A_{\R^{n+1}}\in\mathcal{H}^{\infty}(\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R^{n+1};E))
with
\mathcal{H}^{\infty}
-angle
\phi_{A_{\R^{n+1}}}^{\infty}\leq\phi_{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{ellipt}}
.
In particular,
A_{\R^{n+1}}\in\mathcal{R}\mathcal{S}(\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R^{n};E))
with angle
\phi_{A_{\R^{n+1}}}^{\mathcal{R}}\leq\phi_{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{ellipt}}
and
-A_{\R^{n+1}}
generates a bounded analytic semigroup
(\mathrm{e}^{-tA_{\R^{n+1}}})_{t\geq 0}
on
\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R^{n+1};E)
.
Moreover, for every angle
\phi>\phi_{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{ellipt}}
,

\mathcal{R}(\{\lambda^{1-\tfrac{\nu}{k}}\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(\lambda+A_{\R^{n+1}})^{-1}:\,\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\phi},\,0\leq\left|\boldsymbol{\beta}\right|=\nu\leq 2m\})<\infty.

For the proof of this theorem, we refer to
[
17
, Theorem 5.5 and Corollary 5.6]
.
It crucially uses that the
n
-dimensional sphere
\mathbb{S}^{n}\subseteq\R^{n+1}
is compact and
\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{\xi})
is holomorphic and homogeneous of degree
k=2m
, so that by Proposition
2.18
the family
\{\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{\xi})^{-1}:\,\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\R^{n+1}\setminus\{0\}\}
is
\mathcal{R}
-bounded for each fixed
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\N_{0}^{n+1}
with
\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|=k
, and similarly
\{\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\beta}}[\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{\xi})^{-1}]:\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\R^{n+1}\setminus\{0\}\}
is
\mathcal{R}
-bounded as well.
The bounded
\mathcal{H}^{\infty}
-calculus is then established via Cauchy’s theorem and again Proposition
2.18
.

3.2.
Spatially dependent coefficients and lower order perturbations.

Next, we consider the variable coefficient case with lower order coefficients, i.e.

\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\xi})=\sum_{\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|\leq k}a_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(\boldsymbol{x})\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}

for some spatial-dependent operator-valued coefficients
a_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(\boldsymbol{x})\in\B(E)
,
k\in\N
, and its minimal
\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R^{n};X)
-realisation

[A_{0}u](\boldsymbol{x})=\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{x},\mathrm{D})u(\boldsymbol{x})\quad\text{for }u\in\dom(A_{0})=\mathrm{W}_{p}^{k}(\R^{n};E)\text{ and a.e.\ }\boldsymbol{x}\in\R^{n}.

Using perturbation arguments, the following result can be derived, provided the Banach space
E
is of class
\mathcal{HT}
, see
[
17
, Theorem 5.7]
.

Theorem 3.6
.

Let
E
be a Banach space of class
\mathcal{HT}
,
n,k\in\N
,
p\in(1,\infty)
and
\phi_{0}\in(0,\pi)
.
Assume that
\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{x},\mathrm{D})=\sum_{\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|\leq k}a_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(\boldsymbol{x},\mathrm{D})
is a differential operator of order
k
with variable coefficients which are subject to the following assumptions:

(1)

a_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{l}}(\R^{n};\B(E))
for the top order coefficients where
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\N_{0}^{n}
has length
\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|=k
.

(2)

The principal part
\mathcal{A}_{\#}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\xi})=\sum_{\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|=k}a_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(\boldsymbol{x})\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
is parameter elliptic with angle of ellipticity less or equal
\phi_{0}
, for every
\boldsymbol{x}\in\R^{n}\cup\{\infty\}
, where we formally write
a_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(\infty):=\lim_{\left|\boldsymbol{x}\right|\rightarrow\infty}a_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(\boldsymbol{x})
.

(3)

a_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in[\mathrm{L}_{\infty}+\mathrm{L}_{r_{\nu}}](\R^{n};\B(E))
for each lower order multi-index
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\N_{0}^{n}
of length
\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|=\nu<k
for some
r_{\nu}\geq p
such that
k-\nu>\nicefrac{{n}}{{r_{\nu}}}
.

Then, for each angle
\phi>\phi_{0}
there is a constant
\mu_{\phi}\geq 0
such that the shifted operator
\mu_{\phi}+A_{\R^{n}}
is
\mathcal{R}
-sectorial with
\mathcal{R}
-angle
\phi^{\mathcal{R}}_{\mu_{\phi}+A_{\R^{n}}}\leq\phi
.
In particular, if
\phi_{0}<\nicefrac{{\pi}}{{2}}
, then the abstract Cauchy problem

\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}t}u(t)+A_{\R^{n}}u(t)=f(t),\quad t\in(0,T),\quad u(0)=0

has maximal regularity in the class
\mathrm{L}_{q}((0,T);\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R^{n};E))
for each
q\in(1,\infty)
, i.e. has a unqiue solution in the class
u\in\mathrm{W}_{q,p}^{(1,2)}((0,T)\times\R^{n};E)
if and only if
f\in\mathrm{L}_{q,p}((0,T)\times\R^{n};E)
.

4.
Elliptic vector-valued PDE: The half-space problem

In the preceding section, we have mainly recalled results from
[
17
]
on the full-space problem, and no adjustments have been necessary.
Next, we consider the half-space problem as a prototypical domain and an intermediate step towards general domains.
In this context, we need to adjust the notation and revise the proofs in
[
17
]
to cover the more general class of systems with mixed type boundary conditions.

4.1.
Partial Fourier transforms

Starting with the prototypical case of a half-space
\R_{+}^{n+1}:=\R^{n}\times(0,\infty)
instead of a general domain
\Omega\subseteq\R^{n}
, we are interested in well-posedness and maximal regularity properties of linear systems of parabolic equations of the form

(PIBVP)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}u(t,\boldsymbol{x})+\mathcal{A}(\mathrm{D})u(t,\boldsymbol{x})=f(t,\boldsymbol{x})&\text{for }(t,\boldsymbol{x})\in(0,\infty)\times\R_{+}^{n+1},\\
\mathcal{B}_{j}(\mathrm{D})u(t,\boldsymbol{x})=g_{j}(t,\boldsymbol{x})&\text{for }(t,\boldsymbol{x})\in(0,\infty)\times\partial\R_{+}^{n+1},\end{cases}

accompanied with some suitable initial conditions at
t=0
.
Revisiting the strategy in
[
17
]
, for the moment, let us assume that the differential operators
\mathcal{A}(\mathrm{D})
in the interior and
\mathcal{B}_{j}(\mathrm{D})
on the boundary are homogeneous of degrees
2m
and
m_{j}<2m
, respectively, i.e.

\mathcal{A}(\mathrm{D})=\sum_{\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|=2m}a_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}},\quad\mathcal{B}_{j}(\mathrm{D})=\sum_{\left|\boldsymbol{\beta}\right|=m_{j}}b_{j,\boldsymbol{\beta}}\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\beta}},

where
m\in\N
is a fixed natural number and
m_{j}\in\{0,1,\ldots,2m-1\}
and corresponds to the differentiability order of the operator
\mathcal{B}_{j}(\mathrm{D})
.
The coefficients
a_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}},b_{j,\boldsymbol{\beta}}\in\B(E)
, where
E
may be some arbitrary Banach space, are assumed to be spatially and temporally constant. We employ the multi-index notation
\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}=\prod_{i=1}^{n+1}\mathrm{D}_{x_{i}}^{\alpha_{i}}
with
\mathrm{D}_{x_{i}}=-\mathrm{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}
and write
\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|=\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}\alpha_{i}
for the length of a vector of natural numbers
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n+1})^{\mathsf{T}}\in\N_{0}^{n+1}
.
Then, the interior symbol
\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{\xi})
and each of the boundary symbols
\mathcal{B}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\xi})

\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{\xi})=\sum_{\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|=2m}a_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}},\quad\mathcal{B}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\xi})=\sum_{\left|\boldsymbol{\beta}\right|=m_{j}}b_{j,\boldsymbol{\beta}}\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\quad(j=1,\ldots,m)

are homogeneous in
\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\R^{n+1}
of orders
2m
for
\mathcal{A}
and
m_{j}
for
\mathcal{B}_{j}
,
j=1,\ldots,m
, respectively.
(Throughout, we use the short-hand notation
\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}:=\prod_{i=1}^{n+1}\xi_{i}^{\alpha_{i}}
etc.)
To establish an existence and uniqueness theory for solutions of the parabolic equation
PIBVP
, in
[
17
, Subsection 6.1]
, one may, first only formally, apply the Laplace transform w.r. to the time variable
t\in(0,\infty)
, i.e. we consider

(\mathcal{L}u)(\lambda,\boldsymbol{x})=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda t}u(t,\boldsymbol{x})\,\mathrm{d}t\quad\text{for some set of parameters }\lambda\in U\subseteq\C\text{ and }\boldsymbol{x}\in\R_{+}^{n+1}

so that the parabolic system transforms into a family of elliptic boundary value problems on the half-space
\R_{+}^{n+1}
which are given by

(4)

\displaystyle\lambda(\mathcal{L}u)(\lambda,\boldsymbol{x})+\mathcal{A}(\mathrm{D})(\mathcal{L}u)(\lambda,\boldsymbol{x})

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{L}f)(\lambda,\boldsymbol{x})+u(0,\boldsymbol{x})

\displaystyle\text{for }(\lambda,\boldsymbol{x})\in U\times\R_{+}^{n+1},

(5)

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{j}(\mathrm{D})(\mathcal{L}u)(\lambda,\boldsymbol{x})

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{L}g_{j})(\lambda,\boldsymbol{x})

\displaystyle\text{for }(\lambda,\boldsymbol{x})\in U\times\partial\R_{+}^{n+1},\,j=1,\ldots,m.

In
[
17
]
, this provides the first step towards more general interior and exterior symbols.
More general results are then derived using operator-theoretic results from modern harmonic analysis and employing perturbation methods.
In this manuscript, however, we want to cover situations similar to those in
[
6
]
, where demanding homogeneity of the (adjusted notion of the) principle part of the boundary symbols cannot be guaranteed. Therefore, in comparison with
[
17
]
, we relax the conditions on the
principle parts
of the boundary operators in the following way:

Assumption 4.1
.

The interior symbol
\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{\xi})=\sum_{\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|=2m}a_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
is homogeneous of degree
2m
for some
m\in\N
and parameter-elliptic with angle of ellipticity
\phi^{\mathrm{ellipt}}_{\mathcal{A}}\in[0,\pi)
, i.e. the spectrum is uniformly contained in some sector,

(6)

\sigma(\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{\xi}))\subseteq\Sigma_{\pi-\phi}\quad\text{for all }\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\R^{n+1}\text{ with }\left|\boldsymbol{\xi}\right|=1,

for some
\phi\in[0,\pi)
and where we put
\phi^{\mathrm{ellipt}}_{\mathcal{A}}\in[0,\pi)
to be the infimum of all
\phi
which are admissible for this inclusion.

Moreover, we assume that for
j=1,\ldots,m
there exist continuous, linear projections
\mathcal{P}_{j,k}\in\B(E)
,
k=0,1,\ldots,m_{j}<2m
such that
\mathcal{P}_{j,k}\mathcal{P}_{j,k^{\prime}}=0
for
k\neq k^{\prime}
.
The boundary symbols
\mathcal{B}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\xi})
,
j=1,\ldots,m
are defined by

\mathcal{B}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\xi})=\sum_{k=0}^{m_{j}}\mathcal{B}_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{\xi})\quad\text{with}\quad\mathcal{B}_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{\xi})=\sum_{\left|\boldsymbol{\beta}\right|=k}b_{j,k,\boldsymbol{\beta}}\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\mathcal{P}_{j,k}\quad\text{for }j=1,\ldots,m\text{ and }k=0,1,\ldots,m_{j}.

We assume that
a_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in\B(E)
and
b_{j,k,\boldsymbol{\beta}}\in\B(E)
denote constant coefficients and such that
b_{j,k,\boldsymbol{\beta}}(\ran(\mathcal{P}_{j,k}))\subseteq\ran(\mathcal{P}_{j,k})
for all
\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\N_{0}^{n+1}
with
\left|\boldsymbol{\beta}\right|=k
.

With a slight abuse of notation, for the remainder of this section we focus on the elliptic boundary value problem and simply write
u
,
f
and
g_{j}
for what should actually be the Laplace-transformed versions
\mathcal{L}u
,
\mathcal{L}f-u(0,\cdot)
and
\mathcal{L}g_{j}
, respectively.
We fix an integrability exponent from the reflexive range
p\in(1,\infty)
and an angle
\phi>\phi^{\mathrm{ellipt}}_{\mathcal{A}}
, and consider the boundary value problem

\begin{cases}\lambda u+\mathcal{A}(\mathrm{D})u=f&\text{in }\R_{+}^{n+1}\\
\mathcal{B}_{j}(\mathrm{D})u=g_{j}&\text{on }\partial\R_{+}^{n+1},\quad j=1,\ldots,m\end{cases}

for a given parameter
\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\phi}
, and given data
f\in\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E)
and
g_{j}=\sum_{k=0}^{m_{j}}g_{j,k}
such that
g_{j,k}\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2m-k-1/p}(\R^{n};\ran(\mathcal{P}_{j,k}))
for
j=1,\ldots,m
and
k=0,1,\ldots,m_{j}<2m
.
The function
f
can be extended trivially by zero to
\mathcal{E}_{0}f\in\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R^{n+1};E)
on the full space
\R^{n+1}
; below
\mathcal{E}_{0}
will always denote this trivial extension operator.
Moreover, by assumption we may write
g_{j}=\sum_{k=0}^{m_{j}}g_{j,k}=\sum_{k=0}^{m_{j}}\mathcal{P}_{j,k}g_{j}
(which is a consistent choice since all operators
\mathcal{P}_{j,k}
are projections such that
\mathcal{P}_{j,k}\mathcal{P}_{j,k^{\prime}}=0
for
k\neq k^{\prime}
).
To solve the elliptic boundary value problem, we employ the following strategy (cf.
[
17
]
):
First, we solve the inhomogeneous problem on the full space

\lambda w^{\mathrm{fs}}+\mathcal{A}(\mathrm{D})w^{\mathrm{fs}}=\mathcal{E}_{0}f\quad\text{on }\R^{n+1}.

This is somehow the easiest part, since we may employ the full space theory developed in
[
17
, Section 5]
, to write
\boldsymbol{\xi}=(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},y)
and

w^{\mathrm{fs}}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},y,\lambda)=(P_{\R_{+}^{n+1}}(\lambda+A_{\R^{n+1}})^{-1}\mathcal{E}_{0}f)(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},y).

Here, we write
P_{\R_{+}^{n+1}}
for the restriction from the full space
\R^{n+1}
to the half-space
\R_{+}^{n+1}
, and
A_{\R^{n+1}}
for the
\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R^{n+1})
-realisation of the differential operator
\mathcal{A}(\mathrm{D})
, i.e.
A_{\R^{n+1}}u=\mathcal{A}(\mathrm{D})u
on
\dom(A_{\R^{n+1}})=\{u\in\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R^{n+1}):\,\mathcal{A}(\mathrm{D})u\in\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R^{n+1})\}
.
We refer to
[
17
]
for details, but in view of the inhomogeneous boundary problem on the half-space considered next, let us sketch the basic idea first:
Using the Fourier transformation on
\R^{n+1}
, initially defined for functions
u\in\mathrm{L}^{1}(\R^{n+1};E)
by

(\mathcal{F}_{\R^{n+1}}u)(\boldsymbol{\xi})=\int_{\R^{n+1}}u(\boldsymbol{x})\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\boldsymbol{x}\cdot\boldsymbol{\xi}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x},

the (elliptic) full space problem
(\lambda+\mathcal{A}(\mathrm{D}))w^{\mathrm{fs}}=\mathcal{E}_{0}f
in Fourier space becomes the parameter
\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\R^{n+1}
dependent algebraic system of linear equations on
E

(\lambda+\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{\xi}))(\mathcal{F}_{\R^{n+1}}w^{\mathrm{fs}})(\lambda,\boldsymbol{\xi})=(\mathcal{F}_{\R^{n+1}}\mathcal{E}_{0}f)(\lambda,\boldsymbol{\xi})\quad\text{for }\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\phi}\text{ and }\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\R^{n+1}.

(Note that the solution to this problem is not subject to any boundary conditions prescribed on
\partial\R_{+}^{n+1}
, so that in a further step, we have to subtract a corrective solution to the problem
(\lambda+\mathcal{A}(\mathrm{D}))w^{\mathrm{corr}}=0
in
\R_{+}^{n+1}
and
\mathcal{B}_{j}(\mathrm{D})w^{\mathrm{corr}}=-\mathcal{B}_{j}(\mathrm{D})w^{\mathrm{fs}}
on
\partial\R_{+}^{n+1}
from the constructed solution.)
Subsequently we consider the half-space problem for the difference
w:=u-P_{\R_{+}^{n+1}}w^{\mathrm{fs}}
between the solution
u
