to the original problem which we seek for, and the full-space problem with right-hand side
\mathcal{E}_{0}f
.
This new problem is homogeneous in
\R_{+}^{n+1}
, but inhomogeneous at the boundary
\partial\R_{+}^{n+1}
:
For
u
to be a solution to (
4
)–(
5
), this function
w
has to solve the elliptic boundary value problem

(EBVP)

\begin{cases}\lambda w+\mathcal{A}(\mathrm{D})w=0&\text{in }\R_{+}^{n+1},\\
\mathcal{B}_{j}(\mathrm{D})w=g_{j}-\mathcal{B}_{j}(\mathrm{D})w^{\mathrm{fs}}&\text{on }\partial\R_{+}^{n+1},\,j=1,\ldots,m.\end{cases}

Due to the special structure of the half-space
\R_{+}^{n+1}=\R^{n}\times(0,\infty)
, this inhomogeneous boundary value problem can be approached by performing the
partial Fourier transform
in
\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}
, i.e. in the first
n
variables of the vector
\boldsymbol{x}\in\R_{+}^{n+1}
:
Starting with functions
u=u(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y)\in\mathrm{L}_{1}(\R_{+}^{n+1})
we introduce the partial Fourier transform in
\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}
as

\mathcal{F}u(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},y)=\int_{\R^{n}}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\cdot\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime}}u(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y)\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}.

Formally applying the partial Fourier transformation to the boundary value problem (
EBVP
), leads to the elliptic boundary value problem in the Laplace–Fourier space

(7)

\displaystyle\lambda\mathcal{F}u(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},y)+\sum_{l=0}^{2m}\tilde{a}_{l}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime})\mathrm{D}_{y}^{2m-l}\mathcal{F}u(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},y)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}f(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},y)

\displaystyle\text{in }\R_{+}^{n+1},

(8)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{B}_{j}u)(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},0)=\sum_{k=0}^{m_{j}}\sum_{l=0}^{k}\tilde{b}_{j,k,l}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime})\mathrm{D}_{y}^{k-l}\mathcal{P}_{j,k}\mathcal{F}u(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},0)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}g_{j}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},0)

\displaystyle\text{on }\partial\R_{+}^{n+1},\,j=1,\ldots,m,

where we merge coefficients and factors of the same
(n+1)
-st index
\alpha_{n+1}
and
\beta_{n+1}
, resp., and write

\displaystyle\tilde{a}_{l}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime}\right|=l}a_{(\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime},2m-l)}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime})^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime}},

\displaystyle l=0,1,\ldots,2m,

\displaystyle\tilde{b}_{j,k,l}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\left|\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\prime}\right|=l}b_{j,k,(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\prime},k-l)}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime})^{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\prime}},

\displaystyle j=1,\ldots,m,\,k=1,\ldots,m_{j},\,l=0,1,\ldots,k.

Note that by construction the terms
\tilde{a}_{l}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime})
and
\tilde{b}_{j,k,l}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime})
are homogeneous of order
l
in
\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime}\in\R^{n}
.
The vector-valued ordinary differential equation (
7
) is equivalent to a first order system (in
y
) on the space
E^{2m}
, as can be seen by introducing the
E^{2m}
–valued functions

\mathcal{F}\boldsymbol{v}(\lambda,\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},y):=(\mathcal{F}u(\lambda,\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},y),\frac{1}{\rho}\mathrm{D}_{y}\mathcal{F}u(\lambda,\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},y),\ldots,\frac{1}{\rho^{2m-1}}\mathrm{D}_{y}^{2m-1}\mathcal{F}u(\lambda,\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},y))^{\mathsf{T}},

where
\rho>0
is some additional parameter.
To exploit the homogeneity of
\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{\xi})
,
\mathcal{B}_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{\xi})
in
\boldsymbol{\xi}
later on, we closely follow the lines of
[
17
]
and introduce

\sigma:=\frac{\lambda}{\rho^{2m}}\in\Sigma_{\pi-\phi}\quad\text{and}\quad\boldsymbol{b}:=\frac{\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime}}{\rho}\in\R^{n}

as new variables in the Laplace–Fourier space.
From now on, whenever
\sigma
and
\boldsymbol{b}
will appear, they are related to the variables
\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\phi}
and
\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime}\in\R^{n}
and the parameter
\rho>0
by these defining relations:
In particular, we have

(\left|\lambda\right|^{1/m}+\left|\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime}\right|^{2})^{1/2}=\rho(\left|\sigma\right|^{1/m}+\left|\boldsymbol{b}\right|^{2})^{1/2}\quad\text{and}\quad(\left|\lambda\right|+\left|\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime}\right|^{2m})^{1/2m}=\rho(\left|\sigma\right|+\left|\boldsymbol{b}\right|^{2m})^{1/2}.

Remark 4.2
.

Later on we will choose the parameter
\rho=\rho(\lambda,\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime})
depending on the variables
\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\phi}
and
\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime}\in\R^{n}
.
The particular choice of
\rho=\rho(\lambda,\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime})
will be
\rho=(\lambda+\left|\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime}\right|^{2m})^{1/2m}
, in line with the choice in
[
18
]
, but in contrast to the earlier choice
\rho=(\left|\lambda\right|^{1/m}+\left|\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime}\right|^{2})^{1/2}
in
[
17
]
.
The reason behind this are the respective interpretations of
\rho(\lambda,\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime})
as a multiplication operator in Fourier space: For the choices just mentioned,
\rho(\lambda,\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime})
will be the Fourier symbol of the pseudo-differential operators
((-\Delta)^{m}+\lambda)^{1/2m}
and
((-\Delta)+\left|\lambda\right|^{1/m})^{1/2}
, respectively.
As it turned out, the former choice is more practical, see
[
18
]
.

Let us continue with the formulation of the elliptic BVP (
EBVP
) as a first order system and introduce a matrix-valued function
\boldsymbol{A}_{0}:\R^{n}\times\Sigma_{\pi-\phi}\ni(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},\lambda)\mapsto\boldsymbol{A}_{0}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},\lambda)\in\B(E^{2m})
by setting

\boldsymbol{A}_{0}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},\lambda)=\left[\begin{array}[]{ccccc}0&I_{E}&0&\cdots&0\\
0&0&I_{E}&&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\,\ddots&\,\ddots&\\
0&\cdots&&0&I_{E}\\
c_{2m}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},\lambda)&c_{2m-1}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime})&\cdots&&c_{1}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime})\end{array}\right],

where we choose the (operator-valued) entries of the operator matrix
\boldsymbol{A}_{0}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},\lambda)
as

c_{j}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime})=-a_{0}^{-1}\tilde{a}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime})\quad\text{for }j=1,\ldots,2m-1\text{ and }c_{2m}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},\lambda)=-a_{0}^{-1}(\tilde{a}_{2m}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime})+\lambda I_{E}),

and
I_{E}
denotes the identity map on
E
.
By parameter-ellipticity of the interior symbol
\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{\xi})
, the (
\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime}
-independent) coefficient
a_{0}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime})=a_{0}\in\B(E)
is invertible because

a_{0}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime})=a_{(\boldsymbol{0},2m)}=\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{0},2m)\quad\text{for all }\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime}\in\R^{n},

where
(\boldsymbol{0},2m)=(0,\ldots,0,2m)\in\R^{n+1}
.
Since each of the symbols
\tilde{a}_{l}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime})
is homogeneous in
\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime}\in\R^{n}
of order
l
, we may, reformulate the elliptic BVP (
7
) (with the homogeneous choice
f=0
) equivalently as a first order in
y\in(0,\infty)
system of ordinary differential equations, viz.

\frac{\partial}{\partial y}\mathcal{F}\boldsymbol{v}^{\mathrm{hs}}(\lambda,\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},y)=\mathrm{i}\rho\boldsymbol{A}_{0}(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)\mathcal{F}\boldsymbol{v}^{\mathrm{hs}}(\lambda,\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},y),\quad\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\phi},\,\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime}\in\R^{n},\,y>0.

In contrast to the situation in
[
17
]
, this time, the boundary symbols
\mathcal{B}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\xi})=\sum_{k=0}^{m_{j}}\mathcal{B}_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{\xi})
are
not
homogeneous in
\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\R^{n+1}
of order
m_{j}
, in general, but only each of the symbols
\mathcal{B}_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{\xi}):=\mathcal{B}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\xi})\mathcal{P}_{j,k}=\mathcal{P}_{j,k}\mathcal{B}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\xi})\mathcal{P}_{j,k}
is homogeneous in
\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\R^{n+1}
of order
k
, for each
j=1,\ldots,m
and
k=0,1,\ldots,m_{j}
.
Hence, each (operator-valued) coefficient
\tilde{b}_{j,k,l}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime})\in\B(E)
is homogeneous in
\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime}\in\R^{n}
of degree
l
, and we may write

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}g_{j}(\rho\boldsymbol{b},0)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}\mathcal{B}_{j}u(\rho\boldsymbol{b},0)=\sum_{k=0}^{m_{j}}\sum_{l=0}^{k}\tilde{b}_{j,k,l}(\rho\boldsymbol{b})\mathrm{D}_{y}^{k-l}\mathcal{P}_{j,k}\mathcal{F}u(\rho\boldsymbol{b},0)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{m_{j}}\sum_{l=0}^{k}\rho^{l}\tilde{b}_{j,k,l}(\boldsymbol{b})\mathrm{D}_{y}^{k-l}\mathcal{P}_{j,k}\mathcal{F}u(\rho\boldsymbol{b},0)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{m_{j}}\sum_{l=0}^{k}\rho^{l}\mathcal{P}_{j,k}\tilde{b}_{j,k,l}(\boldsymbol{b})\mathrm{D}_{y}^{k-l}\mathcal{P}_{j,k}\mathcal{F}u(\rho\boldsymbol{b},0)\quad\text{for each }\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}\text{ and }\rho>0.

Therefore, the boundary condition (
8
) is equivalently expressed as

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}}_{j,k}^{0}(\boldsymbol{b})\mathcal{F}\boldsymbol{v}(\rho\boldsymbol{b},0)=\frac{\mathcal{P}_{j,k}\mathcal{F}g_{j}(\rho\boldsymbol{b},0)}{\rho^{k}}\quad\text{for }j=1,\ldots,m\text{ and }k=0,1,\ldots,m_{j},

where we define the row vector
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}}_{j,k}^{0}(\boldsymbol{b})
as follows:

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}}_{j,k}^{0}(\boldsymbol{b})=(\tilde{b}_{j,k,k}(\boldsymbol{b})\mathcal{P}_{j,k},\ldots,\tilde{b}_{j,k,0}\mathcal{P}_{j,k},0,\ldots,0)\quad\text{for }j=1,\ldots,m\text{ and }k=0,1,\ldots,m_{j}.

(At this point, note that the inclusion
\tilde{b}_{j,k,l}(\ran(\mathcal{P}_{j,k}))\subseteq\ran(\mathcal{P}_{j,k})
for the range of the projections
\mathcal{P}_{j,k}
is valid, since we assumed that
b_{j,k,\boldsymbol{\beta}}(\ran(\mathcal{P}_{j,k}))\subseteq\ran(\mathcal{P}_{j,k})
for each
\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\N_{0}^{n+1}
of length
\left|\boldsymbol{\beta}\right|=k
.)
Then
[
17
, Proposition 6.1]
applies and, therefore, the spectrum of
\boldsymbol{A}_{0}(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)\in\B(E^{2m})
is strictly separated by the real axis, i.e.
\sigma(\boldsymbol{A}_{0}(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma))\cap\R=\emptyset
and, hence,
\mathrm{i}\sigma(\boldsymbol{A}_{0}(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma))=\sigma(\mathrm{i}\boldsymbol{A}_{0}(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma))=S_{+}(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)\cup S_{-}(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)
splits into two parts
S_{\pm}(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)
which are
uniformly
(for
(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)\in\R^{n}\times\Sigma_{\pi-\phi}
with
\left|\boldsymbol{b}\right|,\left|\sigma\right|\leq 1
) separated by a strip around the imaginary axis.
This means that, there are constants
c_{1},c_{2}>0
such that

(9)

\displaystyle\inf\{\operatorname{Re}\,\mu:\,\mu\in S_{+}(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)\}

\displaystyle\geq c_{1},

\displaystyle\left|\boldsymbol{b}\right|\leq 1,\,\left|\sigma\right|\leq 1,

(10)

\displaystyle\sup\{\operatorname{Re}\,\mu:\,\mu\in S_{-}(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)\}

\displaystyle\leq-c_{2},

\displaystyle\left|\boldsymbol{b}\right|\leq 1,\,\left|\sigma\right|\leq 1.

Figure 2.
Spectral gap between parts
S_{\pm}(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)
of the spectrum
\mathrm{i}\sigma(\boldsymbol{A}_{0}(\boldsymbol{b},\Sigma))
.

In particular, for each
(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)
the spectral projections
P_{\pm}(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)\in\B(E^{2m})
onto
S_{\pm}(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)
exist.

4.2.
The Lopatinskii-Shapiro condition on the half-space

The
Lopatinskii–Shapiro condition
provides a solvability and uniqueness property for the Fourier space representation of the elliptic boundary value problem (
4
)–(
5
).
It can be stated as follows:

Assumption 4.3

(Lopatinskii–Shapiro condition, half-space version)
.

For each
\lambda\in\overline{\Sigma}_{\pi-\phi}
and
\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime}\in\R^{n}
such that
(\lambda,\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime})\neq(0,\boldsymbol{0})
, the initial value problem

\begin{cases}\lambda v(y)+\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},\mathrm{D}_{y})v(y)=0&\text{for }y>0,\\
\mathcal{B}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},\mathrm{D}_{y})v(0)=g_{j}&\text{for }j=1,\ldots,m\end{cases}

admits a unique solution in the class
v\in\mathrm{C}_{0}(\R_{+};E)=\{v\in\mathrm{C}(\R_{+};E):\,v(y)\rightarrow 0\text{ as }y\rightarrow\infty\}
, for each given
\boldsymbol{g}=(g_{1},\ldots,g_{m})^{\mathsf{T}}\in E^{m}
.

Remark 4.4
.

Below, we introduce a map
\tilde{D}\subseteq\C^{n+1}\rightarrow\B(E^{m};E^{2m})
which (roughly) maps, for each set of parameters
(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},\lambda)\in\tilde{D}
, any given data
g_{j}\in E
to the time trace at zero
\boldsymbol{v}(0)\in E^{2m}
of the unique solution to the elliptic initial value problem from the Lopatinskii–Shapiro condition.
For technical reasons, we want
\tilde{D}
to be an open
complex
neighbourhood of
(\R^{n}\times\Sigma_{\pi-\phi})\setminus\{\boldsymbol{0}\}\subseteq\C^{n+1}
.
We then can prove complex differentiability of this map, provided we know that the Lopatinskii–Shapiro condition is actually also valid for values
(\lambda,\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime})
from this
complex
neighbourhood
\tilde{D}
.
This technical detail had not been addressed in the original work
[
17
]
, however, see
[
29
, Lemma 5.6]
in a master thesis supervised by Robert Denk. By this, the Lopatinskii–Shapiro condition is automatically valid in a complex neighbourhood
\tilde{D}
of
(\R^{n}\times\overline{\Sigma_{\pi-\phi}})\setminus\{\boldsymbol{0}\}\subseteq\C^{n+1}
, provided it is valid on
(\R^{n}\times\overline{\Sigma_{\pi-\phi}})\setminus\{\boldsymbol{0}\}
.

To proceed, we modify the proofs of
[
17
, Propositions 6.2–6.4]
and transfer them to the situation considered here.
We, thereby, obtain the following preliminary result on existence and uniqueness of solutions and a solution formula for the Fourier space representation of the elliptic boundary value problems in half-space which satisfy the Lopatinskii–Shapiro condition.

Proposition 4.5
.

Let
\mathcal{A}(\mathrm{D})
be a homogeneous, parameter-elliptic operator of order
2m
with angle of ellipticity
\phi_{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{ellipt}}\in[0,\pi)
. Let
\phi>\phi_{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{ellipt}}
and
\lambda\in\overline{\Sigma}_{\pi-\phi}
be given and assume that the Lopatinskii-Shapiro condition is valid.
Then there is a unique solution
\mathcal{F}u
of the system (
7
)–(
8
), which is given by the sum

\mathcal{F}u=\mathcal{F}w^{\mathrm{fs}}+\mathcal{F}w^{\mathrm{hs}}+\mathcal{F}w^{\mathrm{corr}}=\mathcal{F}w^{\mathrm{fs}}+(\mathcal{F}\boldsymbol{v}^{\mathrm{hs}})_{1}+(\mathcal{F}\boldsymbol{v}^{\mathrm{corr}})_{1}.

Here,
\mathcal{F}w^{\mathrm{fs}}
denotes the solution to the full-space problem with right-hand side
\mathcal{F}\mathcal{E}_{0}f
,
\mathcal{F}w^{\mathrm{hs}}
is the solution of (
7
)–(
8
) for homogeneous right-hand side
f=0
in
\R_{+}^{n+1}
and boundary conditions
\mathcal{B}_{j}w^{\mathrm{hs}}=g_{j}
on
\partial\R_{+}^{n+1}
,
j=1,\ldots,m
, whereas
\mathcal{F}w^{\mathrm{corr}}
is a correction term with boundary conditions
\mathcal{B}_{j}\mathcal{F}w^{\mathrm{corr}}=-\mathcal{B}_{j}\mathcal{F}w^{\mathrm{fs}}
matching those from the solution to the full-space problem on
\partial\R_{+}^{n+1}
(and
f=0
in
\R_{+}^{n+1}
).
Moreover,
(\cdot)_{1}
denotes the first component of any vector in
E^{2m}
.
The function
\mathcal{F}\boldsymbol{v}^{\mathrm{hs}}
can be computed using the uniformly continuous semigroup generated by
\mathrm{i}\rho\boldsymbol{A}_{0}(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)
,

(11)

\mathcal{F}\boldsymbol{v}^{\mathrm{hs}}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},y,\lambda)=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\rho\boldsymbol{A}_{0}(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)y}\mathcal{F}\boldsymbol{v}^{\mathrm{hs}}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},0,\lambda)\quad\text{for }\lambda=\rho^{2m}\sigma\in\Sigma_{\pi-\phi},\,\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime}=\rho\boldsymbol{b}\in\R^{n},\,y>0,

where the initial value
\mathcal{F}\boldsymbol{v}^{\mathrm{hs}}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},0,\lambda)
has to be chosen according to

\mathcal{F}\boldsymbol{v}^{\mathrm{hs}}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},0,\lambda)=\boldsymbol{M}(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)\mathcal{F}\boldsymbol{g}_{\rho}^{\mathrm{hs}}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},0).

The (operator-valued) function
\boldsymbol{M}:\tilde{D}\mapsto\B(E^{m};E^{2m})
is jointly holomorphic in
(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)\in\tilde{D}
in a complex neighbourhood
\tilde{D}
of
(\R^{n}\times\overline{\Sigma_{\pi-\phi}})\setminus\{\boldsymbol{0}\}\subseteq\C^{n+1}
, and
\boldsymbol{M}(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)
maps any given data

\mathcal{F}\boldsymbol{g}_{\rho}^{\mathrm{hs}}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},0)=\left(\sum_{k=0}^{m_{1}}\tfrac{\mathcal{P}_{1,k}\mathcal{F}g_{1}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},0)}{\rho^{k}},\ldots,\sum_{k=0}^{m_{m}}\tfrac{\mathcal{P}_{m,k}\mathcal{F}g_{m}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},0)}{\rho^{k}}\right),\quad\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime}=\rho\boldsymbol{b}\in\R^{n},\,\rho>0.

to the corresponding unique solution of the algebraic problem

\begin{cases}P_{+}(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)\mathcal{F}\boldsymbol{v}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},0,\lambda)=0,&\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime}\in\R^{n},\\
B_{0}^{j,k}(\boldsymbol{b})\mathcal{F}\boldsymbol{v}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},0,\lambda)=\frac{\mathcal{P}_{j,k}\mathcal{F}g_{j}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},0)}{\rho^{k}},&j=1,\ldots,2m,\,k=0,1,\ldots,m_{j}.\end{cases}

On the other hand, the correction term
\mathcal{F}\boldsymbol{v}^{\mathrm{corr}}
is given by

\mathcal{F}\boldsymbol{v}^{\mathrm{corr}}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},y,\lambda)=-\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\rho\boldsymbol{A}_{0}(\boldsymbol{b},y)}\boldsymbol{M}(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)(\mathcal{F}\boldsymbol{g}_{\rho}^{\mathrm{corr}})(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},0)\quad\text{for }\lambda=\rho^{2m}\sigma\in\Sigma_{\pi-\phi},\,\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime}=\rho\boldsymbol{b}\in\R^{n},\,y>0,

where

\mathcal{F}\boldsymbol{g}_{\rho}^{\mathrm{corr}}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},0)=\left(\sum_{k=0}^{m_{1}}\tfrac{\mathcal{F}g^{\mathrm{corr}}_{1,k}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},0)}{\rho^{k}},\ldots,\sum_{k=0}^{m_{m}}\tfrac{\mathcal{F}g^{\mathrm{corr}}_{m,k}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},0)}{\rho^{k}}\right),\quad\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime}=\rho\boldsymbol{b}\in\R^{n},\,\rho>0.

Here, the functions

\mathcal{F}g^{\mathrm{corr}}_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},0)=\int_{0}^{\infty}h^{\mathrm{corr}}_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},s)(\mathcal{F}f)(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},s)\,\mathrm{d}s,\quad j=1,\ldots,m,\,k=0,1,\ldots,m_{j},\,\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime}\in\R^{n}

are defined using the integral operator kernels

h^{\mathrm{corr}}_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},s)=\int_{\R}\mathcal{B}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},\eta)\mathcal{P}_{j,k}(\lambda+\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},\eta))^{-1}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\eta s}\,\mathrm{d}\eta\quad\text{for }\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime}\in\R^{n},\,s>0.

Proof.

To establish Proposition
4.5
, we proceed as follows and analogously to
[
17
]
:
The linear problem is decomposed into three sub-problems, each of which for itself is easier to handle than the original problem:

\displaystyle\lambda\mathcal{F}w^{\mathrm{fs}}+\mathcal{A}(\mathrm{D})\mathcal{F}w^{\mathrm{fs}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}_{0}\mathcal{F}f

\displaystyle\text{in }\R^{n+1}

(obviously, without any boundary conditions),

\displaystyle\lambda\mathcal{F}w^{\mathrm{hs}}+\mathcal{A}(\mathrm{D})\mathcal{F}w^{\mathrm{hs}}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\text{in }\R_{+}^{n+1},

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{j}(\mathrm{D})\mathcal{F}w^{\mathrm{hs}}

\displaystyle=g_{j},

\displaystyle\text{on }\partial\R_{+}^{n+1},\,j=1,\ldots,m,

and

\displaystyle\lambda\mathcal{F}w^{\mathrm{corr}}+\mathcal{A}(\mathrm{D})\mathcal{F}w^{\mathrm{corr}}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\text{in }\R_{+}^{n+1},

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{j}(\mathrm{D})\mathcal{F}w^{\mathrm{corr}}

\displaystyle=-\mathcal{B}_{j}(\mathrm{D})\mathcal{F}w^{\mathrm{fs}}

\displaystyle\text{on }\partial\R_{+}^{n+1},\,j=1,\ldots,m.

The second problem, i.e. the case where
f=0
, but inhomogeneous boundary conditions are imposed, may be handled similar to
[
17
, Proposition 5.3]
which provides us with a unique solution
(\mathcal{F}\boldsymbol{v}^{\mathrm{hs}})_{1}
to the problem (
4
)–(
5
) for
f=0
, and which has the form (
11
), where
\boldsymbol{M}(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)
is jointly holomorphic in the variables
(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)\in\tilde{D}\supset(\R^{n}\times\overline{\Sigma_{\pi-\phi}})\setminus\{\boldsymbol{0}\}
. Since the projections
\mathcal{P}_{j,k}
project the problem to subspaces on which the corresponding system for the boundary operators is homogeneous in
\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime}
of degree
k
, uniqueness and existence of solutions, as well as the representation formula for the solution can be extracted from the equivalent reformulation as a first order (in
y\in(0,\infty)
) system. It, therefore, remains to ensure holomorphy of the map
\boldsymbol{M}
. This can be done almost literally as in the proof of
[
17
, Proposition 6.2]
:
First, we employ the closed graph theorem to conclude that for each
\boldsymbol{z}\in D
the operator
\boldsymbol{M}(\boldsymbol{z})
is a linear and closed map
E^{m}\rightarrow E^{2m}
, and it is uniformly bounded, first on the set
D=\overline{\mathrm{B}_{1}(0)}\times\overline{\Sigma}_{\pi-\phi}\subseteq\R^{n}\times\C
, and, thereafter, in any complex neighbourhood
\tilde{D}
of
D
, which is sufficiently close to
D
. With use of the spectral projections
P_{-}(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)
and the Lopatinskii-Shapiro condition we may then prove continuity of the map
\boldsymbol{z}\mapsto\boldsymbol{v}(\boldsymbol{z})=\boldsymbol{M}(\boldsymbol{z})\boldsymbol{g}
, for any fixed
\boldsymbol{g}\in E^{m}
, on complex lines.
This uses, in particular, that the Lopatinskii–Shapiro condition is valid on a complex neighbourhood
\tilde{D}
of
(\R^{n}\times\overline{\Sigma_{\pi-\phi}})\setminus\{\boldsymbol{0}\}
.
Moreover, it uses that the spectral projections
P_{-}
and the operators
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}}_{j,k}^{0}
are continuous.
Complex differentiability follows.

To derive an integral representation of the solution
\mathcal{F}w^{\mathrm{corr}}
to the correction problem, we use that, analogous to the half-space problem with given boundary data,
\mathcal{F}w^{\mathrm{corr}}
is the first component of the
E^{2m}
-valued function
\mathcal{F}\boldsymbol{v}^{\mathrm{corr}}
, which can be formally written down as

(\mathcal{F}\boldsymbol{v}^{\mathrm{corr}})(\lambda,\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},y)=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\rho\boldsymbol{A}_{0}(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)y}\boldsymbol{M}(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)\mathcal{F}\boldsymbol{g}_{\rho}^{\mathrm{corr}}(\lambda,\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime}).

The
\rho
-weighted version of the boundary data is

\mathcal{F}\boldsymbol{g}_{\rho}^{\mathrm{corr}}(\lambda,\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime})=\big(\sum_{k=0}^{m_{1}}\frac{\mathcal{F}g_{1,k}^{\mathrm{corr}}(\lambda,\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime})}{\rho^{k}},\ldots,\sum_{k=0}^{m_{m}}\frac{\mathcal{F}g_{m,k}^{\mathrm{corr}}(\lambda,\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime})}{\rho^{k}}\big),

where the boundary data
\mathcal{F}g_{j,k}^{\mathrm{corr}}
result from taking the trace of
\mathcal{B}_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},\mathrm{D}_{y})\mathcal{F}w^{\mathrm{fs}}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},y)
at
y=0
.
We note that

(\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y}w^{\mathrm{fs}})(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},\eta)=(\lambda+\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},\eta))^{-1}\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y}(\mathcal{E}_{0}f)(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},y)=(\lambda+\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{\xi},\eta))^{-1}(\mathcal{L}_{y}\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}}f)(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},\mathrm{i}y)

since by definition
(\mathcal{E}_{0}f)(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y)=0
for
y<0
.
(By
\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}}
and
\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y}
we mean the Fourier transform w.r.t.
\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}
and
\boldsymbol{x}=(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y)
, respectively.)
Thus,

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},\eta)\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y}w^{\mathrm{fs}}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},\eta)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y}\big(\mathcal{B}_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{D})w^{\mathrm{fs}}\big)(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},\eta)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{B}_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},\eta)(\lambda+\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},\eta))^{-1}\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y}(\mathcal{E}_{0}f)(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},\eta)

\displaystyle=\int_{\R}\mathcal{B}_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},\eta)(\lambda+\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},\eta))^{-1}\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{E}_{0}f)(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},y)\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\eta y}\,\mathrm{d}y.

\mathcal{B}_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},\mathrm{D}_{y})\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}}w^{\mathrm{fs}}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},0)
can, thus, be obtained by taking the inverse Laplace transform

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},\mathrm{D}_{y})\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime}}w^{\mathrm{fs}}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},0)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\R}\mathcal{B}_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},\eta)(\lambda+\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},\eta))^{-1}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\eta y}(\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}}\mathcal{E}_{0}f)(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}\eta.

For the last of the three sub-problems, the crucial point is to derive an integral representation of the boundary values
\mathcal{F}g_{j}^{\mathrm{corr}}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},0)=(\mathcal{B}_{j}(\mathrm{D})\mathcal{F}P_{\R_{+}^{n+1}}(\lambda+A_{\R^{n+1}})^{-1}\mathcal{E}_{0}f)(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},0)
as
\mathcal{F}g_{j}^{\mathrm{corr}}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},0)=\sum_{k=0}^{m_{j}}\mathcal{F}g^{\mathrm{corr}}_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{\xi},0)
with

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}g^{\mathrm{corr}}_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},0)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}h^{\mathrm{corr}}_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},s)(\mathcal{F}f)(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},s)\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle h^{\mathrm{corr}}_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},s)

\displaystyle=\int_{\R}\mathcal{B}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},\eta)\mathcal{P}_{j,k}(\lambda+\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},\eta))^{-1}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\eta s}\,\mathrm{d}\eta,\quad 1\leq j\leq m,\,0\leq k\leq m_{j}<2m

and to ensure that the kernels
h^{\mathrm{corr}}_{j,k}
have good decay properties.
∎

4.3.
Kernel estimates

To actually solve the elliptic boundary value problem (
EBVP
) we may now use the kernel representation of the solution corresponding to the boundary value problem in Fourier space, and perform the inverse Laplace–Fourier transform.
To check that the solution operators defined by this procedure are at least continuous on
\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E)
, we may employ theory of integral operators.
Roughly speaking, the following is true:
If a linear operator on
\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E)
has a kernel representation, estimates on the kernel imply estimates on its operator norm.
For a precise formulation of this statement, see e.g.
[
17
, Lemma 6.7]
.
Its proof relies on Young’s and Hölder’s inequality.

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
p,p^{\prime}\in(1,\infty)
such that
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}=1
and
E
be a Banach space and
k\in\mathrm{L}_{p,p^{\prime},1}(\R^{n}\times(0,\infty)\times(0,\infty))
.
Then,

(Kf)(\boldsymbol{x}):=\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\R^{n}}k(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}-\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime},y,\bar{y})f(\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime},\bar{y})\,\mathrm{d}\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}\,\mathrm{d}\bar{y}\quad\text{for }f\in\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R^{n+1};E),\,\text{a.e.\ }\boldsymbol{x}=(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y)\in\R_{+}^{n+1}

defines a bounded linear operator
K\in\B(\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E))
with operator norm which is less or equal the norm
\left\|k\right\|_{p,p^{\prime},1}
of the kernel
k
.

For the kernel functions considered here, it proves helpful to define (analogously to
[
17
, Section 6.3]
) weighted versions of the functions
\mathcal{F}\boldsymbol{g}_{\rho}^{\mathrm{hs}}
and the semigroup associated to
\mathrm{i}\rho\boldsymbol{A}_{0}(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)
according to

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}\boldsymbol{g}_{\rho}^{2m}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},0)

\displaystyle=\rho^{2m}\mathcal{F}\boldsymbol{g}_{\rho}^{\mathrm{hs}}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},0)\in E^{m}

\displaystyle\text{for }\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime}\in\R^{n},\,\rho>0,

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}\boldsymbol{V}_{\rho}^{2m}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},y,\lambda)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\rho^{2m}}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\rho\boldsymbol{A}_{0}(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)y}\boldsymbol{M}(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)\in\B(E^{m},E^{2m})

\displaystyle\text{for }\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime}\in\R^{n},\,y>0\text{ and }\rho>0.

By formula (
11
), the identity

\mathcal{F}\boldsymbol{v}^{\mathrm{hs}}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},y,\lambda)=\mathcal{F}\boldsymbol{V}_{\rho}^{2m}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},y,\lambda)\mathcal{F}\boldsymbol{g}_{\rho}^{2m}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},0)\in E^{2m}

is valid.
Further, let us denote by

(12)

\boldsymbol{K}^{\lambda}(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y)=\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\mathcal{F}\boldsymbol{V}_{\rho}^{2m})(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y)=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{n}}\int_{\R^{n}}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\cdot\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime}}\mathcal{F}\boldsymbol{V}_{\rho}^{2m}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},y,\lambda)\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime}\in\B(E^{2m}),\quad\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\in\R^{n},\,y>0

the inverse (partial) Fourier transform (w.r.t.
\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime}\in\R^{n}
) of the
\mathcal{B}(E)
-valued function
\mathcal{F}\boldsymbol{V}_{\rho}^{2m}
.
Then, the solution to the elliptic boundary value problem

\begin{cases}(\lambda+\mathcal{A}(\mathrm{D}))w^{\mathrm{hs}}=0&\text{in }\R_{+}^{n+1},\\
\mathcal{B}_{j}(\mathrm{D})w^{\mathrm{hs}}=g_{j}&\text{on }\partial\R_{+}^{n+1},\,j=1,\ldots,m\end{cases}

is (formally) given by

w^{\mathrm{hs}}(\lambda,\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y)=\int_{\R^{n}}[\boldsymbol{K}^{\lambda}(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y)]_{1}\mathcal{F}^{-1}\boldsymbol{g}_{\rho}^{2m}(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\quad\text{for }\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\phi},\,\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\in\R^{n},\,y>0

where we denote by
[\boldsymbol{K}^{\lambda}(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y^{\prime})]_{1}
the first row of
\boldsymbol{K}^{\lambda}(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y^{\prime})
.
Here,
\mathcal{F}\boldsymbol{g}_{\rho}^{2m}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},y)\in E^{2m}
with components

(\mathcal{F}\boldsymbol{g}_{\rho}^{2m}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},y))_{j}=\sum_{k=0}^{m_{j}}\rho^{2m-k}\mathcal{F}g_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},y)=\sum_{k=0}^{m_{j}}(\left|\xi^{\prime}\right|^{2m}+\left|\lambda\right|)^{(2m-k)/2m}\mathcal{F}g_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},y).

Since
(\left|\xi^{\prime}\right|^{2m}+\left|\lambda\right|)^{(2m-k)/2m}
is the Fourier symbol of the pseudo-differential operator
((-\Delta_{\R^{n}})^{m}+\left|\lambda\right|)^{(2m-k)/2m}
for the realisation of the Laplacian
\Delta_{\R^{n}}
in
\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R^{n};E)
, we infer that

\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\mathcal{F}\boldsymbol{g}_{\rho}^{2m})_{j}(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y)=\sum_{k=0}^{m_{j}}((-\Delta_{\R^{n}})^{m}+\left|\lambda\right|)^{(2m-k)/2m}g_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y).

Denoting by
K^{\lambda}_{j}:=(\boldsymbol{K}^{\lambda})_{1,j}
the components
(1,j)
of the inverse Fourier transform
\boldsymbol{K}^{\lambda}
of the operator-valued function
\mathcal{F}\boldsymbol{V}_{\rho}^{2m}
as defined by (
12
) and
K^{\lambda}_{j,k}:=K^{\lambda}_{j}\mathcal{P}_{j,k}
,
j=1,\ldots,m
and
k=0,1,\ldots,m_{j}
, we observe the following:

\mathcal{F}\boldsymbol{v}^{\mathrm{hs}}=\mathcal{F}\boldsymbol{V}_{\rho}^{2m}\mathcal{F}\boldsymbol{g}_{\rho}^{2m},\quad\text{then}\quad\boldsymbol{v}^{\mathrm{hs}}=\boldsymbol{K}^{\lambda}\ast\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\mathcal{F}\boldsymbol{g}_{\rho}^{2m}).

With the help of Lemma
3.3
, we may now derive kernel estimates.

Proposition 4.7
.

Let
\mathcal{A}(\mathrm{D})
be a parameter-elliptic operator of order
2m
with angle of ellipticity
\phi_{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{ellipt}}\in[0,\pi)
.
Let
\phi>\phi_{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{ellipt}}
and assume that the Lopatinskii-Shapiro condition is valid.
Then, for every multi-index
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\N_{0}^{n+1}
, there are constants
M,c>0
such that

\displaystyle\left|\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\boldsymbol{K}^{\lambda}(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y)\right|

\displaystyle\leq M\cdot\left|\lambda\right|^{\frac{n-2m+\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|}{2m}}p_{2m,\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|-1}^{n+1}\left(c\left|\lambda\right|^{1/(2m)}\left(\left|\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\right|+\left|y\right|\right)\right),\quad\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\in\R^{n},\,y>0,\,\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\phi}.

Proof.

We proceed as in the proof of
[
17
, Propositions 6.5 & 6.6]
.
The proof heavily relies on theory of complex functions and, in particular, Cauchy’s Theorem.
We use that the identity

\mathcal{F}\boldsymbol{v}^{\mathrm{hs}}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},y,\lambda)=\mathcal{F}\boldsymbol{V}_{\rho}^{2m}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},y,\lambda)\mathcal{F}\boldsymbol{g}_{\rho}^{2m}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},0),\quad\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime}\in\R^{n},\,y>0,\,\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\phi}

is valid independent of the particular choice of
\rho>0
, so that later on, we may choose variables
(a,r)
and then choose
\rho
depending on
(a,r)
as
\rho=(\left|\lambda\right|+\left|\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime}\right|^{2m})^{1/2m}=(\left|\lambda\right|+(a^{2}+r^{2})^{m})^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2m}}}
for
\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\phi}
and
\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime}\in\R^{n}
such that
\left|\lambda\right|+\left|\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime}\right|>0
.
Fix
\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\in\R^{n}
and let
\boldsymbol{Q}\in\R^{n\times n}
be the (uniquely determined) rotation in
\R^{n}
which maps the vector
\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}
to a positive multiple of the first standard basis vector
\boldsymbol{Q}\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}=(\left|\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\right|,0,\ldots,0)^{\mathsf{T}}=:\left|\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\right|\boldsymbol{e}_{1}
. Using spherical coordinates in
\R^{n}
, we then write
\boldsymbol{Q}\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime}=(a,r\boldsymbol{\varphi})^{\mathsf{T}}
where
a\in\R
,
r>0
and
\boldsymbol{\varphi}\in\mathbb{S}^{n-2}=\{\boldsymbol{z}^{\prime}\in\R^{n-1}:\,\left|\boldsymbol{z}^{\prime}\right|=1\}
(the
n-2
-dimensional sphere), hence,
a^{2}+r^{2}=\left|\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime}\right|^{2}
.
Since, by convention,
\sigma=\tfrac{\lambda}{\rho^{2m}}
and
\boldsymbol{b}=\tfrac{\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime}}{\rho}
, the transformation rule will help us to estimate the kernels and their derivatives:
Using orthogonality of the rotation matrix
\boldsymbol{Q}
and the transformation rule, the integral kernel operator
\boldsymbol{K}^{\lambda}=\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{V}_{\rho}^{2m})
can be written as

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{K}^{\lambda}(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},\lambda)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{n}}\int_{\R^{n}}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\cdot\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime}}\mathcal{F}\boldsymbol{V}_{\rho}^{2m}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},y,\lambda)\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{n}}\int_{\R^{n}}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\boldsymbol{Q}\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\cdot\boldsymbol{Q}\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime}}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\rho\boldsymbol{A}_{0}(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)y}\boldsymbol{M}(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)\frac{1}{\rho^{2m}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n-2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}r^{n-2}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\left|\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\right|a}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\rho\boldsymbol{A}_{0}(\boldsymbol{Q}^{\mathsf{T}}(\tfrac{a}{\rho},\tfrac{r}{\rho}\boldsymbol{\varphi}),\tfrac{\lambda}{\rho^{2m}})y}\boldsymbol{M}(\boldsymbol{Q}^{\mathsf{T}}(\tfrac{a}{\rho},\tfrac{r}{\rho}\boldsymbol{\varphi}),\tfrac{\lambda}{\rho^{2m}})\frac{1}{\rho^{2m}}\,\mathrm{d}a\,\mathrm{d}r\,\mathrm{d}\sigma(\boldsymbol{\varphi})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n-2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}r^{n-2}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\left|\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\right|a}\frac{1}{(\left|\lambda\right|+(a^{2}+r^{2})^{m})^{2}}\boldsymbol{F}_{1}(\lambda,a,r,\boldsymbol{\varphi},y)\boldsymbol{F}_{2}(\lambda,a,r,\boldsymbol{\varphi})\,\mathrm{d}a\,\mathrm{d}r\,\mathrm{d}\sigma(\boldsymbol{\varphi})

for operator-valued functions
\boldsymbol{F}_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{F}_{2}
which are defined by

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{F}_{1}(\lambda,a,r,\boldsymbol{\varphi},y)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(\left|\lambda\right|+(a^{2}+r^{2})^{m})^{1/2m}\boldsymbol{A}_{0}((\left|\lambda\right|+(a^{2}+r^{2})^{m})^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2m}}}\boldsymbol{Q}^{\mathsf{T}}(a,r\boldsymbol{\varphi}),\lambda(\left|\lambda\right|+(a^{2}+r^{2})^{m})^{-1})y}\in\B(E^{2m}),

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{F}_{2}(\lambda,a,r,\boldsymbol{\varphi})

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{M}((\left|\lambda\right|+(a^{2}+r^{2})^{m})^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2m}}}\boldsymbol{Q}^{\mathsf{T}}(a,r\boldsymbol{\varphi}),\lambda(\left|\lambda\right|+(a^{2}+r^{2})^{m})^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2m}}})\in\B(E^{m};E^{2m})

for
\lambda\in\overline{\Sigma_{\pi-\phi}}
,
a\in\R
,
r\geq 0
,
\varphi\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
and
y>0
.
The inner integrand can be interpreted as a function depending holomorphically on
a\in\C
as the maps
\boldsymbol{M}(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)
depend holomorphically on
(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)
, and
\boldsymbol{M}(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)
maps into the stable part (corresponding to
S_{-}(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)
) of
\big(\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\rho\boldsymbol{A}_{0}(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)y}\big)_{y\geq 0}
.
Thus, we may and will replace the inner real integral
\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\ldots\,\mathrm{d}a
by a complex contour integral
\int_{\Gamma_{\varepsilon}}\ldots\,\mathrm{d}\omega
for a complex path
\Gamma_{\varepsilon}
which is parametrised by

\gamma_{\varepsilon}(s)=s+\mathrm{i}\varepsilon\left(\left|\lambda\right|+(r^{2}+s^{2})^{m}\right)^{1/2m},\quad s\in(-\infty,\infty)

for some (sufficiently small) parameter
\varepsilon>0
.

Figure 3.
The path
\Gamma_{\varepsilon}
.

Hence,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{K}^{\lambda}(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},\lambda)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n-2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}r^{n-2}\int_{\Gamma_{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\left|\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\right|a}\frac{1}{(\left|\lambda\right|+(a^{2}+r^{2})^{m})^{2}}

\displaystyle\qquad\cdot\boldsymbol{F}_{1}(\lambda,a,r,\boldsymbol{\varphi},y)\boldsymbol{F}_{2}(\lambda,a,r,\boldsymbol{\varphi})\,\mathrm{d}a\,\mathrm{d}r\,\mathrm{d}\sigma(\boldsymbol{\varphi}).

We proceed just as in the proof of
[
17
, Proposition 6.5]
.
First, we find that

\left|\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\left|\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\right|\gamma_{\varepsilon}(s)}\right|=\mathrm{e}^{-\left|\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\right|\operatorname{Im}\,(\gamma_{\varepsilon}(s))}=\mathrm{e}^{-\varepsilon\left|\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\right|(\left|\lambda\right|+(r^{2}+s^{2})^{m})^{1/2m}}.

Moreover, we may estimate the function
\boldsymbol{F}_{1}
as follows:

\left\|\boldsymbol{F}_{1}(\lambda,\gamma_{\varepsilon}(s),r,\boldsymbol{\varphi},y)\right\|_{\B(E^{n})}\leq M\mathrm{e}^{-c_{1}y}.

In fact, from
\sup\{\operatorname{Re}\,\mu:\mu\in S_{-}(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)\}<-c_{1}<0
it follows that for every
(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}\times\Sigma_{\pi-\phi}
with
\left|\sigma\right|\leq 1
, there is a constant
M_{\boldsymbol{b},\sigma}\geq 1
such that

\left\|\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\boldsymbol{A}_{0}(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)y}P_{-}(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)\right\|_{\B(E^{n})}\leq M_{\boldsymbol{b},\sigma}\mathrm{e}^{-c_{1}y}\quad\text{for all }y\geq 0.

Here,
\boldsymbol{A}_{0}(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)\in\B(E^{2m})
is a continuous linear operator and depends continuously on the parameters
(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}\times\Sigma_{\pi-\phi}
.
Therefore, each (uniformly continuous) semigroup
\big(\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\boldsymbol{A}_{0}(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)y}\big)_{y\geq 0}\subseteq\B(E^{n})
satisfies the spectral-determined growth bound condition and, hence, by (
9
) its restriction to the image under the spectral projection
P_{-}(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)
on the stable part has a growth bound which is strictly less than
-c_{1}
.
Moreover, as
\boldsymbol{A}_{0}(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)
depends holomorphically on the parameters
(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)
, so do the (uniformly continuous) semigroups
\big(\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}A_{0}(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)y}\big)_{y\geq 0}
.
Thus, the constant
M_{\boldsymbol{b},\sigma}
can be chosen uniformly as
M\geq 1
for all
(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}\times\Sigma_{\pi-\phi}
with
\left|\sigma\right|\leq 1
.
This establishes the estimate on
\boldsymbol{F}_{1}
.

For the function
\boldsymbol{F}_{2}
, observe that

\left\|\boldsymbol{F}_{2}(\lambda,a,r,\varphi)\right\|_{\B(E^{n})}=\left\|\boldsymbol{M}((\left|\lambda\right|+(a^{2}+r^{2})^{m})^{-1/2m}\boldsymbol{Q}^{\mathsf{T}}(a,r\boldsymbol{\varphi}),\lambda(\left|\lambda\right|+(a^{2}+r^{2})^{m})^{-1})\right\|_{\B(E^{n})},

where
(\left|\lambda\right|+(a^{2}+r^{2})^{m})^{-1/2m}\boldsymbol{Q}^{\mathsf{T}}(a,r\boldsymbol{\varphi})\in\R^{n}
has norm less or equal
1
and
\lambda(\left|\lambda\right|+(a^{2}+r^{2})^{m})^{-1}\in\Sigma_{\pi-\phi}
has modulus less or equal
1
.
Since the map
(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)\mapsto\boldsymbol{M}(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)\in\B(E^{n})
is holomorphic,

\sup_{(\lambda,a,r,\boldsymbol{\varphi})}\left\|\boldsymbol{F}_{2}(\lambda,a,r,\boldsymbol{\varphi})\right\|_{\B(E^{n})}<\infty,

where we take the supremum over all admissible values for
(\lambda,a,r,\boldsymbol{\varphi})
.
For the calculations following next, let us remark that the functions

\rho(\lambda,s,r)=\big(\lambda+(s^{2}+r^{2})^{m}\big)^{1/2m}\quad\text{and}\quad\tilde{\rho}(\lambda,s,r)=\big(\lambda^{1/m}+s^{2}+r^{2}\big)^{1/2}

on
\R_{+}\times\R\times\R

(the latter having been the choice employed in
[
17
]
) are comparable in the sense that there is a constant
c>0
such that

\frac{1}{c}\tilde{\rho}(\lambda,r,s)\leq\rho(\lambda,r,s)\leq c\tilde{\rho}(\lambda,r,s).

This can be seen, e.g., by employing the binomial formula to estimate

\tilde{\rho}(\lambda,s,r)^{2m}=\sum_{k=0}^{m}\binom{m}{k}\left|\lambda\right|^{k/m}(s^{2}+r^{2})^{m-k}\leq C(\left|\lambda\right|+(s^{2}+r^{2})^{m})=\rho(\lambda,s,r)^{2m}

and the trivial estimate

\rho(\lambda,s,r)^{2}=\big(\left|\lambda\right|+(s^{2}+r^{2})^{m}\big)^{1/m}\leq\big(2\left|\lambda\right|\big)^{1/m}+\big(2(s^{2}+r^{2})^{m}\big)^{1/m}=2^{1/m}\tilde{\rho}(\lambda,s,r)^{2}.

We may, therefore, readily estimate
\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\boldsymbol{K}^{\lambda}(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y)
for every multi-index
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=(\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime},\alpha_{n+1})\in\N_{0}^{n+1}
:

\displaystyle\left\|\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\boldsymbol{K}^{\lambda}(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y)\right\|_{\B(E^{2m})}

\displaystyle=\left\|\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{n}}\int_{\R^{n}}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime})^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime}}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\cdot\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime}}\mathrm{D}_{y}^{\alpha_{n+1}}(\mathcal{F}\boldsymbol{V}_{\rho}^{2m}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},y,\lambda))\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime}\right\|_{\B(E^{n})}

\displaystyle=\left\|\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n-2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}r^{n-2}\int_{\Gamma_{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\left|\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\right|a}(\rho\boldsymbol{b})^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime}}\frac{1}{\left|\lambda\right|+(s^{2}+r^{2})^{m}}(\rho\boldsymbol{A}_{0}(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma))^{\alpha_{n+1}}\boldsymbol{F}_{1}\boldsymbol{F}_{2}\,\mathrm{d}a\,\mathrm{d}r\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{S}^{n-2}(\boldsymbol{\varphi})\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{(2\pi)^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n-2}}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{S}^{n-2}(\boldsymbol{\varphi})\int_{0}^{\infty}r^{n-2}\int_{\Gamma_{\varepsilon}}\frac{1}{(\left|\lambda\right|+(s^{2}+r^{2})^{m})^{\frac{2m-\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|}{2m}}}\mathrm{e}^{-(\left|\lambda\right|+(s^{2}+r^{2})^{m})^{1/2m}\cdot(\varepsilon\left|\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\right|+c_{1}y)}|\mathrm{d}a|\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle\leq C_{\varepsilon_{0}}\int_{0}^{\infty}r^{n-2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-(\left|\lambda\right|+(s^{2}+r^{2})^{m})^{1/2m}\cdot(\varepsilon\left|\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\right|+c_{1}y)}\cdot\frac{1}{(\left|\lambda\right|+(s^{2}+r^{2})^{m})^{\frac{2m-\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|}{2m}}}\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle\leq C_{\varepsilon_{0}}\int_{0}^{\infty}r^{n-2}\int_{r}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-c(\left|\lambda\right|^{1/m}+(\tau-r)^{2}+r^{2})^{1/2}\cdot(\varepsilon\left|\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\right|+c_{1}y)}\cdot\frac{1}{(\left|\lambda\right|^{1/2m}+\tau)^{2m-\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|}}\,\mathrm{d}\tau\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle\leq C_{\varepsilon_{0}}\int_{0}^{\infty}r^{n-2}\int_{r}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-c\varepsilon\cdot(\left|\lambda\right|^{1/2m}+\tau)\cdot\left|\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\right|}\mathrm{e}^{-cc_{1}(\left|\lambda\right|^{1/2m}+\tau)y}\cdot\frac{1}{(\left|\lambda\right|^{1/2m}+\tau)^{2m-\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|}}\,\mathrm{d}\tau\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle\leq C_{\varepsilon_{0}}\int_{0}^{\infty}r^{n-2}\int_{r}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-c(\varepsilon\left|\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\right|+c_{1}y)\cdot(\left|\lambda\right|^{1/2m}+\tau)}\frac{1}{(\left|\lambda\right|^{1/2m}+\tau)^{2m-\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|}}\,\mathrm{d}\tau\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle=C_{\varepsilon_{0}}\left|\lambda\right|^{\frac{1}{2m}-\frac{2m-\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|}{2m}+\frac{n-1}{2m}}\int_{0}^{\infty}s^{n-2}\int_{s}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-c(\varepsilon\left|\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\right|+c_{1}y)\left|\lambda\right|^{1/2m}(1+\tilde{\tau})}\frac{1}{(1+\tilde{\tau})^{2m-\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|}}\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{\tau}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=C_{\varepsilon_{0}}\left|\lambda\right|^{\frac{n}{2m}-1+\frac{\left|\alpha\right|}{2m}}\frac{1}{n-1}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\tilde{\tau}^{n-1}}{(1+\tilde{\tau})^{2m-\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|}}\mathrm{e}^{-c\left|\lambda\right|^{1/2m}(\varepsilon\left|\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\right|+c_{1}y)(1+\tilde{\tau})}\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{\tau}

\displaystyle=\frac{C_{\varepsilon_{0}}}{n-1}\left|\lambda\right|^{\frac{n}{m}-1+\frac{\left|\alpha\right|}{2m}}p_{2m,\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|-1}^{n+1}(c\left|\lambda\right|^{1/2m}(\varepsilon\left|\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\right|+c_{1}y))

\displaystyle\leq C_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{\prime}\left|\lambda\right|^{\frac{n-2m+\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|}{2m}}p_{2m,\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|-1}^{n+1}(c_{\varepsilon_{0}}\left|\lambda\right|^{1/2m}(\left|\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\right|+y))

for some constants
C_{\varepsilon_{0}}
,
C_{\varepsilon_{0}}^{\prime}
and
c_{\varepsilon_{0}}>0
, for sufficiently small
\varepsilon_{0}>0
, and all
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}]
.
Thus, we have established the kernel estimate

\displaystyle\left|\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\boldsymbol{K}^{\lambda}(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y)\right|

\displaystyle\leq M\left|\lambda\right|^{\frac{n-2m+\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|}{2m}}p_{2m,\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|-1}^{n+1}(c\left|\lambda\right|^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}m}(\left|\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\right|+\left|y\right|))\quad\text{for }\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\in\R^{n},\,y>0,\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\phi}.

∎

Proposition 4.8
.

Let
\mathcal{A}(\mathrm{D})
be a parameter-elliptic operator of order
2m
with angle of ellipticity
\phi_{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{ellipt}}\in[0,\pi)
.
Let
\phi>\phi_{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{ellipt}}
and assume that the Lopatinskii–Shapiro condition is satisfied.
Then, for every multi-index
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\N_{0}^{n+1}
, there are constants
M,c>0
such that

\left|\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\boldsymbol{K}^{\mathrm{corr},\lambda}_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y,\tilde{y})\right|\leq M\left|\lambda\right|^{\frac{n+1-2m+\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|}{2m}}p_{2m,\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|}^{n+1}\left(c\left|\lambda\right|^{1/(2m)}(\left|\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\right|+y+\tilde{y})\right)

for all
\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\in\R^{n},\,y,\tilde{y}>0
and
\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\phi}
, where

\boldsymbol{K}^{\mathrm{corr},\lambda}_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y,\tilde{y})=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{n}}\int_{\R^{n}}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\cdot\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime}}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\rho\boldsymbol{A}_{0}(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)y}\boldsymbol{M}(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)\boldsymbol{h}^{\mathrm{corr}}_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},\tilde{y})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime}.

Proof.

Consider the inverse Fourier transform of the function

\mathcal{F}w^{\mathrm{corr}}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},y,\lambda)=-\big[\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\rho\boldsymbol{A}_{0}(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)y}\boldsymbol{M}(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)(\mathcal{F}\boldsymbol{g}_{\rho}^{\mathrm{corr}})(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},0)\big]_{1}

corresponding to the correction term for
\mathcal{B}_{j}(\mathrm{D})\mathcal{F}w^{\mathrm{fs}}|_{\partial\R_{+}^{n+1}}
, hence,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{K}^{\mathrm{corr},\lambda}_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y,\tilde{y})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{n}}\int_{\R^{n}}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\cdot\boldsymbol{\xi}}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\rho\boldsymbol{A}_{0}(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)y}\boldsymbol{M}(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)\boldsymbol{h}_{j,k}^{\mathrm{corr}}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},\tilde{y})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime}

where
\boldsymbol{h}_{j}^{\mathrm{corr}}=(0,\ldots,0,h_{j}^{\mathrm{corr}},0,\ldots,0)^{\mathsf{T}}=(0,\ldots,0,\sum_{k=0}^{m_{j}}h_{j,k}^{\mathrm{corr}},0,\ldots,0)^{\mathsf{T}}=\sum_{k=0}^{m_{j}}\boldsymbol{h}_{j,k}^{\mathrm{corr}}
.
We recall that the family
\big(\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\rho\boldsymbol{A}_{0}(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)y}\boldsymbol{M}(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)\boldsymbol{h}_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},0)\big)_{y\geq 0}
is the norm-continuous semigroup corresponding to the (parameter-dependent) initial value problem

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial y}\mathcal{F}\boldsymbol{v}^{\mathrm{corr}}_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},y,\lambda)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{i}\rho\boldsymbol{A}_{0}(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)\mathcal{F}\boldsymbol{v}^{\mathrm{corr}}_{j,k},\quad y>0,

\displaystyle B_{\tilde{j}}^{0}(\boldsymbol{b})\mathcal{F}\boldsymbol{v}^{\mathrm{corr}}_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},0,\lambda)

\displaystyle=\delta_{j,\tilde{j}}\sum_{\tilde{k}=0}^{m_{\tilde{j}}}\delta_{k,\tilde{k}}\mathcal{P}_{\tilde{j},\tilde{k}}\frac{\mathcal{F}g_{\tilde{j}}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime})}{\rho^{\tilde{k}}},\quad\tilde{j}=1,\ldots,m.

Since the function
\mathcal{F}\boldsymbol{v}^{\mathrm{corr}}
is the solution to the boundary value problem

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{y}\mathcal{F}\boldsymbol{v}^{\mathrm{corr}}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},y,\lambda)

\displaystyle=\rho\boldsymbol{A}_{0}(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)\mathcal{F}\boldsymbol{v}^{\mathrm{corr}}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},y,\lambda),

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime}\in\R^{n},\,y>0,

\displaystyle B_{j}^{0}(\boldsymbol{b})\mathcal{F}\boldsymbol{v}^{\mathrm{corr}}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},0)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{m_{j}}\mathcal{P}_{j,k}\frac{\mathcal{F}g_{j}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},0)}{\rho^{k}},

\displaystyle j=1,\ldots,m,\,\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime}\in\R^{n},

we deduce that

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}w^{\mathrm{corr}}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},y,\lambda)

\displaystyle=\big[\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\rho\boldsymbol{A}_{0}(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)y}(\mathcal{F}\boldsymbol{v}^{\mathrm{corr}})(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},0)\big]_{1}=\big[\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\rho\boldsymbol{A}_{0}(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)y}M(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)\mathcal{F}\boldsymbol{g}_{\rho}^{\mathrm{corr}}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},0)\big]_{1}

\displaystyle=\big[\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\rho\boldsymbol{A}_{0}(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)y}\boldsymbol{M}(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)\big(\sum_{k=0}^{m_{j}}\mathcal{P}_{j,k}\frac{\mathcal{F}g_{j}^{\mathrm{corr}}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},0)}{\rho^{k}}\big)_{j}\big]_{1}.

By Cauchy’s theorem and homogeneity considerations

\displaystyle h_{j,k}^{\mathrm{corr}}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},\tilde{y})

\displaystyle=\rho^{k+1-2m}\int_{\R}\mathcal{B}_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{b},a)(\sigma+\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{b},a))^{-1}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\tilde{y}\rho a}\,\mathrm{d}a

\displaystyle=\rho^{k+1-2m}\int_{\Gamma^{-}}\mathcal{B}_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{b},a)(\sigma+\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{b},a))^{-1}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\tilde{y}\rho a}\,\mathrm{d}a,

where
\Gamma^{-}
is a closed curve in the open lower complex plane which encircles
-\mathrm{i}P_{+}(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)
, uniformly for all
(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)
.

Figure 4.
A path
\Gamma^{-}
surrounding
-\mathrm{i}P_{+}(\boldsymbol{b},a)
.

We then obtain, after using a rotation as above and shifting the path of integration as in
[
17
]
, that

(13)

\left|h_{j,k}^{\mathrm{corr}}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},\tilde{y})\right|\leq C\left(\left|\lambda\right|+r^{2m}+s^{2m}\right)^{\nicefrac{{k+1-2m}}{{2m}}}\mathrm{e}^{-c\tilde{y}(\left|\lambda\right|+r^{2m}+s^{2m})^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2m}}}},\quad\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime}\in\R^{n},\,y>0

for sufficiently small values of
\left|\operatorname{arg}(r^{2}+a^{2})\right|
.
For any multi-index
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\N_{0}^{n+1}
, we, thus, obtain the estimate

\displaystyle\left|\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}K^{\mathrm{corr,\lambda}}_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y,\tilde{y})\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}K^{\mathrm{corr,\lambda}}_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y,\tilde{y})\right|

\displaystyle\leq M\left|\lambda\right|^{\frac{n-2m+1+\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|}{2m}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{s^{n-1}}{(1+s)^{2m-1-\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|}}\mathrm{e}^{-c(s+1)\left|\lambda\right|^{1/(2m)}(\left|\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\right|+y+\tilde{y})}\,\mathrm{d}s,

cf. the calculations on
[
17
, p. 80]
.
∎

4.4.
Solution operators

Using the kernel estimates from the last subsection, we can now derive
\mathrm{L}_{p}
-estimates for
w^{\mathrm{fs}}
,
w^{\mathrm{hs}}
and
w^{\mathrm{corr}}
.
For this purpose, let us introduce the
\mathrm{L}_{p}
-realisation for the boundary value problem (
4
)–(
5
) as the closure
A\&B=\overline{(A\&B)_{\mathrm{min}}}
of the
minimal operator

(A\&B)_{\mathrm{min}}
which is defined by

\displaystyle(A\&B)_{\mathrm{min}}:\dom((A\&B)_{\mathrm{min}})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2m}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E)\subseteq\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E)

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E)\times\prod_{j=1}^{m}\left(\sum_{k=0}^{m_{j}}\mathcal{P}_{j,k}\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2m-k}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E)\right),

\displaystyle(A\&B)_{\mathrm{min}}u

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathcal{A}(\mathrm{D})\\
\mathcal{B}_{1}(\mathrm{D})\\
\vdots\\
\mathcal{B}_{m}(\mathrm{D})\end{array}\right)u.

Remark 4.9
.

Note that the differential operators
\mathcal{B}_{j}(\mathrm{D})
corresponding to the boundary symbols act on the function
u:\R_{+}^{n+1}\rightarrow E
and are evaluated on the half-space
\R_{+}^{n+1}
, and not only on its boundary trace
\partial\R_{+}^{n+1}
.

Also, it may not be immediately clear that the operator
(A\&B)_{\mathrm{min}}
is closable, thus let us comment on that.
We may introduce the higher-order reflexion
\mathcal{E}u(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y):=\sum_{i=0}^{2m}\alpha_{i}u(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},-\frac{y}{i+1})
for
\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\in\R^{n}
and
y<0
, which for suitable chosen scalar coefficients
\alpha_{i}\in\R
defines a continuous extension of functions
u\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2m}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E)
to functions
\mathcal{E}u\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2m}(\R^{n+1};E)
.
Then, given any sequence
(u_{n})_{n\geq 1}\subseteq\dom((A\&B)_{\mathrm{min}})
such that
u_{n}\rightarrow 0
and
\mathcal{A}(\mathrm{D})u_{n}\rightarrow v
in
\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E)
for some function
v\in\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E)
, from the definition of the extension operator it follows that
\mathcal{E}u_{n}\rightarrow 0
and
\mathcal{A}(\mathrm{D})u_{n}\rightarrow\tilde{v}
in
\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R^{n+1};E)
for the extension
\tilde{v}=\mathcal{E}v\in\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R^{n+1};E)
of
v
.
We claim that
\tilde{v}=0
, which will imply that
v=v|_{\R_{+}^{n+1}}=0
and, hence,
(A\&B)_{\mathrm{min}}
is closable.
To this end, we use that
\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{\xi})
is parameter-elliptic, so that for every
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\N_{0}^{n+1}
of length
\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|\leq 2m
, the function
\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{\xi})+1)^{-1}
is an
\mathcal{R}
-bounded Fourier multiplier.
From
(\mathcal{A}(\mathrm{D})+1)\mathcal{E}u_{n}=\mathcal{A}(\mathrm{D})\mathcal{E}u_{n}+\mathcal{E}u_{n}=\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{A}(\mathrm{D})u_{n}+u_{n})\rightarrow\tilde{v}
it, thus, follows that also
\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\mathcal{E}u_{n}
converges in
\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R^{n+1};E)
for each multi-index
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\N_{0}^{n+1}
of length
\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|\leq 2m
.
Since
\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2m}(\R^{n+1};E)
is a Banach space and
\mathcal{E}u_{n}\rightarrow 0
, however, this can only be the case if
\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(\mathcal{E}u_{n})\rightarrow\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(\mathcal{E}0)=0
, which in turn implies that
\tilde{v}=0
.
This shows that the operator
(A\&B)_{\mathrm{min}}
is indeed closable.

For each parameter
\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\phi}
and functions
g_{j}\in\sum_{k=0}^{m_{j}}\mathcal{P}_{j,k}\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2m-k}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E)
(for the moment defined on
\R_{+}^{n+1}
,
not
only on
\partial\R_{+}^{n+1}
) we consider the system of differential equations

(14)

\lambda\boldsymbol{J}v+(A\&B)v=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}0\\
\boldsymbol{g}\end{array}\right),

where we write
\boldsymbol{g}=(g_{1},\ldots,g_{m})^{\mathsf{T}}
and
\boldsymbol{J}v=(v,0,\ldots,0)^{\mathsf{T}}\in E^{1+m}
for
v\in E
, and get the following result in which we consider two cases: On the one hand, the situation as above or in
[
17
, Proposition 6.8]
, where
\boldsymbol{g}
is given from the start, and on the other hand, the more subtle case where
\boldsymbol{g}
is only given on
\partial\R_{+}^{n+1}
, but we use a particular extension to
\R_{+}^{n+1}
which allows us to assume that
\partial_{y}g_{j,k}=-L_{\lambda}^{1/2m}g_{j,k}
in
\R_{+}^{n+1}
, thus relating the partial derivative in
y
-direction with a fractional differential operator acting on the
\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}
-components of the function
\boldsymbol{g}
.

Remark 4.10
.

We will use that the pseudo-differential operator
-L_{\lambda}^{1/2m}=-(\left|\lambda\right|+(-\Delta)^{m})^{1/2m}
, i.e. the linear operator associated to the Fourier symbol
-(\left|\lambda\right|+\left|\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime}\right|^{2m})^{1/2m}
, for fixed
\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\phi}
and
\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime}\in\R^{n}
, generates a bounded
\mathrm{C}_{0}
-semigroup on the Bochner–Lebesgue space
\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R^{n};E)
for any given
p\in[1,\infty)
.
In fact, for every
\mu>0
and
\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\phi}
, the resolvent problem

(\mu+L_{\lambda}^{1/2m})u=f

can be solved in Fourier space as

(\mu+(\left|\lambda\right|+\left|\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime}\right|^{2m})^{1/2m})\hat{u}=\hat{f}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\hat{u}=\frac{1}{\mu+(\left|\lambda\right|+\left|\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime}\right|^{2m})^{1/2m}}\hat{f}=:m_{\mu}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime})\hat{f},

where
m_{\mu}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime})
is scalar, and for boundedness of the resolvent, it, thus, suffices to prove boundedness of
m_{\mu}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime})
and
(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime})^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}D_{\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime}}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}m_{\mu}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime})
for all
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\N_{0}^{n}
of length
\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|\leq\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor
, which can easily be checked, and in fact gives
\left\|(\mu(\mu+L_{\lambda}^{1/2m})^{-1})^{k}\right\|\leq C
for all
\mu>0
. Hence, by the Hille–Yosida Theorem
-L_{\lambda}^{1/2m}
generates a bounded
C_{0}
-semigroup on
\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R^{n};E)
, for every
\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\phi}
.
For details, we refer to Proposition
7.1
in the appendix.

We denote by
L_{\lambda}
the operator
\left|\lambda\right|+(-\Delta_{\R^{n}})^{m}
on
\mathrm{L}^{p}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E)
, where
\Delta_{\R^{n}}
is the closure of the minimal realisation of the (
E
-valued) Laplace operator on
\R^{n}
, and extend the functions
g_{j,k}
on the boundary
\partial\R_{+}^{n+1}\cong\R^{n}
with use of the
C_{0}
-semigroup
(\mathrm{e}^{-L_{\lambda}^{1/2m}y})_{y\geq 0}
on
\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R^{n};E)
, which is generated by
-L_{\lambda}^{1/2m}
to functions

(15)

(\mathcal{E}_{\lambda}g_{j,k})(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y)=\mathrm{e}^{-L_{\lambda}^{1/2m}y}g_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y)\quad\text{for }\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\in\R^{n},\,y\geq 0.

This defines functions
\mathcal{E}_{\lambda}g_{j}=\sum_{k=0}^{m_{j}}\mathcal{E}_{\lambda}g_{j,k}\in\sum_{k=0}^{m_{j}}\mathcal{P}_{j,k}\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2m-k}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E)
.
This can be seen as follows:
By assumption
\mathcal{P}_{j,k^{\prime}}(\ran(\mathcal{P}_{j,k}))=\{0\}
for
k\neq k^{\prime}
, and then by continuity of the projection operators also
\mathcal{P}_{j,k^{\prime}}(\overline{\ran(\mathcal{P}_{j,k})})=\{0\}
.
Moreover, both the Fourier-transform and its inverse leave the spaces
\overline{\ran(\mathcal{P}_{j,k})}
invariant, e.g.
\ran(\mathcal{F}v)\subseteq\overline{\ran(\mathcal{P}_{j,k})}
whenever
\ran(v)\subseteq\overline{\ran(\mathcal{P}_{j,k})}
and the same is true for the scalar Fourier-symbol
(\left|\lambda\right|+\left|\boldsymbol{\xi}\right|^{2m})^{1/2m}
of the operator
L_{\lambda}^{1/2m}
.
Therefore, all spaces
\overline{\ran(\mathcal{P}_{j,k})}
are
L_{\lambda}^{1/2m}
-invariant, i.e.
L_{\lambda}^{1/2m}(\dom(L_{\lambda}^{1/2m})\cap\overline{\ran(\mathcal{P}_{j,k})})\subseteq\overline{\ran(\mathcal{P}_{j,k})}
, and, then, these spaces are
(\mathrm{e}^{-yL_{\lambda}^{1/2m}})_{y\geq 0}
-invariant as well.

Proposition 4.11
.

Let
p\in(1,\infty)
and
\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\phi}
be given.

(1)

Let
g_{j}=\sum_{k=0}^{m_{j}}g_{j,k}\in\sum_{k=0}^{m_{j}}\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2m-k}(\R^{n};\ran(\mathcal{P}_{j,k}))
for
j=1,\ldots,m
be given.

Then there is a unique solution
v^{\mathrm{hs}}\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2m-1}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E)\cap\dom(A\&B)
of the initial-boundary value problem (
14
) for
\mathcal{E}_{\lambda}g_{j,k}
as in (
15
) taking the role of
g_{j,k}:\R_{+}^{n+1}\rightarrow E
.
It is given by
v^{\mathrm{hs}}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\sum_{k=0}^{m_{j}}v^{\mathrm{hs}}_{j,k}
for functions

v^{\mathrm{hs}}_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y)=T^{\lambda}_{j,k}h^{\lambda}_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y)

where

\displaystyle(T^{\lambda}_{j,k}h^{\lambda}_{j,k})(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\R^{n}}T_{j,k}(\lambda,\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{\tilde{x}},y+\tilde{y})h^{\lambda}_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{\tilde{x}}^{\prime},\tilde{y})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\tilde{x}}^{\prime}\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{y}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\R^{n}}T_{j,k}(\lambda,\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{\tilde{x}},y+\tilde{y})L_{\lambda}^{\frac{2m-k}{2m}}\mathcal{E}_{\lambda}g_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{\tilde{x}}^{\prime},\tilde{y})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\tilde{x}}^{\prime}\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{y}

and

\displaystyle h^{\lambda}_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y)

\displaystyle=L_{\lambda}^{\frac{2m-k}{2m}}\mathrm{e}^{-L_{\lambda}^{1/2m}y}\mathcal{E}_{\lambda}g_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{-L_{\lambda}^{1/2m}y}L_{\lambda}^{\frac{2m-k}{2m}}\mathcal{E}_{\lambda}g_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y)\quad\text{for }\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\in\R^{n}\text{ and }y>0.

Here, the operator-valued kernel function
T^{\lambda}_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y)=T(\lambda,\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y)
is defined by

T^{\lambda}_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y)=T_{j,k}(\lambda,\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y)=\big(\frac{\partial}{\partial y}K_{j,k}^{\lambda}-K_{j,k}^{\lambda}L_{\lambda}^{1/2m}\big)(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y)

Moreover, there are constants
C>0
(independent of the parameter
\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\phi}
and the boundary data
g_{j}
) such that for every
\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\phi}
,
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\N_{0}^{n+1}
and
j=1,\ldots,m
the following estimates are valid:

\displaystyle\left\|\left|\lambda\right|^{1-\frac{\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|}{2m}}\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}T^{\lambda}_{j,k}h^{\lambda}_{j,k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E)}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|((-\Delta_{\R^{n}})^{m}+\left|\lambda\right|)^{\frac{2m-k}{2m}}g_{j,k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R^{n};E)},

\displaystyle 0\leq\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|\leq 2m,

\displaystyle\left\|\left|\lambda\right|^{\frac{k-2m}{2m}}\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}T^{\lambda}_{j,k}h^{\lambda}_{j,k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E)}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|g_{j,k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R^{n};E)},

\displaystyle 0\leq\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|\leq k\leq m_{j},

\displaystyle\left\|\left|\lambda\right|^{2-\frac{\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|}{2m}}\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\partial_{\lambda}T^{\lambda}_{j,k}h^{\lambda}_{j,k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E)}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|g_{j,k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R^{n};E)},

\displaystyle 0\leq\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|\leq k\leq m_{j}.

(2)

Given functions
g_{j,k}\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2m-k}(\R_{+}^{n+1};\ran(\mathcal{P}_{j,k}))
, we set
h^{\lambda}_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y)=L_{\lambda}^{\frac{2m-k}{2m}}g_{j,k}
.
Then, the following estimates are valid:

\displaystyle\left\|\left|\lambda\right|^{1-\frac{\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|}{2m}}\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}S^{\lambda,I}_{j,k}g_{j,k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E)}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|((-\Delta_{\R^{n}})^{m}+\left|\lambda\right|)^{\frac{2m-k}{2m}}g_{j,k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E)},

\displaystyle\left\|\left|\lambda\right|^{1-\frac{\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|}{2m}}\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}S^{\lambda,II}_{j,k}g_{j,k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E)}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|((-\Delta_{\R^{n}})^{m}+\left|\lambda\right|)^{\frac{2m-k-1}{2m}}\partial_{y}g_{j,k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E)},

\displaystyle\left\|\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}S^{\lambda,I}_{j,k}g_{j,k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E)}

\displaystyle\leq C\left|\lambda\right|^{\frac{\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|-k}{2m}}\left\|g_{j,k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E)},

\displaystyle\left\|\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}S^{\lambda,II}_{j,k}g_{j,k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E)}

\displaystyle\leq C\left|\lambda\right|^{\frac{\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|-k-1}{2m}}\left\|\frac{\partial}{\partial_{y}}g_{j,k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E)}.

Remark 4.12
.

A result analogous to part (
2
) can be found in
[
17
, Proposition 6.8]
.
On the other hand, in part (
1
) (and in
[
18
]
) one starts with functions defined on
\R^{n}
and uses the particular extension
\mathcal{E}_{\lambda}g_{j}
to a function on
\R_{+}^{n+1}
.
A convenient property of this particular extension is the identity

\frac{\partial}{\partial y}h^{\lambda}_{j,k}=\frac{\partial}{\partial y}\mathrm{e}^{-L_{\lambda}^{1/2m}y}L_{\lambda}^{\frac{2m-k}{2m}}g_{j,k}=-L_{\lambda}^{1/2m}h^{\lambda}_{j,k}.

Proof of Proposition
4.11
.

We will focus on part (
1
). Part (
2
) follows with slight modifications, cf.
[
17
, Proposition 6.8]
.
Having the integral formulas for the solution of the IBVP at hand, it remains to prove the asserted
\mathrm{L}_{p}
-estimates.
In fact, from the previous subsection (i.e. for
f=0
, hence,
\mathcal{F}w^{\mathrm{fs}}=\mathcal{F}w^{\mathrm{corr}}=0
), we already know that

w^{\mathrm{hs}}=[\boldsymbol{v}^{\mathrm{hs}}]_{1}\quad\text{for }\boldsymbol{v}^{\mathrm{hs}}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\sum_{k=0}^{m_{j}}\boldsymbol{v}^{\mathrm{hs}}_{j,k}

with

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}^{\mathrm{hs}}(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}^{-1}\big(\mathcal{F}\boldsymbol{V}_{\rho}^{2m}\cdot\mathcal{F}\boldsymbol{g}_{\rho}^{2m}\big)(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y)

\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{n}\big(\mathcal{F}^{-1}\boldsymbol{V}_{\rho}^{2m}\ast_{\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}}\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\mathcal{F}\boldsymbol{g}_{\rho}^{2m})\big)(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y)

\displaystyle=\int_{\R^{n}}\boldsymbol{K}^{\lambda}(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{\tilde{x}}^{\prime},y)\mathcal{F}^{-1}\big(\sum_{k=0}^{m_{j}}\rho^{2m-k}\mathcal{F}g_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{\tilde{x}}^{\prime})\big)_{j=1}^{m}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\tilde{x}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\R^{n}}\big[\boldsymbol{K}^{\lambda}(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{\tilde{x}}^{\prime},y+\tilde{y})\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\sum_{k=0}^{m_{j}}\rho^{2m-k}\mathrm{e}^{-\rho\tilde{y}}\mathcal{F}g_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{\tilde{x}}^{\prime})\big]_{y=0}

\displaystyle=-\int_{\R^{n}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\partial}{\partial\tilde{y}}\big(\boldsymbol{K}^{\lambda}(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{\tilde{x}}^{\prime},y+\tilde{y})\mathcal{F}^{-1}\big(\sum_{k=0}^{m_{j}}\rho^{2m-k}\mathrm{e}^{-\rho\tilde{y}}\mathcal{F}g_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{\tilde{x}^{\prime}})\big)_{j=1}^{m}\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{y}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\tilde{x}}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=-\int_{\R^{n}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\big(\frac{\partial}{\partial\tilde{y}}\boldsymbol{K}^{\lambda}(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{\tilde{x}}^{\prime},y+\tilde{y})-\boldsymbol{K}^{\lambda}(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{\tilde{x}}^{\prime},y+\tilde{y})((-\Delta_{\R^{n}})^{m}+\left|\lambda\right|)^{1/2m}\big)

\displaystyle\qquad\cdot\big(\sum_{k=0}^{m_{j}}((-\Delta_{\R^{n}})^{m}+\left|\lambda\right|)^{(2m-k)/2m}\mathrm{e}^{-L_{\lambda}^{1/2m}\tilde{y}}g_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{\tilde{x}}^{\prime})\big)_{j=1}^{m}

\displaystyle=-\big(\sum_{k=0}^{m_{j}}\int_{\R^{n}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\big(\frac{\partial}{\partial y}K^{\lambda}_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{\tilde{x}},y+\tilde{y})-K^{\lambda}_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y+\tilde{y})L_{\lambda}^{1/2m}\big)

\displaystyle\qquad\cdot((-\Delta_{\R^{n}})^{m}+\left|\lambda\right|)^{\frac{2m-k}{2m}}\mathrm{e}^{-L_{\lambda}^{1/2m}y}g_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{\tilde{x}})\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\tilde{x}}\big)_{j=1,\ldots,m}

\displaystyle=:\big(\sum_{k=0}^{m_{j}}S^{\lambda,\mathrm{I}}_{j,k}g_{j,k}+S^{\lambda,\mathrm{II}}_{j,k}g_{j,k}\big)_{j=1,\ldots,m}=:\big(\sum_{k=0}^{m_{j}}S^{\lambda}_{j,k}g_{j,k}\big)_{j=1,\ldots,m}\big)_{j=1,\ldots,m}

where we write
\big(\mathrm{e}^{-L_{\lambda}^{1/2m}y}\big)_{y\geq 0}
for the strongly continuous semigroup generated by the pseudo-differential operator
-L_{\lambda}^{1/2m}
, cf. Remark
4.10
.
Moreover, we exploited the growth bounds for
\boldsymbol{K}^{\lambda}(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{\tilde{x}},y+\tilde{y})
as
\tilde{y}\rightarrow\infty
and the special form of the extension of
g_{j,k}
from
\R^{n}
to
\R_{+}^{n+1}
by
\mathcal{E}_{\lambda}
.
Analogously to the proof of
[
17
, Proposition 6.8]
, but for the additional parameter
k=0,1,\ldots,m_{j}
, we thus may define

\displaystyle v^{\mathrm{hs}}_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y)

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\R^{n}}T_{j,k}(\lambda,\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{\tilde{x}}^{\prime},y+\tilde{y})h^{\lambda}_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{\tilde{x}}^{\prime},\tilde{y})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\tilde{x}}^{\prime}\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{y}

\displaystyle:=-\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\R^{n}}\big[\frac{\partial}{\partial\tilde{y}}K^{\lambda}_{j,k}-K^{\lambda}_{j,k}L_{\lambda}^{1/2m}\big](\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{\tilde{x}}^{\prime},y+\tilde{y})h^{\lambda}_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{\tilde{x}}^{\prime},\tilde{y})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\tilde{x}}^{\prime}\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{y}.

As in the proof of
[
17
, Proposition 6.8]
and applying Proposition
4.7
, we then find that

\displaystyle\left\|\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\big(\frac{\partial}{\partial\tilde{y}}K^{\lambda}_{j,k}(\cdot,\tilde{y})+K^{\lambda}_{j,k}(\cdot,\tilde{y})L_{\lambda}^{1/2m}\big)\right\|_{\mathrm{L}_{1}(\R^{n};\B(E))}

\displaystyle\leq C\left|\lambda\right|^{\frac{n-2m+\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|}{2m}}\int_{\R^{n}}p_{2m,\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|}^{n+1}(c\left|\lambda\right|^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2m}}}\tilde{y})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},\lambda

\displaystyle\leq C\left|\lambda\right|^{\frac{n-2m+\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|}{2m}}\int_{0}^{\infty}p_{2m,\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|}^{n+1}(c\left|\lambda\right|^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2m}}}(r+\tilde{y}))r^{n-1}\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle=n!\,C\left|\lambda\right|^{\frac{\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|-2m}{2m}}p_{2m+n,\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|}^{n+1}(c\left|\lambda\right|^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\tilde{y}),\quad\tilde{y}>0.

Then, by
[
17
, Lemma 6.7]
,

\left\|\lambda^{1-\frac{\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|}{2m}}\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}T^{\lambda}_{j,k}g_{j,k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R^{n};E)}\leq C\left\|((-\Delta_{\R^{n}})^{m}+\left|\lambda\right|)^{\frac{2m-k}{2m}}g_{j,k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R^{n};E)},

so that the first estimate has been established.
By slight modification of the arguments, the second estimate can be proved as well.
For the third estimate, we may use that the integral operators
T^{\lambda}_{j,k}
depend holomorphically on the parameter
\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\phi}
, so that the third estimate is a direct consequence of the first one when applying the same technique which has been used for the proof of Proposition
2.7
.

The representation and regularity of the solution to (
14
) follow by approximation just as at the end of the proof of
[
17
, Proposition 6.8]
:
Given
g_{j,k}\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2m-k}(\R_{+}^{n+1};\ran(\mathcal{P}_{j,k}))
, let
g_{j,k}^{\nu}\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2m-k+1}(\R_{+}^{n+1};\ran(\mathcal{P}_{j,k}))
with
g_{j,k}^{\nu}\rightarrow g_{j,k}\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2m-k}(\R_{+}^{n+1};\ran(\mathcal{P}_{j,k}))
as
\nu\rightarrow\infty
.
By the estimates above,
v^{\nu}_{j,k}:=T^{\lambda}_{j,k}g_{j,k}^{\nu}\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2m}(\R_{+};\ran(\mathcal{P}_{j,k}))
with
(\lambda J+A)v^{\nu}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
\boldsymbol{g}^{\nu}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
, i.e.
v^{\nu}\in\dom(A\&B)
, and further
v^{\nu}_{j,k}\rightarrow v_{j,k}
in
\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2m-1}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E)
.
As
A\&B
is a closed operator,
v\in\dom(A\&B)\cap\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2m-1}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E)
with
(\lambda\boldsymbol{J}+A\&B)v=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
\boldsymbol{g}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.
∎

Similarly, the results for the boundary value problem

(16)

\lambda Jv+(A\&B)v=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}f\\
\boldsymbol{0}\end{array}\right)

transfer from
[
17
]
to the situation considered here.

Proposition 4.13
.

Let
p\in(1,\infty)
and differential operators
\mathcal{A}(\mathrm{D})
and
\mathcal{B}_{j}(\mathrm{D})
,
j=1,\ldots,m
, be given as above.
Let
\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\phi}
, and
f\in\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E)
.
Then there is a unique solution
w\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2m-1}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E)\cap\dom(A\&B)
of the boundary value problem (
16
), and it is given by
w=w^{\mathrm{fs}}+\sum_{j=1}^{m}\sum_{k=0}^{m_{j}}v^{\mathrm{corr}}_{j,k}
, for functions
v^{\mathrm{corr}}_{j,k}
as in Proposition
4.5
and
w^{\mathrm{fs}}=P(\lambda+A_{\R^{n+1}})^{-1}\mathcal{E}_{0}f
.
Moreover, there is a constant
C>0
(independent of the data
f
) such that

\left\|\lambda^{1-\frac{\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|}{2m}}\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}w\right\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E)}\leq C\left\|f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E)}\quad\text{ for all }\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\N_{0}^{n+1}\text{ such that }\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|\leq 2m-1.

Proof.

The proof can be transferred almost
expressis verbis
from the proof of
[
17
, Proposition 6.9]
.
∎

Summarising these results, we obtain the following theorem, which can be seen as an analogue to
[
17
, Theorem 6.10]
.

Theorem 4.14
.

(1)

Let
p\in(1,\infty)
and let
E
be an arbitrary Banach space.
Let
\mathcal{A}(\mathrm{D})
be a parameter-elliptic operator of order
2m
with angle of ellipticity
\phi_{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{ellipt}}\in[0,\pi)
.
Let
\phi>\phi_{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{ellipt}}
.
For each
j=1,\ldots,m
let
\mathcal{B}_{j}(\mathrm{D})=\sum_{k=0}^{m_{j}}\sum_{\left|\boldsymbol{\beta}\right|=k}b_{j,k,\boldsymbol{\beta}}\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\mathcal{P}_{j,k}
be a boundary differential operator of order
m_{j}<2m
.
Assume that the Lopatinskii–Shapiro condition
{\bf(LS)}
is valid.
Let data
f\in\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E)
and
g_{j}\in\sum_{k=0}^{m_{j}}\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2m-k-\frac{1}{p}}(\R^{n};\ran(\mathcal{P}_{j,k}))
,
j=1,\ldots,m
, be given.
Let
\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\phi}
and let
A\&B
be defined as above.
Then the boundary value problem

\lambda Ju+(A\&B)u=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}f\\
\mathcal{E}_{\lambda}\boldsymbol{g}\end{array}\right)

has a unique solution
u\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2m-1}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E)\cap\dom(A\&B)
which, for
R^{\lambda}_{0}:=P_{\R_{+}^{n+1}}(\lambda+A_{\R^{n+1}})^{-1}\mathcal{E}_{0}
, is given by

u=R^{\lambda}_{0}f+\sum_{j=1}^{m}\sum_{k=0}^{m_{j}}\left(R^{\lambda}_{j,k}f+S^{\lambda}_{j,k}\mathcal{E}_{\lambda}g_{j,k}\right)

and there is a constant
C>0
which is independent of the data
(f,\boldsymbol{g})
and the parameter
\lambda
such that for every multi-index
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\N_{0}^{n+1}
of length
\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|\leq 2m-1
and every
\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\phi}
we have

\displaystyle\left\|\lambda^{1-\frac{\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|}{2m}}\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E)}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\left\|f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E)}+\sum_{j=1}^{m}\sum_{k=0}^{m_{j}}\left\|((-\Delta)^{m}+\left|\lambda\right|)^{\frac{2m-k}{2m}}\mathcal{E}_{\lambda}g_{j,k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E)}\right).

(2)

If we start with given functions
g_{j,k}\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2m-k}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E)
and replace
\mathcal{E}_{\lambda}\boldsymbol{g}
in the statement above by
\boldsymbol{g}
, we obtain the same result, except for the modified estimates

(17)

\displaystyle\left\|\lambda^{1-\frac{\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|}{2m}}\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E)}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\left\|f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E)}+\sum_{j=1}^{m}\sum_{k=0}^{m_{j}}\left\|((-\Delta)^{m}+\left|\lambda\right|)^{\frac{2m-k}{2m}}g_{j,k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E)}\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\left.+\sum_{j=1}^{m}\sum_{k=0}^{m_{j}}\left\|((-\Delta)^{m}+\left|\lambda\right|)^{\frac{2m-k-1}{2m}}\frac{\partial}{\partial y}g_{j,k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E)}\right).

4.5.
\mathcal{H}^{\infty}
-calculus

In this subsection, we transfer the results on the
\mathcal{H}^{\infty}
-calculus as in
[
17
, Section 7.1]
to the situation considered here.
We start by combining Theorem
4.14
and
[
17
, Lemma 7.1]
, and immediately obtain the following theorem.

Theorem 4.15
.

If, in the situation of Theorem
4.14
, the Banach space
E
is of class
\mathcal{HT}
, then
u
lies in
\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2m}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E)
and (
17
) holds true for all
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\N_{0}^{n+1}
with
\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|=2m
as well.
Moreover, in this case

\displaystyle\left\|\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}S^{\lambda}_{j,k}\mathcal{E}_{\lambda}g_{j}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E)}

\displaystyle\leq C\left|\lambda\right|^{\frac{\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|-k}{2m}}\left\|\mathcal{E}_{\lambda}g_{j,k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E)},

\displaystyle 0\leq\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|\leq k\leq m_{j},

if
g_{j,k}
is only given on
\R^{n}\cong\partial\R_{+}^{n+1}
, else

\displaystyle\left\|\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}S^{\lambda,\mathrm{I}}_{j,k}\mathcal{E}_{\lambda}g_{j,k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E)}

\displaystyle\leq C\left|\lambda\right|^{\frac{\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|-k}{2m}}\left\|g_{j,k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E)},

\displaystyle 0\leq\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|\leq k\leq m_{j},

\displaystyle\left\|\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}S^{\lambda,\mathrm{II}}_{j,k}g_{j,k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E)}

\displaystyle\leq C\left|\lambda\right|^{\frac{\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|-k-1}{2m}}\left\|\tfrac{\partial}{\partial y}g_{j,k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E)},

\displaystyle 0\leq\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|\leq k+1\leq m_{j}+1

and the
\mathrm{L}_{p}
-realisation
A_{B}
of the boundary value problem (
16
) is

(18)
