\displaystyle A_{B}u

\displaystyle=\mathcal{A}(\mathrm{D})u,

(19)

\displaystyle\dom(A_{B})

\displaystyle=\{u\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2m}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E):\,\mathcal{B}_{j}(\mathrm{D})u=0\quad\text{for all }j=1,\ldots,m\}.

Together with the kernel estimates of Proposition
4.7
, it follows that
A_{B}
admits a
bounded
\mathcal{H}^{\infty}
-calculus
, see the following analogue to
[
17
, Theorem 7.4]
:

Theorem 4.16
.

Let
E
be a Banach space of class
\mathcal{HT}
and let
A_{B}:\dom(A_{B})\rightarrow\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E)
be defined as in Theorem
4.15
. Then
A_{B}\in\mathcal{H}^{\infty}(\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E))
with
\mathcal{H}^{\infty}
-angle
\phi_{A_{B}}^{\infty}\leq\phi_{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{ellipt}}
.

The proof can be executed similarly to the proof of
[
17
, Theorem 7.4]
, using
[
17
, Lemma 7.1]
and an adjusted version of
[
17
, Remark 7.2c]
.

Proof.

Let us fix any
\phi\in(\phi_{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{ellipt}},\pi)
and recall that we may write the resolvents of the operator
A_{B}
as

(\lambda+A_{B})^{-1}=R^{\lambda}_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{m}\sum_{k=0}^{m_{j}}R^{\lambda}_{j,k}\quad\text{for all }\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\phi}.

The terms correspond to the resolvent for the full-space problem plus corrective terms due to the homogeneous boundary conditions imposed at
\partial\R_{+}^{n+1}
.

Let
h\in\mathcal{H}^{\infty}(\Sigma_{\phi})
be an arbitrary polynomially decaying function, i.e. there is
C,s>0
such that

\left|h(z)\right|\leq C(1+\left|z\right|)^{-s},

and denote by
\Gamma
the contour given by
(0,\infty]\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\theta}\cup(0,\infty)\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\theta}
for some
\theta\in(\phi_{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{ellipt}},\phi)
.
We may then use the Dunford integral calculus to define

h(A_{B}):=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\Gamma}h(\lambda)(\lambda+A_{B})^{-1}\mathrm{d}\lambda.

We want to show that
A_{B}\in\mathcal{H}^{\infty}(\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E))
with
\mathcal{H}^{\infty}
-angle
\phi_{A_{B}}^{\infty}\leq\phi_{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{ellipt}}
, thus it remains to prove that for all such
\phi>\phi_{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{ellipt}}
and
h\in\mathcal{H}_{0}(\Sigma_{\phi})
which polynomially decay to zero at infinity, we have an estimate

\left\|h(A_{B})\right\|_{\B(\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E))}\leq C\left\|h\right\|_{\infty}

for some constant
C>0
which is independent of
h\in\mathcal{H}_{0}(\Sigma_{\phi})
.
For the full-space problem related term
R^{\lambda}_{0}
such an estimate has already been established in
[
17
, Theorem 5.5]
, relying on the condition that
E
is a Banach space of class
\mathcal{HT}
.
The remaining terms
R^{\lambda}_{j,k}
are the integral operators associated with the integral kernels
K^{\mathrm{corr},\lambda}_{j,k}
, hence,
\int_{\Gamma}h(\lambda)R^{\lambda}_{j,k}\,\mathrm{d}\lambda
is the integral operator associated to the kernel
\int_{\Gamma}h(\lambda)K^{\mathrm{corr},\lambda}_{j,k}(\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)\,\mathrm{d}\lambda
.
Since the estimate

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\Gamma}h(\lambda)K^{\mathrm{corr},\lambda}_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y,\tilde{y})\,\mathrm{d}\lambda\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|h\right\|_{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}r^{\frac{n+1}{2m}}p_{2m,0}^{n+1}(cr^{\frac{1}{2m}}(\left|\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\right|+y+\tilde{y})\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|h\right\|_{\infty}\frac{1}{(\left|\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\right|+y+\tilde{y})^{n+1}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\tau^{n}p_{2m,0}^{n+1}(c\tau)\,\mathrm{d}\tau

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|h\right\|_{\infty}\frac{1}{(\left|\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\right|+y+\tilde{y})^{n+1}}

is valid thanks to
\int_{0}^{\infty}\tau^{n}p_{2m,0}^{n+1}(\tau)\mathrm{d}\tau<\infty
, we may employ Proposition
2.18
and
[
17
, Remark 7.2 c)]
to conclude the assertion.
∎

Corollary 4.17
.

Let
E
be a Banach space of class
\mathcal{HT}
.
Then

\mathcal{R}(\{\lambda^{1-\frac{\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|}{2m}}\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(\lambda+A_{B})^{-1}:\,\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\phi},\,\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\N_{0}^{n+1}\text{ such that }0\leq\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|\leq 2m\})<\infty.

Proof.

By Theorem
4.16
and Remark
2.17
,
A_{B}\in\mathcal{H}^{\infty}(E)\subseteq\mathcal{BIP}(E)
, hence, for every
\sigma\in(0,1)

\dom(A_{B}^{\sigma})=\big(\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E),\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2m}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E)\big)_{\sigma,p}=\mathrm{H}_{p}^{2m\sigma}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E).

Thus, for each multi-index
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\N_{0}^{n+1}
of length
\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|\leq 2m
, the linear operator
\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}A_{B}^{-\frac{k}{2m}}
with
k=\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|
is a bounded operator on
E
.
As
\{\lambda(\lambda+A_{B})^{-1}:\,\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\phi}\}
is
\mathcal{R}
-bounded, all the sets

\{\lambda^{-\frac{\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|}{2m}}\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\lambda(\lambda+A_{B})^{-1}:\,\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\phi}\}\quad\text{for each }\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|\leq 2m

are
\mathcal{R}
-bounded.
Since, here, the set of admissible multi-indices
\{\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\N_{0}^{n+1}:\,\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|\leq 2m\}
is finite, the family of operators

\{\lambda^{-\frac{\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|}{2m}}\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\lambda(\lambda+A_{B})^{-1}:\,\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\phi},\,\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|\leq 2m\}

is
\mathcal{R}
-bounded as well.
∎

4.6.
\mathcal{R}
-bounds for solution operators

By Corollary
4.17
, the solution map for the boundary value problem (
16
) with homogeneous boundary data
\boldsymbol{g}=\boldsymbol{0}
admits
\mathcal{R}
-bounds. Next, we consider the boundary value problem (
16
) for
f=0
and inhomogeneous boundary data
\boldsymbol{g}\neq 0
and also conclude
\mathcal{R}
-boundedness for the corresponding solution maps. By Proposition
4.11
the solution
v
to
(\lambda\boldsymbol{J}+A\&B)v=(0,\mathcal{E}_{\lambda}\boldsymbol{g})^{\mathsf{T}}
may be expressed as

v=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\sum_{k=0}^{m_{j}}S^{\lambda}_{j,k}\mathcal{E}_{\lambda}g_{j,k}.

Our goal is to prove that the boundary value problem

\begin{cases}(\lambda+\mathcal{A}(\mathrm{D}))u=f&\text{in }\R_{+}^{n+1},\\
\mathcal{B}_{j}(\mathrm{D})u=g_{j,k}&\text{on }\partial\R_{+}^{n+1},\,j=1,\ldots,m\end{cases}

has a (unique) solution in the class
u\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2m}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E)
if and only if the given functions lie in the classes
f\in\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E)
and
g_{j,k}\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2m-k-1/p}(\partial\R_{+}^{n+1};E)
for
j=1,\ldots,m
and
k=0,1,\ldots,m_{j}
.
Using the extension operator
\mathcal{E}_{\lambda}
, the latter is equivalent to
\mathcal{E}_{\lambda}g_{j,k}\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2m-k}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E)
for all
j=1,\ldots,m
and
k=0,1,\ldots,m_{j}
, and since for
\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\phi}
, i.e.
\left|\lambda\right|>0
,
((-\Delta)^{m}+\left|\lambda\right|)^{\frac{2m-k}{2m}}
acts as a top-linear isomorphy between
\mathrm{W}_{p}^{k}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E)
and
\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E)
, we may reformulate the problem as follows:
Show that that

\begin{cases}(\lambda+\mathcal{A}(\mathrm{D}))u=f&\text{in }\R_{+}^{n+1},\\
((-\Delta_{\R^{n}})^{m}+\left|\lambda\right|)^{\frac{2m-k}{2m}}\mathcal{B}_{j,k}(\mathrm{D})u|_{y=0}=\tilde{g}_{j,k}&\text{on }\partial\R_{+}^{n+1},\,1\leq j\leq m,\,0\leq k\leq m_{j}\end{cases}

where
\tilde{g}_{j,k}:=((-\Delta_{\R^{n}})^{m}+\left|\lambda\right|)^{\frac{2m-k}{2m}}\mathcal{E}_{\lambda}g_{j,k}|_{y=0}
, has a (unique) solution
u\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2m}(\R_{+};E)
if and only if
f\in\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E)
and
((-\Delta_{\R^{n}})^{m}+\left|\lambda\right|)^{\frac{2m-k}{2m}}\mathcal{E}_{\lambda}g_{j,k}\in\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R_{+};E)
for all
j=1,\ldots,m
and
k=0,1,\ldots,m_{j}
.
Therefore, for
\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\phi}
,
j=1,\ldots,m
and
k=0,\ldots,m_{j}
, and using the operators
S_{j,k}^{\lambda,\mathrm{I}}
and
S_{j,k}^{\lambda,\mathrm{I}}
from Proposition
4.13
we introduce operators

(20)

\displaystyle U^{\lambda}_{j,k}

\displaystyle=S^{\lambda,\mathrm{I}}_{j,k}((-\Delta)^{m}+\left|\lambda\right|)^{\frac{k-2m}{2m}}\mathcal{E}_{\lambda},

(21)

\displaystyle V^{\lambda}_{j,k}

\displaystyle=S^{\lambda,\mathrm{II}}_{j,k}((-\Delta)^{m}+\left|\lambda\right|)^{\frac{k-2m}{2m}}\mathcal{E}_{\lambda}.

The families consisting of these operators allow for the following
\mathcal{R}
-bounds, cf.
[
17
, Proposition 7.6]
.

Proposition 4.18
.

For each
j=1,\ldots,m
and
k=0,1,\ldots,m_{j}
, the sets

\displaystyle\{\lambda^{1-\frac{\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|}{2m}}\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}U^{\lambda}_{j,k}:\,\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\phi},\,\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|\leq 2m\}

\displaystyle\subseteq\B(\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E)),

\displaystyle\{\lambda^{1-\frac{\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|}{2m}}\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}V^{\lambda}_{j,k}:\,\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\phi},\,\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|\leq 2m\}

\displaystyle\subseteq\B(\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E)),

\displaystyle\{\lambda^{2-\frac{\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|}{2m}}\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\partial_{\lambda}U^{\lambda}_{j,k}:\,\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\phi},\,\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|\leq 2m\}

\displaystyle\subseteq\B(\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E)),

\displaystyle\{\lambda^{2-\frac{\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|}{2m}}\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\partial_{\lambda}V^{\lambda}_{j,k}:\,\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\phi},\,\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|\leq 2m\}

\displaystyle\subseteq\B(\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E))

are
\mathcal{R}
-bounded.

Proof.

By Proposition
4.11
, the operators
\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}U^{\lambda}_{j,k}
and
\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}V^{\lambda}_{j,k}
are integral operators on
\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E)
with kernels
\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}K^{\lambda}_{U^{j,k}}
and
\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}K^{\lambda}_{V_{j,k}}
, resp., where

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}K^{\lambda}_{U^{j,k}}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},y)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}\big(\frac{\partial}{\partial y}K^{\lambda,\mathrm{I}}_{j,k}\big)(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},y)(\left|\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime}\right|^{2m}+\left|\lambda\right|)^{\frac{2m-k}{2m}}\mathcal{P}_{j,k}(\left|\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime}\right|^{2m}+\left|\lambda\right|)^{\frac{k-2m}{2m}}\mathcal{E}_{\lambda},

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}K^{\lambda}_{V^{j,k}}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},y)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}K^{\lambda,\mathrm{II}}_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},y)\frac{\partial}{\partial y}(\left|\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime}\right|^{2m}+\left|\lambda\right|)^{\frac{2m-k-1}{2m}}\mathcal{P}_{j,k}(\left|\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime}\right|^{2m}+\left|\lambda\right|)^{\frac{k+1-2m}{2m}}\mathcal{E}_{\lambda},

for
j=1,\ldots,m
and
k=0,1,\ldots,m_{j}
.
By
[
17
, Proposition 4.1 and Lemma 7.1]
, it suffices to prove that there is
C>0
such that for all
\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\in\R^{n}
and
y,\tilde{y}>0

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(\{\lambda^{1-\frac{\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|}{2m}}\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\tilde{y}}K^{\lambda}_{U^{j,k}}(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y+\tilde{y}):\,\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\phi},\,\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|\leq 2m\})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{(\left|\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\right|+y+\tilde{y})^{n+1}},

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(\{\lambda^{1-\frac{\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|}{2m}}\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\tilde{y}}K^{\lambda}_{V^{j,k}}(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y+\tilde{y}):\,\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\phi},\,\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|\leq 2m\})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{(\left|\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\right|+y+\tilde{y})^{n+1}}.

This can be proved as follows, analogously to
[
17
, Proposition 7.7]
.
For each
\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\in\R^{n}
,
y>0
,
\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\phi}
and
\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\N_{0}^{n}
,
\gamma\in\N_{0}
such that
\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|\leq 2m
and
\left|\boldsymbol{\beta}\right|+\gamma=\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|
, we have the identity

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\frac{\partial}{\partial y}K^{\lambda}_{U^{j,k}}(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{n}}\int_{\R^{n}}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\cdot\boldsymbol{\xi}}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\rho\boldsymbol{A}_{0}(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)y}}{\rho^{2m}}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime})^{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(A_{0}(b,\sigma)\rho)^{\gamma}\mathrm{i}\boldsymbol{A}_{0}(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)M(b,\sigma)\rho^{2m-k}J_{\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},\lambda}^{2m-k}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},

where
J_{\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},\lambda}^{2m-k}=(\left|\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime}\right|^{2m}+\left|\lambda\right|)^{\frac{k-2m}{2m}}
.
As in the proof of Proposition
4.7
, we choose a rotation
\boldsymbol{Q}\in\R^{n\times n}
such that
\boldsymbol{Q}\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}=(\left|\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\right|,0,\ldots,0)^{\mathsf{T}}
and write
\boldsymbol{Q}\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime}=(a,r\boldsymbol{\varphi})
for some
a\in\R
,
r>0
and
\boldsymbol{\varphi}\in\mathbb{S}^{n-2}
. Then

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\frac{\partial}{\partial y}K^{\lambda}_{U^{j,k}}(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n-2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}r^{n-2}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\left|\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\right|a}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\rho\boldsymbol{A}_{0}(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)y}}{\rho^{2m-1}}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime})^{\boldsymbol{\beta}}

\displaystyle\quad\cdot(\boldsymbol{A}_{0}(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)\rho)^{\gamma}\mathrm{i}\boldsymbol{A}_{0}(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)\boldsymbol{M}(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)\rho^{2m-k}J_{\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},\lambda}^{2m-k}\,\mathrm{d}a\,\mathrm{d}r\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{S}^{n-2}(\boldsymbol{\varphi})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n-2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}r^{n-2}\int_{\Gamma^{-}}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\left|\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\right|a}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\rho\boldsymbol{A}_{0}(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)y}}{\rho^{2m-1}}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime})^{\boldsymbol{\beta}}

\displaystyle\quad\cdot(\boldsymbol{A}_{0}(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)\rho)^{\gamma}\mathrm{i}\boldsymbol{A}_{0}(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)\boldsymbol{M}(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)\rho^{2m-k}J_{\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},\lambda}^{2m-k}\,\mathrm{d}a\,\mathrm{d}r\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{S}^{n-2}(\boldsymbol{\varphi}),

where as in the proof of Proposition
4.7

\gamma^{-}(s)=s+\mathrm{i}\varepsilon(r+\left|s\right|+\left|\lambda\right|^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}m})
,
s\in\R
, is a parametrisation of the curve
\Gamma^{-}
, and Cauchy’s integral theorem as well as holomorphy of
\boldsymbol{A}_{0}
and
\boldsymbol{M}
have been employed.
Setting
c=\nicefrac{{c_{1}}}{{2}}
for
c_{1}>0
from (
9
), as in Proposition
4.7
we obtain the identity

\displaystyle\left|\lambda\right|^{1-\frac{\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|}{2m}}\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\frac{\partial}{\partial y}K^{\lambda}_{U^{j,k}}(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y)(\left|\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\right|+y)^{n+1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n-2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}F(r,\tau,\boldsymbol{\varphi},\lambda,\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y)r^{n-2}(\left|\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\right|+y)^{n}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}(\varepsilon\left|\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\right|+cy)(r+\tau)}\,\mathrm{d}r\,\mathrm{d}\tau\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{S}^{n-2}(\boldsymbol{\varphi}),

where

F(r,\tau,\boldsymbol{\varphi},\lambda,\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y)=G(r,\tau,\boldsymbol{\varphi},\lambda,y)H(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y,r,\tau,\lambda)

with

\displaystyle G(r,\tau,\boldsymbol{\varphi},\lambda,y)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\rho\boldsymbol{A}_{0}(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)y}\mathrm{e}^{cy(r+\tau+\left|\lambda\right|^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}m})}\boldsymbol{A}_{0}(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)^{\gamma}\boldsymbol{A}_{0}(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma)\boldsymbol{M}(\boldsymbol{b},\sigma),

\displaystyle H(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y,r,\tau,\lambda)

\displaystyle=(\left|\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\right|+y)\mathrm{e}^{-(\varepsilon\left|\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\right|+cy)\left|\lambda\right|^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}m}}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}(\varepsilon\left|\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\right|+cy)(r+\tau)}{\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime}}^{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\rho^{\gamma}\frac{\lambda^{1-\frac{\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|}{2m}}}{\rho^{2m-1}}J_{\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},\lambda}^{2m-k}

and where
\rho,\boldsymbol{b},\sigma
depend on
r,\tau,\lambda
and
\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|=\left|\boldsymbol{\beta}\right|+\gamma
.
Setting
M=\mathbb{S}^{n-2}\times\R_{+}\times\R_{+}
, this integral equals
\int_{M}F(r,\tau,\boldsymbol{\varphi},\lambda,\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(r,\tau,\boldsymbol{\varphi})
, where

\ \mathrm{d}\mu(r,\tau,\boldsymbol{\varphi})=r^{n-2}(\left|\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\right|+y)^{n}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}(\varepsilon\left|\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\right|+cy)(r+\tau)}\,\mathrm{d}r\,\mathrm{d}\tau\,\mathrm{d}\sigma(\boldsymbol{\varphi}).

Here,
\mu
is a finite measure on
M
and such that
\int_{M}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(r,\tau,\boldsymbol{\varphi})\leq C
for some
C>0
independent of
\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\in\R^{n}
and
y>0
.
By
[
17
, Proposition 3.8]
it follows that for
\left|\boldsymbol{\beta}\right|+\gamma=\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|

\displaystyle(\left|\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\right|+y)^{n+1}\mathcal{R}(\{\lambda^{1-\frac{\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|}{2m}}\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\frac{\partial}{\partial y}K^{\lambda}_{U^{j,k}}(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y):\,\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\phi},\,\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|\leq 2m\})

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{R}(\{(\left|\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\right|+y)^{n+1}\lambda^{1-\frac{\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|}{2m}}\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\frac{\partial}{\partial y}K^{\lambda}_{U^{j,k}}(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y):\,\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\phi},\,\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|\leq 2m,\,\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\in\R^{n},\,y>0\})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{R}\big(\big\{\int_{M}F(r,\tau,\boldsymbol{\varphi},\lambda,\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(r,\tau,\boldsymbol{\varphi}):\,\lambda\geq 0,\,\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\in\R^{n},\,\boldsymbol{\varphi}\in\mathbb{S}^{n-2}\big\}\big).

The assertion for
V^{\lambda}_{j,k}
follows similarly and those for the
\lambda
-derivatives follow
\mathcal{R}
-boundedness for the operators
U^{\lambda}_{j,k}
,
V^{\lambda}_{j,k}
and the abstract result Proposition
2.7
.
∎

4.7.
Spatially dependent interior and boundary symbols

As situations with spatial dependent parameters are of utmost importance, in particular for the localisation procedure by which results on the half-space are transferred to general, but sufficiently regular domains, we consider spatially dependent perturbations of the principle part next, i.e. the interior symbol
\mathcal{A}
is allowed to be of the form

\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{x},D)=\sum_{\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|=2m}a_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(\boldsymbol{x})\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}

with spatially dependent, but bounded and uniformly continuous coefficients
a_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}:\R_{+}^{n+1}\rightarrow\B(E)
, and similarly for the boundary symbols

\mathcal{B}_{j}(\boldsymbol{x},\mathrm{D})=\sum_{k=0}^{m_{j}}\sum_{\left|\boldsymbol{\beta}\right|=k}b_{j,\boldsymbol{\beta}}(\boldsymbol{x})\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\mathcal{P}_{j,k},\quad j=1,\ldots,m

where we demand regularity of the coefficients
b_{j,\boldsymbol{\beta}}\in\mathrm{BUC}^{2m-k}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E)
for each multi-index
\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\N_{0}^{n+1}
of length
\left|\boldsymbol{\beta}\right|=k
.
Throughout this subsection, we assume that some
\phi>\phi_{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{ellipt}}
is given, the Banach space
E
is of class
\mathcal{HT}
and the Lopatinskii–Shapiro condition is satisfied at every boundary point
\boldsymbol{x}\in\partial\R_{+}^{n+1}
.
For now, let us fix
\boldsymbol{x}_{0}\in\R_{+}^{n+1}
and, as an intermediate step, assume that, for some given small
\varepsilon>0
, the coefficients
a_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
and
b_{j,\boldsymbol{\beta}}
have uniformly small oscillation, in the sense that

\displaystyle\sup_{\boldsymbol{x}\in\R_{+}^{n+1}}\sum_{\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|=2m}\left\|a_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(\boldsymbol{x})-a_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(\boldsymbol{x}_{0})\right\|_{\B(E)}

\displaystyle<\varepsilon,

\displaystyle\sup_{\boldsymbol{x}\in\R_{+}^{n+1}}\sum_{\left|\boldsymbol{\beta}\right|=k}\left\|b_{j,k,\boldsymbol{\beta}}(\boldsymbol{x})-b_{j,k,\boldsymbol{\beta}}(\boldsymbol{x}_{0})\right\|_{\B(E)}

\displaystyle<\varepsilon\quad\text{for }j=1,\ldots,m,\,k=0,\ldots,m_{j}.

We may then interpret
\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{x},D)
and
\mathcal{B}_{j}(\boldsymbol{x},D)
as small perturbations from the constant coefficient case:

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{x},\mathrm{D})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\mathrm{D})+\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{sm}}(\boldsymbol{x},\mathrm{D})

\displaystyle\text{for }\boldsymbol{x}\in\R_{+}^{n+1}

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{j}(\boldsymbol{x},\mathrm{D})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{B}_{j}(\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\mathrm{D})+\mathcal{B}_{j}^{\mathrm{sm}}(\boldsymbol{x},\mathrm{D})

\displaystyle\text{for }\boldsymbol{x}\in\partial\R_{+}^{n+1}.

For each
\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\phi}
with
\phi>\phi_{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{ellipt}}
and a given right-hand side
f\in\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E)
, the variable coefficient problem

(22)

\begin{cases}(\lambda+\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{x},\mathrm{D}))u=f&\text{in }\R_{+}^{n+1},\\
\mathcal{B}_{j}(\boldsymbol{x},\mathrm{D})u=0&\text{on }\partial\R_{+}^{n+1},\,j=1,\ldots,m\end{cases}

has a unique solution
u\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2m}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E)
if and only if it is the unique solution
u\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2m}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E)
to the problem

(23)

\begin{cases}(\lambda+\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\mathrm{D}))u=f-\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{sm}}(\boldsymbol{x},D)u&\text{in }\R_{+}^{n+1},\\
\mathcal{B}_{j}(\boldsymbol{x}_{0})u(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},0)=-\mathcal{B}_{j}^{\mathrm{sm}}(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},\mathrm{D})u(\boldsymbol{x},0)&\text{on }\partial\R_{+}^{n+1},\text{ for }j=1,\ldots,m.\end{cases}

A procedure as in
[
17
, Section 7.3]
, based on the contraction mapping principle, then delivers that, for sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
and
\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\phi}
with sufficiently large modulus, the system of equations (
22
) (or, equivalently, equation (
23
)) admits a unique solution
u\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2m}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E)
of the form

u=[I+(\lambda+A_{B^{0}}^{0})^{-1}\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{sm}}+\sum_{j=1}^{m}\sum_{k=0}^{m_{j}}S^{\lambda}_{j,k}\mathcal{B}_{j}^{\mathrm{sm}}]^{-1}(\lambda+A_{B^{0}}^{0})^{-1}f.

Here, we set
A_{B^{0}}^{0}:=\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\mathrm{D})|_{\operatorname{ker}(\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\mathrm{D}))}
and then define

\displaystyle A_{B}u

\displaystyle=\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{x},\mathrm{D})u,

\displaystyle\dom(A_{B})

\displaystyle=\{u\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2m}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E):\,\mathcal{B}_{j}(\boldsymbol{x}_{0},D)u(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},0)=0,\,\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\in\R^{n},\,j=1,\ldots,m\}.

Thus, for
\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\phi}
with sufficiently large modulus the operator
\lambda+A_{B}
is invertible and its inverse is implicitly given by the relation

(\lambda+A_{B})^{-1}=(\lambda+A_{B^{0}}^{0})^{-1}-(\lambda+A_{B^{0}}^{0})^{-1}\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{sm}}(\cdot,\mathrm{D})(\lambda+A_{B})^{-1}-\sum_{j=1}^{m}\sum_{k=0}^{m_{j}}S^{\lambda}_{j,k}\mathcal{P}_{j,k}\mathcal{B}_{j}^{\mathrm{sm}}(\lambda+A_{B})^{-1},

hence, solving for
(\lambda+A_{B})^{-1}
we find that

(\lambda+A_{B})^{-1}=[I+(\lambda+A_{B^{0}}^{0})^{-1}\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{sm}}(\cdot,\mathrm{D})+\sum_{j=1}^{m}\sum_{k=0}^{m_{j}}S^{\lambda}_{j,k}\mathcal{P}_{j,k}\mathcal{B}^{\mathrm{sm}}_{j,k}(\cdot,\mathrm{D})]^{-1}(\lambda+A_{B^{0}}^{0})^{-1}.

With this formula at hand, we may establish
\mathcal{R}
-bounds on the set of operators
\lambda^{1-\frac{\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|}{2m}}\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(\lambda+A_{B})^{-1}
as follows.

Lemma 4.19
.

There is
\lambda_{0}>0
such that the set

\{\lambda^{1-\frac{\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|}{2m}}\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(\lambda+A_{B})^{-1}:\,\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\phi},\,\left|\lambda\right|\geq\lambda_{0},\,0\leq\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|\leq 2m\}\subseteq\B(\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E))

is
\mathcal{R}
-bounded.

Proof.

The proof is essentially the same as in
[
17
, Section 7.3]
. Instead of the operators
\mathcal{B}_{j}(\mathrm{D})
, we consider each of the operators
\mathcal{B}_{j,k}(\mathrm{D})=\mathcal{B}_{j}(\mathrm{D})\mathcal{P}_{j,k}
(
k=0,1,\ldots,m_{j}
) separately and use that
\mathcal{B}_{j}(\mathrm{D})=\sum_{k=0}^{m_{j}}\mathcal{B}_{j,k}(\mathrm{D})
is the sum of these operators.
We leave out the details here.
∎

4.8.
Variable and lower order coefficients in a half-space

To generalise the results of the previous subsection to more general spatially dependent coefficients, i.e. to get rid of the small oscillation constraint, and elliptic operators with lower order coefficients, we use the same localisation procedure as in
[
17
, Section 5.3]
. In comparison with
[
17
, Section 7.4]
we use a more subtle notion of the principal part, which allows us to handle the mixed type boundary conditions considered here. Throughout this subsection, we assume that there are projections
\mathcal{P}_{j,k}
(independent of the spatial variable
\boldsymbol{x}
), for
j=1,\ldots,m
and
k=0,1,\ldots,m_{j}<2m
, such that
\mathcal{P}_{j,k}\mathcal{P}_{j,k^{\prime}}=0
for each
k\neq k^{\prime}
and we allow that the interior symbol
\mathcal{A}
and the boundary symbols
\mathcal{B}_{j}
have the spatial, but not temporal dependent form

\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{x},\mathrm{D})=\sum_{\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|\leq 2m}a_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(\boldsymbol{x})\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}},\quad\mathcal{B}_{j}(\boldsymbol{x},\mathrm{D})=\sum_{k=0}^{m_{j}}\sum_{\left|\boldsymbol{\beta}\right|\leq k}b_{j,k,\boldsymbol{\beta}}(\boldsymbol{x})\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\mathcal{P}_{j,k},\quad j=1,\ldots,m.

The principle parts
\mathcal{A}_{\#}
of the interior symbol and
\mathcal{B}_{j,\#}
of the boundary symbols are defined as

\mathcal{A}_{\#}(\boldsymbol{x},\mathrm{D})=\sum_{\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|=2m}a_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(\boldsymbol{x})\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}},\quad\mathcal{B}_{j,\#}(\boldsymbol{x},\mathrm{D})=\sum_{k=0}^{m_{j}}\sum_{\left|\boldsymbol{\beta}\right|=k}b_{j,k,\boldsymbol{\beta}}(\boldsymbol{x})\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\mathcal{P}_{j,k},\quad j=1,\ldots,m.

Moreover, we demand the following regularity of the coefficients
a_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
and
b_{j,k,\boldsymbol{\beta}}
.

Assumption 4.20
.

Assume that the following conditions are met by the interior and boundary symbols and their coefficients.

(1)

Smoothness assumptions:

(a)

a_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{l}}(\overline{\R_{+}^{n+1}};\B(E))
for all
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\N_{0}^{n+1}
of length
\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|=2m
;

(b)

a_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in[\mathrm{L}_{\infty}+\mathrm{L}_{r_{k}}](\R_{+}^{n+1};\B(E))
for all
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\N_{0}^{n+1}
of length
\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|=k<2m
where
r_{k}\geq p
such that
2m-k>\tfrac{n}{r_{k}}
;

(c)

b_{j,k,\boldsymbol{\beta}}\in\mathrm{C}^{2m-k}(\partial\R_{+}^{n+1};\B(E))
and
b_{j,k,\boldsymbol{\beta}}(\ran(\mathcal{P}_{j,k}))\subseteq\ran(\mathcal{P}_{j,k})+\ldots+\ran(\mathcal{P}_{j,m_{j}})
for each
j=1,\ldots,m
,
0\leq k\leq m_{j}
and
\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\N_{0}^{n+1}
of length
\left|\boldsymbol{\beta}\right|=k
.

(2)

There is
\phi_{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{ellipt}}\in[0,\pi)
such that

(a)

Ellipticity of the interior symbol:
The principal symbol
\mathcal{A}_{\#}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\xi})
is parameter-elliptic with angle of ellipticity
\phi\leq\phi_{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{ellipt}}
for each
\boldsymbol{x}\in\overline{\R_{+}^{n+1}}
.

(b)

Lopatinskii–Shapiro condition: For each
\boldsymbol{x}_{0}\in\overline{\R_{+}^{n+1}}
,
\lambda\in\overline{\Sigma_{\pi-\phi_{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{ellipt}}}}
and
\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime}\in\R^{n}
with
(\lambda,\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime})\neq(0,\boldsymbol{0})
, the initial value problem for the principle symbols

\displaystyle(\lambda+\mathcal{A}_{\#}(\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},\mathrm{D}_{n+1}))v(y)

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle y>0,

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{j,\#}(\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},\mathrm{D}_{n+1})v(0)

\displaystyle=h_{j},

\displaystyle j=1,\ldots,m

has a unique solution
u\in\mathrm{C}_{0}(\R_{+};E)
for each
(h_{1},\ldots,h_{m})^{\mathsf{T}}\in E^{m}
.

A procedure similar to the approach in the preceeding subsection then gives the following result.

Theorem 4.21
.

Let
E
be a Banach space of class
\mathcal{HT}
,
n,m\in\N
,
p\in(1,\infty)
and assume that for some
\phi_{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{ellipt}}\in[0,\pi)
the boundary value problem
(\mathcal{A},(\mathcal{B}_{j})_{j=1}^{m})
satisfies Assumption
4.20
.
Let
A_{B}
be the
\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E)
-realisation of the homogeneous boundary value problem with domain

\dom(A_{B})=\{u\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2m}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E):\,\mathcal{B}_{j}(\boldsymbol{x},\mathrm{D})u=0\,\text{on }\partial\R_{+}^{n+1},\,j=1,\ldots,m\}.

Then, for each
\phi>\phi_{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{ellipt}}
, there is
\mu_{\phi}\geq 0
such that
\mu_{\phi}+A_{B}
is
\mathcal{R}
-sectorial with
\phi_{\mu_{\phi}+A_{B}}^{\mathcal{R}}\leq\phi
.
In particular, if
\phi_{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{ellipt}}<\tfrac{\pi}{2}
, then the parabolic initial-boundary value problem

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u+A_{B}u+\mu_{\phi}u

\displaystyle=f,

\displaystyle t>0,

\displaystyle u(0)

\displaystyle=0

has the property of maximal regularity in the class
\mathrm{L}_{q,p}(\R_{+}\times\R_{+}^{n+1};E))
for each
q\in(1,\infty)
, i.e. for every given
f\in\mathrm{L}_{q,p}(\R_{+}\times\R_{+}^{n+1};E)
there is a unique solution
u\in\mathrm{W}_{q,p}^{(1,2)}(\R_{+}\times\R_{+}^{n+1};E)
and

\left\|u\right\|_{\mathrm{W}_{q,p}^{(1,2)}}\leq C\left\|f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}_{q,p}}\quad\text{for all }f\in\mathrm{L}_{q,p}(\R_{+}\times\R_{+}^{n+1};E).

Since the technique of proof is very similar to parts which appear in the proof for the general domain case, at this point we skip the details.

5.
Elliptic vector-valued PDE: The case of
\mathrm{C}^{2m}
-domains

5.1.
Localisation techniques for domains

So far, we have considered the half-space
\Omega=\R_{+}^{n+1}
as a model case.
Via bending and localisation techniques, we may now transfer the result to more general domains which are sufficiently regular.
Let
E
be a Banach space of class
\mathcal{HT}
and
m,n,m_{1},\ldots,m_{m}\in\N
be natural numbers such that
m_{j}<2m
for
j=1,\ldots,m
.
For each
j=1,\ldots,m
and
k=0,\ldots,m_{j}
, let
\mathcal{P}_{j,k}
be a projection such that
\mathcal{P}_{j,k}\mathcal{P}_{j,k^{\prime}}=0
whenever
k\neq k^{\prime}
.
Let
\Omega\subseteq\R^{n+1}
be a domain with compact
\mathrm{C}^{2m}
-boundary, i.e. we assume that
\partial\Omega\subseteq\R^{n+1}
is compact, and for each boundary point
\boldsymbol{x}_{0}\in\partial\Omega
there exist local coordinates of class
\mathrm{C}^{2m}
which are obtained from the original one by rotating and shifting, and such that the positive
x_{n+1}
-axis corresponds to the direction of the inner normal vector
-\boldsymbol{n}(\boldsymbol{x}_{0})
to
\Omega
at
\boldsymbol{x}_{0}
.
These local coordinates may be chosen such that they depend continuously on boundary point
\boldsymbol{x}_{0}\in\partial\Omega
.
We now consider the differential operators with spatial dependent coefficients of the form

\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{x},\mathrm{D})=\sum_{\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|\leq 2m}a_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(\boldsymbol{x})\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}},\quad\mathcal{B}_{j}(\boldsymbol{x},\mathrm{D})=\sum_{k=0}^{m_{j}}\sum_{\left|\boldsymbol{\beta}\right|\leq k}b_{j,k,\boldsymbol{\beta}}(\boldsymbol{x})\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\mathcal{P}_{j,k},\quad j=1,\ldots,m,

where the
\B(E)
-valued coefficients satisfy the following regularity and ellipticity conditions.

Assumption 5.1
.

Let the following conditions be satisfied:

(1)

Smoothness conditions:

(a)

a_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{l}}(\overline{\Omega};\B(E))
for each multi-index
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\N_{0}^{n+1}
of length
\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|=2m
;

(b)

a_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in[\mathrm{L}_{\infty}+\mathrm{L}_{r_{k}}](\Omega;\B(E))
for some
r_{k}\geq p
such that
2m-k>\tfrac{n}{r_{k}}
, for each multi-index
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\N_{0}^{n+1}
of length
\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|=k<2m
;

(c)

b_{j,k,\boldsymbol{\beta}}\in\mathrm{C}^{2m-k}(\partial\Omega;\B(E))
and
b_{j,k,\boldsymbol{\beta}}(\ran(\mathcal{P}_{j,k}))\subseteq\ran(\mathcal{P}_{j,k})+\ldots+\ran(\mathcal{P}_{j,m_{j}})
for each
j=1,\ldots,m
,
0\leq k\leq m_{j}
and each multi-index
\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\N_{0}^{n+1}
of length
\left|\boldsymbol{\beta}\right|=k
.

(2)

There is an angle
\phi_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}}\in[0,\pi)
such that the following assertions hold true:

(a)

ellipticity of the interior symbol:
The principal symbol

\mathcal{A}_{\#}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\xi})=\sum_{\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|=2m}a_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(\boldsymbol{x})\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}

is parameter elliptic with angle of ellipticity less than
\phi_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}}
, for each
\boldsymbol{x}\in\overline{\Omega}\cup\{\infty\}
.

(b)

Lopatinskii–Shapiro condition:
At each boundary point
\boldsymbol{x}\in\partial\Omega
, and for all
\lambda\in\overline{\Sigma}_{\pi-\phi_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}}}
and
\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\R^{n+1}
such that
\boldsymbol{\xi}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}(\boldsymbol{x}_{0})=0
, but
(\lambda,\boldsymbol{\xi})\neq(0,\boldsymbol{0})
, the initial value problem

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{\#}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\xi}+\mathrm{i}\boldsymbol{n}\tfrac{\partial}{\partial y})v(y)

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\text{for }y>0,

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{j}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\xi}+\mathrm{i}\boldsymbol{n}\tfrac{\partial}{\partial y})v(0)

\displaystyle=h_{j}

\displaystyle\text{for }j=1,\ldots,m,

has a unique solution
v\in\mathrm{C}_{0}(\R_{+};E)
, for each
(h_{1},\ldots,h_{m})^{\mathsf{T}}\in E^{m}
.

Under these assumptions, we may now prove maximal regularity of the parabolic problem associated to the
\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Omega;E)
-realisation of the boundary value problem.

Theorem 5.2
.

Let
E
be a Banach space of class
\mathcal{HT}
,
n,m\in\N
and
p\in(1,\infty)
.
Let
\Omega\subseteq\R^{n+1}
be a domain with compact
\mathrm{C}^{2m}
-boundary. Suppose that for some angle
\phi_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}}\in[0,\pi)
the boundary value problem
(\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{x},D),(\mathcal{B}_{j}(\boldsymbol{x},D))_{j=1}^{m})
satisfies Assumption
5.1
.
Let
A_{B}
denote the
\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Omega;E)
-realisation of the boundary value problem with domain

\dom(A_{B})=\{u\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2m}(\Omega;E):\,\mathcal{B}_{j}(\boldsymbol{x},\mathrm{D})u=0\,\text{on }\partial\Omega,\,j=1,\ldots,m\}.

Then, for each
\phi>\phi_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}}
, there is a constant
\mu_{\phi}\geq 0
such that
\mu_{\phi}+A_{B}
is
\mathcal{R}
-sectorial with
\phi^{\mathcal{R}}_{\mu_{\phi}+A_{B}}\leq\phi
.
In particular, if
\phi_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}}<\tfrac{\pi}{2}
, the parabolic initial-boundary value problem

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u+A_{B}u+\mu_{\phi}u

\displaystyle=f,

\displaystyle t>0,

\displaystyle u(0)

\displaystyle=0

has the property of maximal regularity in the class
\mathrm{L}_{q}(\R_{+};\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Omega;E))
for each
q\in(1,\infty)
.

Proof.

The localisation procedure as presented in
[
17
, Section 8.2]
carries over almost literally; again we consider the operators
\mathcal{B}_{j}(\mathrm{D})\mathcal{P}_{j,k}
for each pair
(j,k)
separately, but the technique stays the same.

As a first step, one needs to find suitable (local) diffeomorphisms which can be used to
flatten
the boundary. To this end, given any
\boldsymbol{x}_{0}\in\partial\Omega
there are local coordinates such that the inner normal vector
-\boldsymbol{n}(\boldsymbol{x}_{0})
to
\Omega
at
\boldsymbol{x}_{0}
corresponds to the
n+1
-st direction, and then, since
\Omega
is a
\mathrm{C}^{2m}
-domain, one finds open sets
U_{1}\subseteq\R^{n}
and
U_{2}\subseteq\R
such that
\boldsymbol{x}_{0}\in U:=U_{1}\times U_{2}\subseteq\R^{n+1}
, and there is a height function
h\in\mathrm{C}^{2m}(\overline{U_{1}};\R)
such that
\Omega\cap U=\{(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y)\in U:\,y>h(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})\}
and
\partial\Omega\cap U=\{(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y)\in U:\,y=h(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})\}
and
\Phi(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y)=(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y-h(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}))^{\mathsf{T}}
defines a
\mathrm{C}^{2m}
-diffeomorphism between
U
and
\tilde{U}:=\Phi(U)
, which can be extended to a
\mathrm{C}^{2m}
-diffeomorphism

\tilde{\Phi}:\{\boldsymbol{x}=(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y)\in\R^{n+1}:\,y>\tilde{h}(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})\}\rightarrow\R_{+}^{n+1}\quad\text{with }\tilde{\Phi}|_{U}=\Phi.

In the following, we often will not distinguish between
\Phi
and its extension
\tilde{\Phi}
, if no confusion may arise.
Moreover, such a diffeomorphism
\Phi
can be chosen to be
\mathrm{C}^{2m}
-admissible
, i.e. the normal direction to
\partial\Omega
at
\boldsymbol{x}_{0}\in\partial\Omega
and the tangent space
T_{\partial\Omega;\boldsymbol{x}_{0}}
to
\partial\Omega
at
\boldsymbol{x}_{0}\in\partial\Omega
are transformed into the normal direction and the tangential space
T_{\R_{+}^{n+1};\Phi(\boldsymbol{x}_{0})}
at
\Phi(\boldsymbol{x}_{0})\in\partial\R_{+}^{n+1}
to
\R_{+}^{n+1}
.
The latter property can be characterised by a block-diagonal form of the Jacobi matrix
\mathrm{D}\Phi(\boldsymbol{x}_{0})
, i.e.

\mathrm{D}\Phi(\boldsymbol{x}_{0})=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\boldsymbol{H}(\boldsymbol{x}_{0})&0\\
0&d(\boldsymbol{x}_{0})\end{array}\right),

where
\boldsymbol{H}(\boldsymbol{x}_{0})\in\R^{n\times n}
is invertible and
d(\boldsymbol{x}_{0})>0
is a positive scalar.
Here, replacing
\Phi(\boldsymbol{x})
by
(\mathrm{D}\Phi(\boldsymbol{x}_{0}))^{-1}\Phi(\boldsymbol{x})
, we may w.l.o.g. assume that
(\mathrm{D}\Phi)(\boldsymbol{x}_{0})=\boldsymbol{I}_{n+1}
is the identity matrix.
Using the admissible
\mathrm{C}^{2m}
-diffeomorphisms, we may now
push forward
any differential equation on
\Omega
by defining

\mathcal{G}^{\Phi}:u\mapsto\mathcal{G}^{\Phi}u:=v,\quad\mathcal{G}^{\Phi}u(\boldsymbol{y})=v(\boldsymbol{y}):=u(\Phi^{-1}(\boldsymbol{y})),\quad\boldsymbol{y}\in\tilde{U}\cap\R_{+}^{n+1}.

Given a multi-index
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\N_{0}^{n+1}\setminus\{\boldsymbol{0}\}
, by the Leibniz formula for derivatives we then obtain that

\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(\mathcal{G}^{\Phi}u)(\boldsymbol{y})=\sum_{1\leq\left|\boldsymbol{\gamma}\right|\leq\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|}q_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\gamma}}(\Phi;\boldsymbol{y})(\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}u)(\Phi^{-1}(\boldsymbol{y})),

where each
q_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
is a homogeneous polynomial of degree
\left|\boldsymbol{\gamma}\right|
in the derivatives
\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(\Phi^{-1})
of order
\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\N_{0}^{n+1}
such that
1\leq\left|\boldsymbol{\beta}\right|\leq\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|-\left|\boldsymbol{\gamma}\right|-1
.
Therefore, in this way the differential operators
\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{D})=\sum_{\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|\leq 2m}a_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(\boldsymbol{x})\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
and
\mathcal{B}_{j}(\boldsymbol{x},\mathrm{D})=\sum_{k=0}^{m_{j}}\sum_{\left|\boldsymbol{\beta}\right|\leq k}b_{j,k,\boldsymbol{\beta}}(\boldsymbol{x})\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\mathcal{P}_{j,k}
will be pushed forward to differential operators of the same order

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{\Phi}(\boldsymbol{y},\mathrm{D})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|\leq 2m}a^{\Phi}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(\boldsymbol{y})\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}},

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{j}^{\Phi}(\boldsymbol{y},\mathrm{D})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{m_{j}}\sum_{\left|\boldsymbol{\beta}\right|\leq k}b^{\Phi}_{j,k,\boldsymbol{\beta}}(\boldsymbol{y})\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\mathcal{P}_{j,k},\quad j=1,\ldots,m.

Alternatively, we may write
\mathcal{A}^{\Phi}(\boldsymbol{y},\mathrm{D})=\mathcal{G}\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{x},\mathrm{D})\mathcal{G}^{-1}
for
y\in\tilde{U}\cap\R_{+}^{n+1}
and
\mathcal{B}^{\Phi}_{j}(\boldsymbol{y},\mathrm{D})=\mathcal{G}\mathcal{B}^{\Phi}_{j}(\boldsymbol{x},\mathrm{D})\mathcal{G}^{-1}
for
y\in\partial\R_{+}^{n+1}\cap\tilde{U}
.
Since

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{\Phi}(\boldsymbol{y},\mathrm{D})(\mathcal{G}u)(\boldsymbol{y})

\displaystyle=\big(\mathcal{G}\sum_{\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|\leq 2m}a_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(\boldsymbol{x})\mathrm{D}_{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\mathcal{G}^{-1}\mathcal{G}v\big)(\boldsymbol{y})=\mathcal{G}\sum_{\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|\leq 2m}a_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(\Phi^{-1}(\boldsymbol{y}))\mathrm{D}_{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}|_{\boldsymbol{x}=\Phi^{-1}(\boldsymbol{y})}u(\Phi^{-1}(\boldsymbol{y}))

\displaystyle=\sum_{\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|\leq 2m}a_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(\Phi^{-1}(\boldsymbol{y}))\big(\mathrm{D}\Phi(\Phi^{-1}y))^{\mathsf{T}}\mathrm{D}_{\boldsymbol{y}}\big)^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(\mathcal{G}u)(\boldsymbol{y}),

the principal part of the interior differential operator is given by

\mathcal{A}^{\Phi}_{\#}(\boldsymbol{y},\mathrm{D})=\sum_{\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|=2m}a_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(\Phi^{-1}(\boldsymbol{y}))\big(\mathrm{D}\Phi(\Phi^{-1}(\boldsymbol{y}))^{\mathsf{T}}\mathrm{D}\big)^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}=\mathcal{A}_{\#}(\Phi^{-1}(\boldsymbol{y}),\mathrm{D}\Phi(\Phi^{-1}(\boldsymbol{y}))^{\mathsf{T}}\mathrm{D}).

Similarly, for the boundary operators we obtain that

\mathcal{B}^{\Phi}_{j}(\boldsymbol{y},\mathrm{D})=\mathcal{G}\big(\sum_{k=0}^{m_{j}}\sum_{\left|\boldsymbol{\beta}\right|\leq k}b_{j,k,\boldsymbol{\beta}}(\boldsymbol{x})\mathrm{D}_{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\mathcal{P}_{j,k}\big)\mathcal{G}^{-1}=\sum_{k=0}^{m_{j}}\sum_{\left|\boldsymbol{\beta}\right|\leq k}b_{j,k,\boldsymbol{\beta}}(\Phi^{-1}(\boldsymbol{y}))\big[\mathrm{D}\Phi(\Phi^{-1}(\boldsymbol{y}))^{\mathsf{T}}\mathrm{D}\big]^{\boldsymbol{\beta}}

and from this their principle parts

\mathcal{B}^{\Phi}_{j,\#}(\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{\xi})=\sum_{k=0}^{m_{j}}\sum_{\left|\boldsymbol{\beta}\right|=k}b_{j,k,\boldsymbol{\beta}}(\Phi^{-1}(\boldsymbol{y}))\big[\mathrm{D}\Phi(\Phi^{-1}(\boldsymbol{y}))^{\mathsf{T}}\boldsymbol{\xi}\big]^{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=\mathcal{B}_{j,\#}(\Phi^{-1}(\boldsymbol{y}),\mathrm{D}\Phi(\Phi^{-1}(\boldsymbol{y}))^{\mathsf{T}}\boldsymbol{\xi}).

With these relations at hand, we may check that the Lopatinskii–Shapiro condition is unaffected under
\mathrm{C}^{2m}
-admissible transformations.
By this, we mean the following.
The Lopatinskii–Shapiro condition for the original problem reads:
For every
\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\phi}
, every point
\boldsymbol{x}\in\partial\Omega
and every
\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\R^{n}
such that
\boldsymbol{\xi}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}(\boldsymbol{x})=0
for the outer normal vector
\boldsymbol{n}(\boldsymbol{x}_{0})
at
\boldsymbol{x}_{0}
, and every
\boldsymbol{w}=(w_{1},\ldots,w_{m})^{\mathsf{T}}\in E^{m}
, the initial boundary value problem

\begin{cases}(\lambda+\mathcal{A}_{\#}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\xi}+\mathrm{i}\boldsymbol{n}\frac{\partial}{\partial\eta})v(\eta)=0,&\eta>0,\\
\mathcal{B}_{j,\#}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\xi}+\mathrm{i}\boldsymbol{n}\frac{\partial}{\partial\eta})v(0)=w_{j},&j=1,\ldots,m\end{cases}

has a unique solution
v=v_{\boldsymbol{x},\lambda,\boldsymbol{\xi}}(\boldsymbol{w})
in the class
v\in\mathrm{C}_{0}(\R_{+};E)
.
The Lopatinskii–Shapiro condition for the transformed problem involves the initial value problem

\begin{cases}(\lambda+\mathcal{A}^{\Phi}_{\#}(\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},-\mathrm{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial\sigma})v^{\Phi}(\sigma)=0,&\sigma>0,\\
\mathcal{B}^{\Phi}_{j,\#}(\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},-\mathrm{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial\sigma})v^{\Phi}(\sigma)=w^{\Phi}_{j},&j=1,\ldots,m.\end{cases}

From the previous considerations, this means that

\begin{cases}(\lambda+\mathcal{A}_{\#}(\Phi^{-1}(\boldsymbol{y}),\mathrm{D}\Phi(\Phi^{-1}(\boldsymbol{y}))^{\mathsf{T}}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},\frac{\partial}{\partial\sigma})^{\mathsf{T}})v^{\Phi}(\sigma)=0,&\sigma>0,\\
\mathcal{B}_{j,\#}(\Phi^{-1}(\boldsymbol{y}),\mathrm{D}\Phi(\Phi^{-1}(\boldsymbol{y}))^{\mathsf{T}}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},\frac{\partial}{\partial\sigma})^{\mathsf{T}})v^{\Phi}(\sigma)=w^{\Phi}_{j},&j=1,\ldots,m,\end{cases}

where we may write
\mathrm{D}\Phi(\Phi^{-1}(\boldsymbol{y}))^{\mathsf{T}}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime},-\mathrm{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial\sigma})^{\mathsf{T}}=\kappa(\boldsymbol{y})\big(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\xi}}(\Phi^{-1}(\boldsymbol{y}),\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime})+\mathrm{i}\boldsymbol{n}(\Phi^{-1}(\boldsymbol{y}))\big)
for some
\kappa(\boldsymbol{y})>0
and
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in\R^{n}
.
Since
\Phi
is an admissible
\mathrm{C}^{2m}
-diffeomorphism it holds that
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}=0
.
Therefore, the solutions to the original (at
\boldsymbol{x}
) and the transformed (at
\boldsymbol{y}=\Phi(\boldsymbol{x})
) initial value problem are related via

w^{\Phi}_{\boldsymbol{y},\lambda,\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime}}(\sigma)=w_{\Phi^{-1}(\boldsymbol{y}),\lambda,\tilde{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\frac{\sigma}{\kappa}),\quad\text{that is}\quad w_{\boldsymbol{x},\lambda,\boldsymbol{\xi}}(\eta)=w^{\Phi}_{\Phi(\boldsymbol{y}),\lambda,\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\prime}}(\kappa\eta),

where
\kappa>0
is the norm of
\mathrm{D}\Phi(\Phi^{-1}(\boldsymbol{y}))^{\mathsf{T}}\boldsymbol{n}=\mathrm{D}\Phi(\boldsymbol{x})^{\mathsf{T}}\boldsymbol{n}
.
This shows, in particular, that the validity of the Lopatinskii–Shapiro condition is not affected by admissible
\mathrm{C}^{2m}
-diffeomorphisms.

Now, we may find such an admissible
\mathrm{C}^{2m}
-diffeomorphism
\Phi_{\boldsymbol{x}_{0}}:U_{\boldsymbol{x}_{0}}\rightarrow\tilde{U}_{\boldsymbol{x}_{0}}
for each boundary point
\boldsymbol{x}_{0}\in\partial\Omega
, and moreover, since
U_{\boldsymbol{x}_{0}}
is an open neighbourhood of
\boldsymbol{x}_{0}
, we find radii
r(\boldsymbol{x}_{0})>0
such that
\Phi_{\boldsymbol{x}_{0}}^{-1}\big(B_{2r(\boldsymbol{x}_{0})}(\boldsymbol{y}_{0})\big)\subseteq U_{\boldsymbol{x}_{0}}
for each
\boldsymbol{y}_{0}=\Phi(\boldsymbol{x}_{0})
and, for some
\varepsilon_{0}>0
which is given a priori, the coefficients for the principle parts of the pushed-forward operators have locally small oscillations in the sense that

\displaystyle\sum_{\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|}\left\|a^{\Phi}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(\boldsymbol{y})-a^{\Phi}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(\boldsymbol{y}_{0})\right\|

\displaystyle<\varepsilon_{0},

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{m_{j}}\sum_{\left|\boldsymbol{\beta}\right|=k}\left\|b^{\Phi}_{j,k,\boldsymbol{\beta}}(\boldsymbol{y})-b^{\Phi}_{j,k,\boldsymbol{\beta}}(\boldsymbol{y}_{0})\right\|

\displaystyle<\varepsilon_{0}\quad\text{for all }\boldsymbol{y}\in B_{2r(\boldsymbol{x}_{0})}(\boldsymbol{y}_{0}).

Then, trivially

\bigcup_{\boldsymbol{x}_{0}\in\partial\Omega}\Phi_{\boldsymbol{x}_{0}}^{-1}\big(B_{r(\boldsymbol{x}_{0})}(\boldsymbol{x}_{0})\big)\supset\partial\Omega

is an open cover for the compact boundary
\partial\Omega
, so that we may choose a finite subcover
\bigcup_{\nu=1}^{N}U_{\nu}
with
U_{\nu}=\Phi_{x_{\nu}}^{-1}(B_{r(\boldsymbol{x}_{\nu})}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\nu}))
for some
\{\boldsymbol{x}_{1},\ldots,x_{N}\}\subseteq\partial\Omega
and
\boldsymbol{y}_{\nu}:=\Phi(\boldsymbol{x}_{\nu})
,
\nu=1,\ldots,N
.

For each of these
\nu=1,\ldots,N
, we may then extend the top-order coefficients
a^{\Phi}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(\boldsymbol{y})
on
B_{r(\boldsymbol{x}_{\nu})}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\nu})\cap\R_{+}^{n+1}
to the half-space
\R_{+}^{n+1}
by reflection at the surface
\partial B_{r(\boldsymbol{x}_{\nu})}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\nu})
, viz.

\displaystyle a^{\nu}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(\boldsymbol{y}):=\begin{cases}a^{\Phi_{\boldsymbol{x}_{\nu}}}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(\boldsymbol{y}),&\boldsymbol{y}\in\overline{B_{r(\boldsymbol{x}_{\nu})}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\nu})}\cap\overline{\R_{+}^{n+1}},\\
a^{\Phi_{\boldsymbol{x}_{\nu}}}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\nu}+\frac{r(\boldsymbol{x}_{\nu})^{2}}{\left|\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{y}_{\nu}\right|^{2}}(\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{y}_{\nu})),&\boldsymbol{y}\in\overline{\R_{+}^{n+1}}\setminus\overline{B_{r(\boldsymbol{x}_{\nu})}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\nu})}\end{cases}\quad\text{for }\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\N_{0}^{n+1}\text{ with }\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|=2m,

and also extend the coefficients of the boundary symbols for all
j=1,\ldots,m
and
k=0,\ldots,m_{j}
according to

\displaystyle b^{\nu}_{j,k,\boldsymbol{\beta}}(\boldsymbol{y})

\displaystyle=b^{\Phi_{\boldsymbol{x}_{\nu}}}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\nu}+\chi(\frac{\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{y}_{\nu}}{r(\boldsymbol{x}_{\nu})})(\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{y}_{\nu})),\quad\boldsymbol{y}\in\R_{+}^{n+1}

where
\chi\in\mathrm{C}_{0}^{\infty}(\R^{n})
has compact support which is contained in
B_{2}(\boldsymbol{0})
, and is identically
1
on
\overline{B_{1}(\boldsymbol{0})}
.
Note that these definitions lead to top order coefficients on
\overline{\R_{+}^{n+1}}
which still have small oscillations, i.e.

\sum_{\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|=2m}\left\|a^{\nu}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(\boldsymbol{y})-a^{\nu}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\nu})\right\|,\quad\sum_{k=0}^{m_{j}}\sum_{\left|\boldsymbol{\beta}\right|=k}\left\|b^{\nu}_{j,k,\boldsymbol{\beta}}(\boldsymbol{y})-b^{\nu}_{j,k,\boldsymbol{\beta}}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\nu})\right\|<\varepsilon_{0},\quad j=1,\ldots,m,\,\boldsymbol{y}\in\overline{\R_{+}^{n+1}}.

For any given
\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\phi}
and
f\in\mathrm{L}_{p}(\Omega;E)
, let us consider the boundary value problem

\begin{cases}\lambda u+\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{x},\mathrm{D})u=f,&\text{in }\Omega,\\
\mathcal{B}_{j}(\boldsymbol{x},\mathrm{D})u=0,&\text{on }\partial\Omega,\,j=1,\ldots,m.\end{cases}

We choose a partition of unity
(\varphi_{\nu})_{\nu=1,\ldots,M}\subseteq\mathrm{C}^{\infty}(\R^{n})
with
M\geq N
and such that
0\leq\varphi_{\nu}\leq 1
for all
\nu=1,\ldots,M
, and
\operatorname{supp}\varphi_{\nu}\subseteq U_{\nu}
for
\nu=1,\ldots,N
whereas
\operatorname{supp}\varphi_{\nu}\subseteq\R^{n}\setminus\partial\Omega
for
\nu=N+1,\ldots,M
.
Then a function
u\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{2m}(\Omega;E)
solves the boundary value problem (
5.1
) if and only if for
\nu=1,\ldots,N

\begin{cases}\lambda(\varphi_{\nu}u)+\varphi_{\nu}\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{x},\mathrm{D})u=\varphi_{\nu}f&\text{in }\Omega\cap U_{\nu},\\
\varphi_{\nu}\mathcal{B}_{j}(\boldsymbol{x},\mathrm{D})u=0&\text{on }\partial\Omega\cap U_{\nu},\,j=1,\ldots,m\end{cases}

and for
\nu=N+1,\ldots,M

\lambda(\varphi_{\nu}u)+\varphi_{\nu}\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{x},\mathrm{D})u=\varphi_{\nu}f\quad\text{on }\Omega.

Note that in the latter case no boundary conditions are involved since
\varphi_{\nu}\equiv 0
in a neighbourhood of
\partial\Omega
.
For
\nu=N+1,\ldots,M
, we may write the equation equivalently as

\lambda(\varphi_{\nu}u)+\mathcal{A}_{\#}(\boldsymbol{x},\mathrm{D})(\varphi_{\nu}u)=\varphi_{\nu}f+\big[\mathcal{A}_{\#},\varphi_{\nu}\big]u-\varphi_{\nu}(\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{x},\mathrm{D})-\mathcal{A}_{\#}(\boldsymbol{x},\mathrm{D}))u=:\varphi_{\nu}f+\mathcal{C}_{\nu}(\boldsymbol{x},\mathrm{D})u,

where the latter terms on the right-hand side include the commutator
[\mathcal{A}_{\#}(\boldsymbol{x},\mathrm{D}),\varphi_{\nu}]
between the principle part
\mathcal{A}_{\#}(\boldsymbol{x},\mathrm{D})
of the elliptic differential operator
\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{x},\mathrm{D})
and the multiplication operator for multiplication with
\varphi_{\nu}
, and
\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{x},\mathrm{D})-\mathcal{A}_{\#}(\boldsymbol{x},\mathrm{D})
are the lower-order terms of
\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{x},\mathrm{D})
.
We may, therefore, for
\nu=N+1,\ldots,M
, denote by
A_{\nu}
the
\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E)
-realisation of the operator

\mathcal{A}^{\nu}(\boldsymbol{x},\mathrm{D})=\sum_{\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|=2m}a^{\nu}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(\boldsymbol{x})\mathrm{D}^{\alpha}

and set
R^{\lambda}_{\nu}:=(\lambda+A_{\nu})^{-1}
for the resolvent operator, if it exists.
Then,
u
has to respect the identity

\varphi_{\nu}u=R^{\lambda}_{\nu}(\varphi_{\nu}f)+R^{\lambda}_{\nu}\mathcal{C}_{\nu}(\boldsymbol{x},\mathrm{D})u,\quad\nu=N+1,\ldots,M.

On the other hand, for
\nu=1,\ldots,N
, we use the local coordinates and the
\mathrm{C}^{2m}
-diffeomorphisms introduced above and note that the pull-back operators
\mathcal{G}_{\nu}
corresponding to the diffeomorphisms
\Phi_{\nu}=\Phi_{\boldsymbol{x}_{\nu}}
,
\nu=1,\ldots,N
, act as continuous operators between the Sobolev spaces
\mathrm{W}_{p}^{l}(U_{k};E)
and
\mathrm{W}_{p}^{l}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E)
, viz.

\mathcal{G}_{\nu}:\quad u\mapsto u^{\Phi_{\nu}},\,u^{\Phi_{\nu}}(\boldsymbol{y})=u(\Phi_{\nu}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{y})),\quad\mathrm{W}_{p}^{l}(U_{\nu};E)\mapsto\mathrm{W}_{p}^{l}(\R_{+}^{n+1};E),\quad\nu=1,\ldots,N,\,l=0,1,\ldots,2m.

We set

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{\nu}(\boldsymbol{y},\mathrm{D})

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|=2m}a^{\nu}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(\boldsymbol{y})\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}},

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}^{\nu}_{j}(\boldsymbol{y},\mathrm{D})

\displaystyle:=\sum_{k=0}^{m_{j}}b^{\nu}_{j,k,\boldsymbol{\beta}}(\boldsymbol{y})\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\mathcal{P}_{j,k},\quad j=1,\ldots,m,

and rewrite the corresponding boundary value problems for
\varphi_{\nu}u
,
\nu=1,\ldots,N
, as

\begin{cases}\lambda\mathcal{G}_{\nu}(\varphi_{\nu}u)+\mathcal{A}^{\nu}(\boldsymbol{y},\mathrm{D})\mathcal{G}_{\nu}(\varphi_{\nu}u)&=\mathcal{G}_{\nu}(\varphi_{\nu}f)+\mathcal{G}_{\nu}\big(\big[\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{x},\mathrm{D}),\varphi_{\nu}\big]-\mathcal{G}_{k}^{-1}(\mathcal{A}^{\Phi_{\nu}}(\boldsymbol{y},\mathrm{D})-\mathcal{A}^{\nu}(\boldsymbol{y},\mathrm{D}))\mathcal{G}_{k}\varphi_{\nu}\big)u,\\
\mathcal{B}^{\nu}_{j}(\boldsymbol{y},\mathrm{D})\mathcal{G}_{\nu}(\varphi_{\nu}u)&=\mathcal{G}_{\nu}\big(\big[\mathcal{B}_{j}(\boldsymbol{x},\mathrm{D}),\varphi_{\nu}\big]-\mathcal{G}_{\nu}^{-1}(\mathcal{B}^{\Phi_{\nu}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{y},\mathrm{D})-\mathcal{B}^{\nu}_{j}(\boldsymbol{y},\mathrm{D}))\mathcal{G}_{\nu}\varphi_{\nu}\big)u.\end{cases}

Since we imposed the condition that
b_{j,k,\boldsymbol{\beta}}(\ran\mathcal{P}_{j,k})\subseteq\ran(\mathcal{P}_{j,k})+\ldots+\ran(\mathcal{P}_{m_{j},k})
, on the right-hand side only lower order differential operators appear, i.e. the right-hand side includes only derivatives up to order
2m-1
resp. up to order
k-1
on every subspace
\ran\mathcal{P}_{j,k}
,
k=1,\ldots,m_{j}
.
Hence, these terms can be considered as a small perturbation to the operators
\mathcal{A}^{\nu}(\boldsymbol{y},\mathrm{D})
and
\mathcal{B}^{\nu}_{j}(\boldsymbol{y},\mathrm{D})
,
j=1,\ldots,m
, respectively. The solution then will satisfy the implicit relation

\mathcal{G}_{\nu}(\varphi_{\nu}u)=\tilde{R}^{\lambda,\nu}_{0}\big(\mathcal{G}_{\nu}(\varphi\nu u)+\mathcal{G}_{\nu}\mathcal{C}^{\nu}(\boldsymbol{x},\mathrm{D})u\big)+\sum_{j=0}^{m}\sum_{k=0}^{m_{j}}\tilde{S}^{\lambda,\nu}_{j,k}\mathcal{G}_{\nu}\mathcal{D}^{\nu}_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{x},\mathrm{D})u,

where we write

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{\nu}(\boldsymbol{x},\mathrm{D})u

\displaystyle:=\big[\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{x},\mathrm{D}),\varphi_{\nu}\big]u-\mathcal{G}_{\nu}^{-1}(\mathcal{A}^{\Phi_{\nu}}(\boldsymbol{y},\mathrm{D})-\mathcal{A}^{\nu}(\boldsymbol{y},\mathrm{D}))\mathcal{G}_{\nu}u,

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{\nu}_{j,k}u

\displaystyle:=\big[\mathcal{B}_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{x},\mathrm{D}),\varphi_{\nu}\big]-\mathcal{G}_{\nu}^{-1}(\mathcal{B}_{j,k}^{\Phi_{\nu}}(\boldsymbol{y},\mathrm{D})-\mathcal{B}^{\nu}_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{y},\mathrm{D}))\mathcal{G}_{\nu}u.

From the solution and maximal regularity theory on the half-space we know that we may write

\displaystyle\tilde{S}^{\lambda,\nu}_{j,k}\mathcal{D}^{\nu}_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{x},\mathrm{D})

\displaystyle=\big(\tilde{S}^{\lambda,\nu,\mathrm{I}}_{j,k}+\tilde{S}^{\lambda,\nu,\mathrm{II}}_{j,k}\big)\mathcal{D}^{\nu}_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{x},\mathrm{D})

\displaystyle=\tilde{T}^{\lambda,\nu}_{j,k}(\left|\lambda\right|+(-\Delta)^{2m})^{(2m-k)/2m}\mathcal{D}^{\nu}_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{x},\mathrm{D})

\displaystyle\quad+\tilde{R}^{\lambda,\nu}_{j,k}(\left|\lambda\right|+(-\Delta)^{2m})^{(2m-k-1)/2m}\partial_{n+1}\mathcal{D}^{\nu}_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{x},\mathrm{D})

where the operators
\tilde{T}^{\lambda,\nu}_{j,k}
and
R^{\lambda,\nu}_{j,k}
satisfy
\mathcal{R}
-bounds

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(\{\left|\lambda\right|^{1-\frac{\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|}{2m}}\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\tilde{T}^{\lambda,\nu}_{j,k}:\,\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\phi},\,\left|\lambda\right|\geq\lambda_{0},\,\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|\leq 2m\})

\displaystyle<\infty,

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(\{\left|\lambda\right|^{1-\frac{\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|}{2m}}\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\tilde{R}^{\lambda,\nu}_{j,k}:\,\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\phi},\,\left|\lambda\right|\geq\lambda_{0},\,\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|\leq 2m\})

\displaystyle<\infty,

for each fixed
j=1,\ldots,m
,
k=0,1,\ldots,m_{j}
and
\nu=1,\ldots,N
.
However, the set of admissible indices
(j,k,\nu)
is finite, which implies that

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(\{\left|\lambda\right|^{1-\frac{\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|}{2m}}\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\tilde{T}^{\lambda,\nu}_{j,k}:\,\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\phi},\,\left|\lambda\right|\geq\lambda_{0},\,\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|\leq 2m,\,1\leq j\leq m,\,0\leq k\leq m_{j},\,1\leq\nu\leq N\})

\displaystyle<\infty,

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(\{\left|\lambda\right|^{1-\frac{\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|}{2m}}\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\tilde{R}^{\lambda,\nu}_{j,k}:\,\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\phi},\,\left|\lambda\right|\geq\lambda_{0},\,\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|\leq 2m,\,1\leq j\leq m,\,0\leq k\leq m_{j},\,1\leq\nu\leq N\})

\displaystyle<\infty.

Introducing

T^{\lambda,\nu}_{j,k}:=\mathcal{G}_{\nu}^{-1}\tilde{T}^{\lambda,\nu}_{j,k}\mathcal{G}_{\nu}\quad\text{and}\quad R^{\lambda,\nu}_{j,k}:=\mathcal{G}_{\nu}^{-1}\tilde{R}^{\lambda,\nu}_{j,k}\mathcal{G}_{\nu}

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{\nu,I}_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{x},\mathrm{D})

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{G}_{\nu}^{-1}\big((-\Delta)^{2m}+\left|\lambda\right|\big)^{(2m-k)/2m}\mathcal{G}_{\nu}\mathcal{D}^{\nu}_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{x},\mathrm{D}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{\nu,II}_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{x},\mathrm{D})

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{G}_{\nu}^{-1}\big((-\Delta)+\left|\lambda\right|\big)^{(2m-k-1)/2m}\partial_{n+1}\mathcal{G}_{\nu}\mathcal{D}^{\nu}_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{x},\mathrm{D})

by pull-back we get (for
\nu=1,\ldots,N
) the identities

\displaystyle\varphi_{\nu}u

\displaystyle=R^{\lambda,\nu}_{0}\big(\varphi\nu u+\mathcal{C}^{\nu}(\boldsymbol{x},\mathrm{D})u\big)+\sum_{j=0}^{m}\sum_{k=0}^{m_{j}}\tilde{S}^{\lambda,\nu}_{j,k}\mathcal{D}^{\nu}_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{x},\mathrm{D})u

\displaystyle=R^{\lambda,\nu}_{0}\big(\varphi\nu u+\mathcal{C}^{\nu}(\boldsymbol{x},\mathrm{D})u\big)+\sum_{j=0}^{m}\sum_{k=0}^{m_{j}}T^{\lambda,\nu}_{j,k}\mathcal{D}^{\nu,I}_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{x},\mathrm{D})u+R^{\lambda,\nu}_{j,k}\mathcal{D}^{\nu,II}_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{x},\mathrm{D})u.

Summing over
\nu=1,\ldots,M
, we arrive at

\displaystyle(\lambda+A_{B})^{-1}f

\displaystyle=u=\sum_{\nu=1}^{M}\phi_{\nu}u

\displaystyle=\sum_{\nu=1}^{M}R^{\lambda,\nu}(\phi_{\nu}f+\mathcal{C}^{\nu}(\boldsymbol{x},\mathrm{D})u)+\sum_{\nu=1}^{N}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\sum_{k=0}^{m_{j}}(T^{\lambda,\nu}_{j,k}\mathcal{D}^{\nu,I}_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{x},\mathrm{D})+R^{\lambda,\nu}_{j,k}\mathcal{D}^{\nu,II}_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{x},\mathrm{D}))u

\displaystyle=R(\lambda)f+S(\lambda)u,

where we set

R(\lambda)=\sum_{\nu=1}^{M}R^{\lambda,\nu}_{0}\phi_{\nu},\quad S(\lambda)=\sum_{\nu=1}^{N}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\sum_{k=0}^{m_{j}}(T^{\lambda,\nu}_{j,k}\mathcal{D}^{\lambda,\nu}_{j,k}+R^{\lambda,\nu}\mathcal{D}^{\lambda,\nu}_{j,k}).

Thus, provided
\left\|S(\lambda)\right\|<1
, we may solve for the resolvent operator by employing the Neumann series

(\lambda+A_{B})^{-1}=(I-S(\lambda))^{-1}R(\lambda)f=\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}S(\lambda)^{l}R(\lambda)f.

Here, each summand
S(\lambda)^{l}
,
l\geq 0
, is a sum of products of the form

U_{\lambda}^{0}\prod_{\eta=1}^{l}V_{\lambda}^{\eta}U_{\lambda}^{\eta},

where
U_{\lambda}^{\eta}\in\{R^{\lambda,\nu},R^{\lambda,\nu}_{j,k},T^{\lambda,\nu}_{j,k}:\,j=1,\ldots,m,\,k=0,\ldots,m_{j}\}
is one of the resolvent or solution operators for the BVP, and
V_{\lambda}^{\eta}\in\{\mathcal{C}^{\nu}(\boldsymbol{x},\mathrm{D}),\mathcal{D}^{\nu,I}_{j,k},\mathcal{D}^{\nu,II}_{j,k}:\,j=1,\ldots,m,\,k=0,\ldots,m_{j}\}
are all lower-order operators, such that any product
U_{\lambda}^{\eta}V_{\lambda}^{\eta}
is, in fact, a compact operator on
X
.
Finally, we may employ Remark
2.3
to conclude that for sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
and sufficiently large
\lambda_{0}\geq 0
, the family of operators given by such Neumann series
\{\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}S(\lambda)^{l}R(\lambda):\,\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pi-\phi},\,\left|\lambda\right|\geq\lambda_{0}\}
not only is well-defined, but actually
\mathcal{R}
-bounded.
By Theorem
2.12
, this implies that the abstract Cauchy problem

\begin{cases}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}u(t)+A_{B}u(t)=f(t),&t\geq 0,\\
u(0)=0\end{cases}

has
\mathrm{L}_{q}
-maximal regularity for all
q\in(1,\infty)
.
∎

6.
Inhomogeneous initial data and anisotropic Sobolev spaces

In
[
18
]
, the authors extended their results in
[
17
]
to the case of inhomogeneous initial data and anisotropic Sobolev spaces, i.e. distinct integrability parameters
p,q\in(1,\infty)
for the time and spatial variable, resp., by identifying the optimal spaces for the data to obtain optimal
\mathrm{L}_{p}
-
\mathrm{L}_{q}
-estimates.
These results again heavily rely on the results and techniques as in
[
17
]
, especially vector-valued multiplier theorems on Banach spaces with property
\mathcal{HT}
(i.e. UMD-spaces), Gagliardo-Nirenberg type estimates as well as optimal embedding results for Sobolev, Besov and Triebel-Lizorkin spaces. The methods used in
[
18
]
translate more or less directly to the situation considered here: Obviously, the optimal regularity spaces have to be adjusted since the boundary operators have different orders
k
in the subspaces
\ran(\mathcal{P}_{j,k})
corresponding to the projections
\mathcal{P}_{j,k}
.
We introduce the following assumptions for this section.

(D)

Assumptions on the data:

(i)

f\in\mathrm{L}_{p}(J\times\Omega;E)
, where
J=[0,T]
for some
T>0
;

(ii)

g_{j,k}\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2m)\cdot\kappa_{k}}(J\times\partial\Omega;\ran\mathcal{P}_{j,k})
, where
\kappa_{k}=\frac{2m-k-\nicefrac{{1}}{{p}}}{2m}
, and we then set
g_{j}=\sum_{k=0}^{m_{j}}g_{j,k}
;

(iii)

u_{0}\in\mathrm{B}_{p,p}^{2m(1-\nicefrac{{1}}{{p}})}(\Omega;E)
;

(iv)

if
\kappa_{k}>\nicefrac{{1}}{{p}}
, then
\mathcal{B}_{j}(0,\boldsymbol{x})\mathcal{P}_{j,k}u_{0}(\boldsymbol{x})=g_{j,k}(0,\boldsymbol{x})
for
\boldsymbol{x}\in\partial\Omega
.

(SD)

Regularity assumptions on the interior symbol:

There are
r_{l},s_{l}\geq p
with
\frac{1}{s_{l}}+\frac{n}{2mr_{l}}<1-\frac{l}{2m}
such that

\displaystyle a_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}

\displaystyle\in\mathrm{L}_{s_{l}}(J;[\mathrm{L}_{r_{l}}+\mathrm{L}_{\infty}](\Omega;\B(E))),

\displaystyle\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|=l<2m,

\displaystyle a_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}

\displaystyle\in\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{l}}(J\times\overline{\Omega};\B(E)),

\displaystyle\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|=2m.

(SB)

Regularity assumptions on the boundary symbols:

There are
s_{j,k,l},r_{j,k,l}\geq p
such that
\frac{1}{s_{j,k,l}}+\frac{n-1}{2mr_{j,k,l}}<\kappa_{k}+\frac{l-k}{2m}
and

b_{j,k,\boldsymbol{\beta}}\in\mathrm{W}_{s_{j,k,l},r_{j,k,l}}^{(1,2m)\cdot\kappa_{k}}(J\times\partial\Omega;\B(E)),\quad\text{for each }\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\N_{0}^{n+1}\text{ of length }\left|\boldsymbol{\beta}\right|=l\leq k\leq m_{j}.

Moreover,
b_{j,k,\boldsymbol{\beta}}(t,\boldsymbol{x})(\ran\mathcal{P}_{j,k})\subseteq\ran(\mathcal{P}_{j,k})+\ldots+\ran(\mathcal{P}_{j,m_{j}})
for a.e.
(t,\boldsymbol{x})\in J\times\partial\Omega
and
j=1,\ldots,m
,
k=0,1,\ldots,m_{j}
and
\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\N_{0}^{n+1}
such that
\left|\boldsymbol{\beta}\right|\leq k
.

(E)

Ellipticity of the interior symbol:
For all
t\in J
,
\boldsymbol{x}\in\overline{\Omega}
and
\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
, the spectrum of the principle part of the interior symbol lies in the open right-half plane,

\sigma(\mathcal{A}_{\#}(t,\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\xi}))\subseteq\C_{0}^{+},

i.e.
\mathcal{A}(t,\boldsymbol{x},\mathrm{D})
is
normally elliptic
.
If
\Omega
is unbounded, the same condition is imposed at
\boldsymbol{x}=\infty
for
\mathcal{A}_{\#}(t,\infty,\boldsymbol{\xi}):=\lim_{\left|\boldsymbol{x}\right|\rightarrow\infty}\sum_{\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|=2m}a_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(t,\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\xi})\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
.

(LS)

Lopatinskii-Shapiro condition:
For all
t\in J
,
\boldsymbol{x}\in\partial\Omega
and all
\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\R^{n}
with
\boldsymbol{\xi}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}(\boldsymbol{x})=0
, and all
\lambda\in\overline{\C_{0}^{+}}
such that
(\lambda,\boldsymbol{\xi})\neq(0,\boldsymbol{0})
, the initial value problem

\displaystyle\lambda v(y)+\mathcal{A}_{\#}(t,\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\xi}+\mathrm{i}\boldsymbol{n}(\boldsymbol{x})\tfrac{\partial}{\partial y})v(y)

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle y>0,

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{j,\#}(t,\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\xi}+\mathrm{i}\boldsymbol{n}(\boldsymbol{x})\frac{\partial}{\partial y})v(0)

\displaystyle=h_{j},

\displaystyle j=1,\ldots,m,

has a unique solution in the class
v\in\mathrm{C}_{0}(\R_{+};E)
.

The theorem on
\mathrm{L}_{p}
-
\mathrm{L}_{p}
-optimal regularity then reads as follows.

Theorem 6.1

(
\mathrm{L}_{p}
–
\mathrm{L}_{p}
-optimal regularity)
.

Let
E
be a Banach space of class
\mathcal{HT}
and
\Omega\subseteq\R^{n}
be a domain with compact boundary of class
\partial\Omega\in\mathrm{C}^{2m}
.
Let
p\in(1,\infty)
and suppose that assumptions
(E)
,
(LS)
,
(SD)
and
(SB)
hold true. Then the inhomogeneous parabolic initial-boundary value problem

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u+\mathcal{A}(t,\boldsymbol{x},\mathrm{D})u

\displaystyle=f(t,\boldsymbol{x}),

\displaystyle t\in J,\,\boldsymbol{x}\in\Omega,

(iIBVP)

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{j}(t,\boldsymbol{x},\mathrm{D})u

\displaystyle=g_{j}(t,\boldsymbol{x}),

\displaystyle t\in J,\,\boldsymbol{x}\in\partial\Omega,\,j=1,\ldots,m,

\displaystyle u(0,\boldsymbol{x})

\displaystyle=u_{0}(\boldsymbol{x}),

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}\in\Omega

has a unique solution in the class

u\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{(1,2)}(J\times\Omega;E)

if and only if the data
f
,
\boldsymbol{g}
and
u_{0}
are subject to condition
(D)
.

We refrain from giving a proof, but only refer to the proof of
[
18
, Theorem 2.1]
which can be modified in a similar manner as the proof of Theorem
6.2
below.

For
\mathrm{L}_{p}
–
\mathrm{L}_{q}
-estimates with
p\neq q
, where
p\in(1,\infty)
corresponds to the integrability parameter w.r.t. time
t\geq 0
and
q\in(1,\infty)
reflects integrability w.r.t. the spatial variable
\boldsymbol{x}\in\Omega
, a similar result holds true, but the smoothness conditions on the coefficients and on the data have to be slightly modified, cf. the corresponding result
[
18
, Theorem 2.3]
by Denk, Hieber and Prüss.

(D1)

Assumptions on the data in case the
p\neq q
:

(i)

f\in\mathrm{L}_{p}(J;\mathrm{L}_{q}(\Omega;E))
,

(ii)

g_{j,k}\in\mathrm{F}_{p,q}^{\kappa_{k}}(J;\mathrm{L}_{q}(\partial\Omega;\ran\mathcal{P}_{j,k}))\cap\mathrm{L}_{p}(J;\mathrm{B}_{q,q}^{2m\kappa_{k}}(\partial\Omega;\ran\mathcal{P}_{j,k}))
with
\kappa_{k}=\frac{2m-k-1/q}{2m}
, and we then set
g_{j}=\sum_{k=0}^{m_{j}}g_{j,k}
,

(iii)

u_{0}\in\mathrm{B}_{q,p}^{2m(1-\nicefrac{{1}}{{p}})}(\Omega;E)
,

(iv)

if
\kappa_{k}>1/q
, then
\mathcal{B}_{j}(0,\boldsymbol{x})\mathcal{P}_{j,k}u_{0}(\boldsymbol{x})=g_{j,k}(0,\boldsymbol{x})
for
\boldsymbol{x}\in\partial\Omega
, for each
j=1,\ldots,m
and
k=0,1,\ldots,m_{j}
.

(SD1)

There are
s_{l}\geq p
and
r_{l}\geq q
with
\frac{1}{s_{l}}+\frac{n}{2mr_{l}}<1-\frac{l}{2m}
such that

\displaystyle a_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}

\displaystyle\in\mathrm{L}_{s_{l}}(J;(\mathrm{L}_{r_{l}}+\mathrm{L}_{\infty})(\Omega;\B(E)),

\displaystyle\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|=l<2m,

\displaystyle a_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}

\displaystyle\in\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{l}}(J\times\overline{\Omega};\B(E)),

\displaystyle\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|=2m.

(SB1)

There are
s_{j,k,l}\geq p
and
r_{j,k,l}\geq q
with
\frac{1}{s_{j,k,l}}+\frac{n-1}{2mr_{j,k,l}}<\kappa_{k}+\frac{k-l}{2m}
such that

b_{j,k,\boldsymbol{\beta}}\in\mathrm{W}_{s_{j,k,l},r_{j,k,l}}^{(1,2m)\cdot\kappa_{k}}(J\times\partial\Omega;\B(E)))\quad\text{for each }\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\N_{0}^{n+1}\text{ of length }\left|\boldsymbol{\beta}\right|=l\leq k\leq m_{j}.

Moreover,
b_{j,k,\boldsymbol{\beta}}(t,\boldsymbol{x})(\ran\mathcal{P}_{j,k})\subseteq\ran(\mathcal{P}_{j,k})+\ldots+\ran(\mathcal{P}_{j,m_{j}})
for a.e.
(t,\boldsymbol{x})\in J\times\partial\Omega
and
j=1,\ldots,m
,
k=0,1,\ldots,m_{j}
and
\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\N_{0}^{n}
such that
\left|\boldsymbol{\beta}\right|\leq k
.

Theorem 6.2

(
\mathrm{L}_{p}
–
\mathrm{L}_{q}
-optimal regularity)
.

Let
E
be a Banach space of class
\mathcal{HT}
and
\Omega\subseteq\R^{n}
be a domain with compact boundary of class
\partial\Omega\in\mathrm{C}^{2m}
.
Let
p,q\in(1,\infty)
and suppose that assumptions
(E)
,
(LS)
,
(D1)
,
(SD1)
and
(SB1)
hold true. Then the problem (
iIBVP
) has a unique solution in the class

u\in\mathrm{W}_{p,q}^{(1,2)}(J\times\Omega;E)

if and only if the data
f
,
\boldsymbol{g}
and
u_{0}
are subject to conditions
(D1)
.

Proof.

Let us sketch the strategy which has been employed in
[
18
]
and comment on adjustments that are necessary for the proof to carry over to the situation considered here.
As for the case of homogeneous initial data, i.e.
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}=\boldsymbol{0}
, the basic strategy is to consider step by step the full-space, the half-space and finally via localisation the general domain problem.
As the full-space problem obviously does not involve any boundary conditions, by
[
18
, Proposition 6.1]
, we know that the full-space problem with constant coefficients admits a unique solution of
iIBVP
in the class
\mathrm{W}^{(1,2m)}_{p,q}(\R_{+}\times\R^{n};E)
if and only if
f\in\mathrm{L}_{p,q}(\R_{+}\times\R^{n};E)
and
u_{0}\in\mathrm{B}_{q,p}^{2m(1-\frac{1}{p})}(\R^{n};E)
.
For the next step, the initial-boundary value problem on the half-space,
[
18
, Proposition 6.4]
and its proof can easily be modified as follows:

Proposition 6.3
.

Assume that the BVP associated to the operators
(\mathcal{A}(\mathrm{D}),\mathcal{B}_{1}(\mathrm{D}),\ldots,\mathcal{B}_{m}(\mathrm{D}))
satisfy conditions
(E)
and
(LS)
and let
\mu>0
.
Then the parabolic initial-boundary value problem

\displaystyle(\mu+\partial_{t}+\mathcal{A}(\mathrm{D}))u

\displaystyle=f

\displaystyle\text{in }\R_{+}\times\R_{+}^{n},

(24)

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{j}(\mathrm{D})u

\displaystyle=g_{j},

\displaystyle\text{in }\R_{+}\times\R^{n-1},

(25)

\displaystyle u(t,\cdot)

\displaystyle=u_{0}

\displaystyle\text{in }\R_{+}^{n}

has a (unique) solution in the class
\mathrm{W}_{p,q}^{(1,2m)}(\R_{+}\times\R_{+}^{n};E)
if and only if
f\in\mathrm{L}_{p,q}(\R_{+}\times\R_{+}^{n};E)
,
u_{0}\in\mathrm{B}_{q,p}^{2m(1-\frac{1}{p})}(\R_{+}^{n};E)
,
g_{j,k}=\mathcal{P}_{j,k}g_{j}\in\mathrm{F}_{p,q}^{1-\frac{1}{p}-\frac{k}{2m}}(\R_{+};\mathrm{L}_{q}(\R^{n-1};E))\cap\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R_{+};\mathrm{B}_{q,q}^{2m(1-\frac{1}{p})-k}(\R^{n-1};E))
and satisfy the compatibility conditions

\mathcal{B}_{jk}(\mathrm{D})u_{0}|_{y=0}=g_{j,k}|_{t=0}\quad\text{whenever }(1-\frac{1}{q})-\frac{k}{2m}>\frac{1}{q}.

Proof.

Necessity of the regularity of the data:

The necessity part can again be splitted into necessary spatial regularity and necessary of the time regularity.
For the spatial regularity, we may follow the lines of the proof for
[
18
, Proposition 6.4]
where by means of
[
18
, Proposition 6.1]
and taking boundary resp. time trace of a presupposed solution
u\in\mathrm{W}_{p,q}^{(1,2m)}(\R_{+}\times\R_{+}^{n};E)
, we may conclude that
u_{0}\in\mathrm{B}_{q,p}^{2m(1-\frac{1}{p})}(\R_{+}^{n};E)
and
u|_{y=0}\in\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R_{+};\mathrm{B}_{q,q}^{2m-\frac{1}{q}}(\partial\R_{+}^{n};E))
, so that each
g_{j,k}=\mathcal{B}_{jk}(\mathrm{D})u_{0}|_{y=0}\in\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R_{+};\mathrm{B}_{q,q}^{2m-k-\frac{1}{q}}(\R^{n-1};E))
,
j=1,\ldots,m
and
k=0,1,\ldots,m_{j}
.

The necessity of the time regularity can be observed based on the important fact that since
E
is a Banach space of class
\mathcal{HT}
and
p\in(1,\infty)
lies in the reflexive range, then the auxiliary space
\tilde{E}=\mathrm{L}_{q}(\R^{n-1};E)
is of class
\mathcal{HT}
as well, see Lemma
2.1
.
Moreover, since
q\in(1,\infty)
we may identify
\mathrm{L}_{q}(\R_{+}^{n};E)
with
\mathrm{L}_{q}(\R_{+};\tilde{E})
and
\mathrm{W}_{q}^{2m}(\R_{+}^{n};E)
with a subspace of
\mathrm{W}_{q}^{2m}(\R_{+};\tilde{E})
via the relation
u(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y)=(u(\cdot,y))(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})
.
In this sense,
u\in\mathrm{W}_{p,q}^{(1,2m)}(\R_{+}\times\R_{+}^{n};E)
implies that
u\in\mathrm{W}_{p}^{1}(\R_{+};\tilde{E})
.
Considering the (resolvent-commutative) operators
A=\partial_{y}
with
\dom(A)=\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R_{+};\mathrm{W}_{q}^{1}(\R_{+};\tilde{E}))
and
B=(\partial_{t})^{1/2m}
on
\dom(B)=\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{p}^{1/2m}(\R_{+};\mathrm{L}_{q}(\R_{+};\tilde{E}))
, after reducing the problem to the special case
u_{0}=0
, as in
[
18
]
it follows for the functions
u_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}:=B^{2m-\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|-1}\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}u
that
u_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}|_{y=0}\in\mathring{F}_{p,q}^{\frac{1}{2m}(1-\frac{1}{q})}(\R_{+};\tilde{E})
, thus
\mathrm{D}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}u|_{y=0}\in\mathring{\mathrm{F}}_{p,q}^{1-\frac{\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|}{2m}-\frac{1}{2mq}}(\R_{+};\tilde{E})=\{u\in\mathrm{F}_{p,q}^{1-\frac{\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|-\frac{1}{2mq}}{2m}}(\R_{+};\tilde{E}):\,u(0)=0\}
for each multi-index
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\N_{0}^{n}
of length
\left|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right|\leq 2m-1
, hence, we find that
g_{j,k}=\mathcal{B}_{jk}(\mathrm{D})u|_{y=0}\in\mathring{\mathrm{F}}_{p,q}^{1-\frac{k}{2m}-\frac{1}{2mq}}(\R_{+};\tilde{E})
.

Sufficiency of the regularity of the data:

Similar as we might have done it for the necessity of the time regularity, we demonstrate how to reduce the problem to the zero-initial value problem: Consider the zero-extension operator in space

\mathcal{E}_{0}\in\B(\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R_{+};\mathrm{L}_{q}(\R_{+}^{n};E)),\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R_{+};\mathrm{L}_{q}(\R^{n};E))

and any continuous extension operator
\mathcal{E}\in\B(\mathrm{B}_{q,p}^{2m(1-\frac{1}{p})}(\R_{+}^{n};E),\mathrm{B}_{q,p}^{2m(1-\frac{1}{p})}(\R^{n};E))
, e.g. defined via higher order reflections.
By
[
18
, Proposition 6.1]
, for any given data
f\in\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R_{+};\mathrm{L}_{q}(\R_{+}^{n}))
and
u_{0}\in\mathrm{B}_{q,p}^{2m(1-\frac{1}{p})}(\R_{+}^{n};E)
, there is a (unique) solution
u_{1}\in\mathrm{W}_{p,q}^{(1,2m)}(\R_{+}\times\R^{n};E)
to the full-space initial-value problem

\displaystyle(\mu+\partial_{t}+\mathcal{A}(\mathrm{D}))u_{1}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}_{0}f

\displaystyle\text{in }\R_{+}\times\R^{n},

\displaystyle u_{1}(0,\cdot)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}u_{0}

\displaystyle\text{in }\R^{n}.

We now set
u_{2}=u-u_{1}
, which reduces the problem to finding a function
u_{2}\in\mathrm{W}_{p,q}^{(1,2m)}(\R_{+}\times\R_{+}^{n})
such that

(26)

\displaystyle(\mu+\partial_{t}+\mathcal{A}(\mathrm{D}))u_{2}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\text{in }\R_{+}\times\R_{+}^{n},

(27)

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{j}(\mathrm{D})u_{2}

\displaystyle=\tilde{g}_{j}

\displaystyle\text{in }\R_{+}\times\R_{+}^{n-1},\,j=1,\ldots,m,

(28)

\displaystyle u_{2}(0,\cdot)

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\text{in }\R_{+}^{n},

where we have set
\tilde{g}_{j}=\sum_{k=0}^{m_{j}}\mathcal{P}_{j,k}\tilde{g}_{j}=\sum_{k=0}^{m_{j}}\tilde{g}_{j,k}
for adjusted boundary data defined by

\tilde{g}_{j,k}=g_{j,k}-\mathcal{B}_{j}(\mathrm{D})u_{1}\in\mathrm{F}_{p,q}^{(1-\frac{1}{q})-\frac{k}{2m}}(\R_{+};\mathrm{L}_{q}(\R^{n-1};E))\cap\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R_{+};\mathrm{B}_{q,q}^{2m(1-\frac{1}{q})-k}(\R^{n-1};E)).

The compatibility conditions on the boundary and initial data tell us that
\tilde{g}_{j,k}(0,\cdot)=0
, whenever the time trace exists, and, therefore, we may simply extend
\tilde{g}_{j,k}
by
0
(for time
t<0
) to a function (still denoted by
\tilde{g}_{j,k}
) in the class
\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R_{+};\mathrm{B}_{q,q}^{2m(1-\frac{1}{q})-k}(\R^{n-1};E))
.
Similar to the procedure in
[
18
]
, we next set

h_{j,k}(t,\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y):=L^{\frac{2m-k}{2m}}\mathrm{e}^{-yL^{1/2m}}\tilde{g}_{j,k}(t,\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})

for the parabolic operator

(29)

L=\mu+\partial_{t}+(-\Delta)^{m}\text{ defined on }\dom(L)=\mathring{\mathrm{W}}_{p}^{1}(\R_{+};\mathrm{L}_{q}(\R^{n-1};E))\cap\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R_{+};\mathrm{W}_{q}^{2m}(\R^{n-1};E)).

Using the solution operators
T_{j,k}=\mathcal{L}^{-1}T^{\lambda}_{j,k}\mathcal{L}\in\B(\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R_{+};\mathrm{L}_{q}(\R^{n};E)),\mathrm{W}_{p,q}^{(1,2)}(\R_{+}\times\R_{+}^{n};E))
for the corresponding parabolic IBVP, we find the solution to the system (
26
)–(
28
) to be

u_{2}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\sum_{k=0}^{m_{j}}T_{j,k}h_{j,k}\in\mathrm{W}_{p,q}^{(1,2m)}(\R_{+}\times\R_{+}^{n};E),

where, by the proof of
[
18
, Proposition 6.4]
, the pseudo-differential operator
L^{\frac{2m-k}{2m}}
maps continuously from
\mathring{W}_{p}^{1-\frac{k}{2m}}(\R_{+};\mathrm{L}_{q}(\R_{+}^{n};E))\cap\mathrm{L}_{p}(\R_{+};\mathrm{W}_{q}^{2m-k}(\R_{+};E))
into
X:=\mathrm{L}_{p,q}(\R_{+}\times\R_{+}^{n};E)
.
We are, thus, done, provided we can establish the following adjusted version of
[
18
, Proposition 4.5]
.

Proposition 6.4
.

Let
p,q\in(1,\infty)
,
\mu>0
and
E
be a Banach space of class
\mathcal{HT}
.
Assume that the differential operators
(\mathcal{A}(\mathrm{D}),\mathcal{B}_{1}(\mathrm{D}),\ldots,\mathcal{B}_{m}(\mathrm{D}))
satisfy conditions
(E)
,
(LS)
,
(D1)
,
(SB1)
and
(SD1)
.
Let

g_{j,k}\in\mathrm{W}_{p,q}^{(1,2m)\cdot(1-\frac{1}{q})\frac{k}{2m}}(\R_{+}\times\R^{n-1};\ran(\mathcal{P}_{j,k}))

for
j=1,\ldots,m
and such that
g_{j,k}(0,\cdot)=0
whenever
(1-\frac{1}{q})\frac{k}{2m}>\frac{1}{p}
.
Then the initial-boundary value problem

\displaystyle(\mu+\partial_{t}+\mathcal{A}(\mathrm{D}))u

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\text{in }\R_{+}\times\R_{+}^{n},

(hIBVP)

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{j}(\mathrm{D})u

\displaystyle=g_{j}=\sum_{k=0}^{m_{j}}g_{j,k}

\displaystyle\text{on }\R_{+}\times\partial\R_{+}^{n},

\displaystyle u(0,\cdot)

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\text{on }\R_{+}^{n}

admits a unique solution in the class
u\in\mathrm{W}_{p,q}^{(1,2m)}(\R_{+}\times\R_{+}^{n};E)
, which continuously depends on
\boldsymbol{g}
.

Proof.

Since by assumption
g_{j,k}(0,\cdot)=0
whenever
\kappa_{k}:=(1-\frac{1}{q})\frac{k}{2m}>\frac{1}{p}
, we may extend each function
g_{j,k}
for
t<0
by zero (still denoted by
g_{j,k}
) to get
g_{j,k}\in\mathrm{W}_{p,q}^{(\kappa_{k},2m\kappa_{k})}(\R\times\R^{n-1};E)
.
For
L=\mu+\partial_{t}+(-\Delta_{\R^{n-1}})^{m}
as in (
29
), we then find that
h_{j,k}(t,\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},y):=L^{\frac{2m-k}{2m}}\mathrm{e}^{-yL^{1/2m}}g_{j,k}(t,\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})
defines a function
h_{j,k}\in\mathrm{L}_{p,q}(\R\times\R_{+}^{n};\ran(\mathcal{P}_{j,k}))
, so that by Proposition
4.11
(an adjusted version of
[
18
, Lemma 4.3]
), the unique solution is given by
u=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\sum_{k=0}^{m_{j}}\mathcal{L}^{-1}T^{\lambda}_{j,k}\mathcal{L}h_{j,k}
for
